7.1 Bedford–Taylor products and local capacity

9 Bedford–Taylor products and local capacity

Let $u$ be a plurisubharmonic function on an open set $\Omega \subset \mathbb {C}^ n$. By theorem 8.2.1, $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u$ is a closed positive $(1,1)$-current, but in general its coefficients are only measures. Since products of measures have no general meaning, the exterior powers of $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u$ cannot be formed by multiplying coefficients. For $u\in C^2(\Omega )$ the top power is the Monge–Ampère measure $(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u)^ n=n!\, \pi ^{-n}\det (u_{j\bar k})\, \mathrm{d}V_{2n}$. This fully nonlinear expression in the second derivatives of $u$ plays in pluripotential theory the role that the Laplacian plays in classical potential theory. Bedford and Taylor showed that for locally bounded $u$ these powers can be defined by induction, and so can the mixed products $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_1\wedge \cdots \wedge \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_ q\wedge T$ with a closed positive current $T$. Indeed, since $T$ has measure coefficients and $u$ is a locally bounded Borel function, the product $uT$ makes sense, and one puts $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u\wedge T=\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}(uT)$. This chapter develops the local theory of these products, whose factors are always locally bounded; products of unbounded potentials are treated in chapter 15.

The first two sections establish the basic properties of the products: positivity, the Chern–Levine–Nirenberg mass bounds, continuity along decreasing sequences and symmetry (theorem 9.2.5), and the comparison principle. The products then define the Monge–Ampère capacity, a complex analogue of the relative capacity of chapter 4. Locally, pole sets of plurisubharmonic functions have outer capacity zero; plurisubharmonic functions are quasi-continuous with respect to this capacity, and the products are continuous under convergence in capacity and along increasing sequences. Solving the homogeneous Monge–Ampère equation on a ball, we show that a locally bounded plurisubharmonic function is maximal if and only if its Monge–Ampère measure vanishes. These are the tools with which chapter 10 characterizes pluripolar sets.

The remaining sections, needed for non-pluripolar products and envelopes on compact manifolds in chapter 15, chapter 16, solve the equation $(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u)^ n=\mu $ on a ball for increasingly singular measures $\mu $: continuous densities, bounded densities, and finally compactly supported measures dominated by the Monge–Ampère measure of a bounded plurisubharmonic function. The main application is a lower bound for mixed products: if $(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_ i)^ n\geq f_ i\mu $ for $1\le i\le n$, then $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_1\wedge \cdots \wedge \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_ n\geq (f_1\cdots f_ n)^{1/n}\mu $ (theorem 9.11.1). The chapter closes with the locality of complex Hessians on contact sets and a chain rule for gradients against positive currents.

Historical remarks and references for this chapter are collected in section B.9.

9.1 Bounded potentials and recursive products

Throughout this section and the next, $\Omega \subset \mathbb {C}^ n$ is open, $n\ge 1$, and $T$ is a closed positive current on $\Omega $ of bidimension $(r,r)$, where $0\le r\le n$ is an integer; thus $T$ has bidegree $(n-r,n-r)$. Let $\beta =(\mathrm{i}/2)\sum _ j\mathrm{d}z_ j\wedge \mathrm{d}\bar z_ j$ be the form of (7.4). For a positive current $S$ of bidimension $(s,s)$ on $\Omega $ and a compact set $K\subset \Omega $ we write

\[ \| S\| _ K=\int _ KS\wedge \frac{\beta ^ s}{s!} \]

for the mass of $S$ on $K$; it is $1/s!$ times the value on $K$ of the trace measure of $S$ (definition 7.2.4). By theorem 7.2.5, $S$ is a current of order zero, and the total variations of its coefficient measures are bounded by a constant multiple, depending only on $n$ and $s$, of its trace measure. Since plurisubharmonic functions are upper semicontinuous, they are bounded above on compact sets; hence a plurisubharmonic function is locally bounded if and only if it is bounded below on compact sets.

Definition 9.1.1. Suppose that $r\ge 1$, and let $u$ be a locally bounded plurisubharmonic function on $\Omega $. Let $uT$ be the current of order zero obtained by multiplying the coefficient measures of $T$ by the locally bounded Borel function $u$ (section 7.2), and define

\[ \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u\wedge T=\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}(uT). \]

For an integer $1\le q\le r$ and locally bounded plurisubharmonic functions $u_1,\ldots ,u_ q$ on $\Omega $, define recursively

\[ \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_1\wedge \cdots \wedge \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_ q\wedge T =\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\bigl (u_1\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_2\wedge \cdots \wedge \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_ q\wedge T\bigr ). \]

Here $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_2\wedge \cdots \wedge \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_ q\wedge T$ is a closed positive current, hence of order zero, by proposition 9.1.2 below applied to $q-1$ factors; so its product with $u_1$ is defined as before. The product with no factors is $T$. When $T=1$ (so that $r=n$), $q=n$ and $u_1=\cdots =u_ n=u$, the resulting positive current of bidegree $(n,n)$ is a positive measure, the Monge–Ampère measure $(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u)^ n$ of $u$.

Proposition 9.1.2. Every product $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_1\wedge \cdots \wedge \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_ q\wedge T$ of definition 9.1.1 is a closed positive current, and it has the following properties.

  1. If $u_1,\ldots ,u_ q$ are smooth, it coincides with the exterior product of the smooth forms $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_ j$ with $T$.

  2. The construction is local: if $u_ j=v_ j$ for every $j$ and $T=T'$ on an open set $U\subset \Omega $, then the two products agree on $U$.

  3. It is additive and positively homogeneous in each $u_ j$ and in $T$, whenever the summands are locally bounded plurisubharmonic functions, respectively closed positive currents of bidimension $(r,r)$, and it does not change if a real constant is added to some $u_ j$.

  4. If $\gamma $ is a smooth closed form of bidegree $(s,s)$, $q\le r-s$, such that $T\wedge \gamma $ is positive (for instance, $\gamma $ strongly positive), then

    \[ \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_1\wedge \cdots \wedge \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_ q\wedge (T\wedge \gamma )=(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_1\wedge \cdots \wedge \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_ q\wedge T)\wedge \gamma . \]

  5. It is invariant under biholomorphic maps: if $F\colon \Omega '\to \Omega $ is biholomorphic and $F^*$ denotes the pullback of currents of proposition 7.5.2, then

    \[ F^*\bigl (\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_1\wedge \cdots \wedge \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_ q\wedge T\bigr )=\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}(u_1\circ F)\wedge \cdots \wedge \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}(u_ q\circ F)\wedge F^*T. \]

Proof. We first record two consequences of the Leibniz rule of section 7.2. Let $R$ be a current of even degree on an open subset of $\mathbb C^ n$ such that $\partial R=\bar\partial R=0$; this holds if $R$ is closed and of pure bidegree. If $f$ is a smooth function, then $\mathrm d(fR)=\mathrm df\wedge R$ and $\mathrm d^{\mathrm c}(fR)=\mathrm d^{\mathrm c}f\wedge R$, and applying $\mathrm d$ to the latter gives

9.1
\begin{equation} \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}(fR)=\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}f\wedge R. \end{equation}

If $\gamma $ is a smooth closed form of pure bidegree, then $\partial \gamma $ and $\bar\partial \gamma $ are the components of $\mathrm d\gamma =0$ of two different bidegrees, so they vanish, and the Leibniz rule gives $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}(R'\wedge \gamma )=\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}R'\wedge \gamma $ for every current $R'$.

We now prove positivity and closedness, first for a single factor $u$. By proposition 8.2.2, the regularizations $u_\varepsilon =u*\rho _\varepsilon $ by the radial mollifiers of section 1.4 are smooth plurisubharmonic functions on $\Omega _\varepsilon $ with positive semidefinite complex Hessians, and they decrease to $u$ as $\varepsilon \downarrow 0$. On each compact set the $u_\varepsilon $ are bounded uniformly in $\varepsilon $, because $u\le u_\varepsilon \le u_{\varepsilon _0}$ for $\varepsilon \le \varepsilon _0$ and $u$ is locally bounded. Dominated convergence with respect to the total variations of the coefficient measures of $T$ therefore gives $u_\varepsilon T\to uT$ weakly. By (9.1), $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}(u_\varepsilon T)=\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_\varepsilon \wedge T$. Since weak and strong positivity agree in bidegree $(1,1)$ (section 7.2), the positive smooth form $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_\varepsilon $ is strongly positive, so its product with the positive current $T$ is positive; it is also closed. As $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}$ is continuous for weak convergence, $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_\varepsilon \wedge T\to \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}(uT)$ weakly, and positivity and closedness pass to the limit. Thus $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u\wedge T$ is positive and closed. Applying this with $T$ replaced by the closed positive current $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_2\wedge \cdots \wedge \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_ q\wedge T$, we obtain the first assertion by induction on $q$.

(1) If the $u_ j$ are smooth, (9.1) shows at each step of the recursion that the product is the exterior product of smooth forms with $T$.

(2), (3) The definition uses only multiplication of coefficient measures by functions and the operator $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}$; both operations are local and linear, and no product of two measures occurs. This gives locality, and additivity and positive homogeneity in $u_1$ and in $T$; for the other factors they follow by induction on $q$, since multiplication by $u_1$ and $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}$ are linear. If $c$ is a constant and $R=\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_2\wedge \cdots \wedge \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_ q\wedge T$, then $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}((u_1+c)R)=\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}(u_1R)+c\, \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}R=\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}(u_1R)$, since $R$ is closed; for the other factors the invariance under constants follows again by induction.

(4) The product $T\wedge \gamma $ is closed, and multiplication of coefficient measures by $u_1$ commutes with the exterior product by $\gamma $. By the identity $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}(R'\wedge \gamma )=\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}R'\wedge \gamma $ and induction on $q$, every step of the recursion commutes with $\wedge \gamma $.

(5) The pullback $F^*T$ is closed and positive and commutes with $\partial $ and $\bar\partial $, hence with $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}$ (proposition 7.5.2), and the functions $u_ j\circ F$ are locally bounded and plurisubharmonic (proposition 8.2.3). We claim that $F^*(uT)=(u\circ F)F^*T$ for a locally bounded plurisubharmonic $u$. For a smooth function $f$ we have $F^*(fT)=(f\circ F)F^*T$: both sides are weakly continuous in $T$, they agree when $T$ is smooth, and on every relatively compact open subset of $\Omega $ the current $T$ is the weak limit of its smooth regularizations (discussion following theorem 7.2.5). Applying this to the regularizations $u_\varepsilon $ on relatively compact open subsets, and letting $\varepsilon \downarrow 0$, we obtain the claim: $u_\varepsilon T\to uT$ as shown above, so $F^*(u_\varepsilon T)\to F^*(uT)$, while $(u_\varepsilon \circ F)F^*T\to (u\circ F)F^*T$ by dominated convergence with respect to the coefficient measures of the positive current $F^*T$. Consequently every step of the recursion commutes with $F^*$, which proves (5). In particular, by (2) and (5), products of locally bounded plurisubharmonic functions with closed positive currents on a complex manifold can be computed in holomorphic charts: the products defined in two charts agree on their overlap. $\square$

Let $u\in C^2(\Omega )$ be real, and write $a_{jk}=u_{j\bar k}$, so that $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u=\frac{\mathrm i}{2\pi }\sum _{j,k}a_{jk}\, \mathrm dz_ j\wedge \mathrm d\bar z_ k$ by (7.5). The $2$-forms $\alpha _{jk}=\mathrm dz_ j\wedge \mathrm d\bar z_ k$ commute with each other, and $\alpha _{j_1k_1}\wedge \cdots \wedge \alpha _{j_ nk_ n}=0$ unless $(j_1,\ldots ,j_ n)$ and $(k_1,\ldots ,k_ n)$ are permutations of $(1,\ldots ,n)$. If $j_ i=\sigma (i)$ and $k_ i=\tau (i)$ for permutations $\sigma ,\tau $, we may reorder the commuting factors so that the first indices increase; with $\kappa =\tau \circ \sigma ^{-1}$ we get $\mathrm dz_1\wedge \mathrm d\bar z_{\kappa (1)}\wedge \cdots \wedge \mathrm dz_ n\wedge \mathrm d\bar z_{\kappa (n)}$, and permuting the $1$-forms $\mathrm d\bar z_{\kappa (i)}$ among themselves gives the sign $\operatorname {sgn}\kappa $. Hence

\[ \Bigl (\sum _{j,k}a_{jk}\alpha _{jk}\Bigr )^ n =\sum _{\sigma ,\tau }\prod _{i=1}^ na_{\sigma (i)\tau (i)}\, \operatorname {sgn}(\tau \sigma ^{-1})\, \bigwedge _{j=1}^ n\alpha _{jj} =n!\det (a_{jk})\bigwedge _{j=1}^ n\alpha _{jj}, \]

because for each of the $n!$ permutations $\sigma $ the sum over $\tau $ is $\sum _\kappa \operatorname {sgn}\kappa \prod _ ja_{j\kappa (j)}=\det (a_{jk})$. Since $\mathrm{i}\, \mathrm{d}z_ j\wedge \mathrm{d}\bar z_ j=2\, \mathrm{d}x_ j\wedge \mathrm{d}y_ j$, we have $\bigwedge _ j(\mathrm i\alpha _{jj})=2^ n\mathrm dV_{2n}$, and therefore

9.2
\begin{equation} (\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u)^ n=\Bigl (\frac{\mathrm i}{2\pi }\Bigr )^ nn!\det (u_{j\bar k})\bigwedge _{j=1}^ n\alpha _{jj}=\frac{n!}{\pi ^ n}\det (u_{j\bar k})\, \mathrm{d}V_{2n}. \end{equation}

Here $(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u)^ n$ is the exterior power of the continuous form $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u$; when $u$ is smooth and plurisubharmonic, it is the Monge–Ampère measure of $u$, by proposition 9.1.2(1), and the same holds for plurisubharmonic $u\in C^2(\Omega )$ (see the remark after proposition 9.1.4). In particular $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}|z|^2=\frac{\mathrm i}{2\pi }\sum _ j\mathrm dz_ j\wedge \mathrm d\bar z_ j=\beta /\pi $ and $(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}|z|^2)^ n=n!\pi ^{-n}\, \mathrm dV_{2n}$. (The formula also holds for $n=0$: the empty product is the constant $1$, and both sides are the unit mass $\mathrm{d}V_0$ on the point $\mathbb C^0$.)

Theorem 9.1.3 (Chern–Levine–Nirenberg). Let $K,L\subset \Omega $ be compact sets with $K\subset \operatorname{Int}L$, and let $0\le q\le r$ be an integer. There is a constant $C_{K,L,q}$, depending only on $K$, $L$, $n$ and $q$, such that

\[ \| \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_1\wedge \cdots \wedge \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_ q\wedge T\| _ K \le C_{K,L,q}\| T\| _ L\prod _{j=1}^ q\| u_ j\| _{L^\infty (L)} \]

for every closed positive current $T$ of bidimension $(r,r)$ on $\Omega $ and all locally bounded plurisubharmonic functions $u_1,\ldots ,u_ q$ on $\Omega $. Here $\| u_ j\| _{L^\infty (L)}=\sup _ L|u_ j|$ is the pointwise supremum.

Proof. For $q=0$ the estimate holds with constant $1$, since $K\subset L$. Let $q=1$ and write $u=u_1$. By lemma 1.4.1 there is a smooth function $0\le \chi \le 1$ with compact support in $\operatorname{Int}L$ which equals $1$ on a neighborhood of $K$; it depends only on $K$ and $L$. Since $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u\wedge T\wedge \beta ^{r-1}$ is a positive measure, $\beta $ is closed, and $(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}S)(\chi )=S(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\chi )$ for currents $S$ of bidimension $(1,1)$, we have

\[ \begin{aligned} \int _ K\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u\wedge T\wedge \beta ^{r-1} & \le \int \chi \, \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}(uT)\wedge \beta ^{r-1} =\bigl (\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}(uT\wedge \beta ^{r-1})\bigr )(\chi )\\ & =\int uT\wedge \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\chi \wedge \beta ^{r-1} \le C\| u\| _{L^\infty (L)}\| T\| _ L. \end{aligned} \]

The form $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\chi \wedge \beta ^{r-1}$ is smooth with support in $L$, though in general not positive; its coefficients are bounded by a constant depending only on $\chi $ and $n$. The last inequality therefore holds because the total variations of the coefficient measures of $T$ on $L$ are bounded by a constant multiple of $\| T\| _ L$, the multiple depending only on $n$ and $r$ (theorem 7.2.5). Dividing by $(r-1)!$ and taking the largest of the constants obtained for $r=1,\ldots ,n$, we obtain the case $q=1$ with a constant depending only on $K$, $L$ and $n$.

For $q>1$, let $\delta >0$ be the distance from $K$ to $\mathbb C^ n\setminus \operatorname{Int}L$ (if $K=\varnothing $ there is nothing to prove), and put $L_ j=\{ z:\operatorname {dist}(z,K)\le j\delta /(q+1)\} $ for $0\le j<q$ and $L_ q=L$. These are compact sets, depending only on $K$, $L$ and $q$, with $K=L_0$ and $L_{j-1}\subset \operatorname{Int}L_ j$ for $1\le j\le q$. By proposition 9.1.2, the currents $S_ j=\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_{j+1}\wedge \cdots \wedge \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_ q\wedge T$, $0\le j\le q$, are positive and closed, and $S_{j-1}=\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_ j\wedge S_ j$. The case $q=1$, applied to $S_ j$ and the pair $L_{j-1}\subset \operatorname{Int}L_ j$, gives $\| S_{j-1}\| _{L_{j-1}}\le C_ j\| u_ j\| _{L^\infty (L)}\| S_ j\| _{L_ j}$ for $j=1,\ldots ,q$, since $\| u_ j\| _{L^\infty (L_ j)}\le \| u_ j\| _{L^\infty (L)}$. Combining these $q$ inequalities, we obtain the estimate with $C_{K,L,q}=C_1\cdots C_ q$. $\square$

For continuous potentials, uniform convergence is enough to pass to the limit in the products.

Proposition 9.1.4. Let $T_ k\to T$ weakly, where $T_ k$ and $T$ are closed positive currents on $\Omega $ of bidimension $(r,r)$, and let $0\le q\le r$. For $1\le j\le q$, let $(u_{j,k})_{k\ge 1}$ be locally bounded plurisubharmonic functions on $\Omega $ converging locally uniformly, as $k\to \infty $, to a continuous function $u_ j$, and let $(u_{0,k})_{k\ge 1}$ be locally bounded Borel functions on $\Omega $ converging locally uniformly to a continuous function $u_0$. Then $u_1,\ldots ,u_ q$ are plurisubharmonic, and

\[ \begin{aligned} \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_{1,k}\wedge \cdots \wedge \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_{q,k}\wedge T_ k & \longrightarrow \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_1\wedge \cdots \wedge \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_ q\wedge T,\\ u_{0,k}\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_{1,k}\wedge \cdots \wedge \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_{q,k}\wedge T_ k & \longrightarrow u_0\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_1\wedge \cdots \wedge \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_ q\wedge T \end{aligned} \]

weakly as $k\to \infty $.

Proof. We first show that $u_ j$ is plurisubharmonic for $1\le j\le q$. Since plurisubharmonicity is a local property, it suffices to prove this on every ball $W\Subset \Omega $. Choose $k_1<k_2<\cdots $ such that $|u_{j,k_ m}-u_ j|\le 2^{-m}$ on $W$, and put $w_ m=u_{j,k_ m}+3\cdot 2^{-m}$. These functions are plurisubharmonic on $W$, and they decrease, because

\[ w_{m+1}\le u_ j+2^{-m-1}+3\cdot 2^{-m-1}=u_ j+2^{1-m}\le u_{j,k_ m}+2^{-m}+2^{1-m}=w_ m. \]

Their limit $u_ j$ is finite, so it is plurisubharmonic on $W$ by theorem 8.3.1.

Since the $u_{j,k}$ converge locally uniformly to continuous functions, they are bounded on each compact set uniformly in $k$. Since $T_ k\to T$ weakly, the masses $\| T_ k\| _ L$ are bounded for each compact set $L$: if $\chi \in C^\infty _ c(\Omega )$ satisfies $0\le \chi \le 1$ and $\chi =1$ on $L$, then $\| T_ k\| _ L\le T_ k(\chi \beta ^ r)/r!$, and the right-hand side converges. By theorem 9.1.3, the masses of all the products considered below are therefore bounded on compact sets uniformly in $k$, and so are the total variations of their coefficient measures (theorem 7.2.5).

We claim: if $S_ k\to S$ weakly, where $S_ k$ and $S$ are closed positive currents whose masses are bounded on compact sets uniformly in $k$, and if locally bounded Borel functions $v_ k$ converge locally uniformly to a continuous function $v$, then $v_ kS_ k\to vS$ weakly. Applying this to $S_ k=\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_{2,k}\wedge \cdots \wedge \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_{q,k}\wedge T_ k$ and $v_ k=u_{1,k}$, and then $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}$, which is weakly continuous, proves the first assertion by induction on $q$; the case $q=0$ is the hypothesis. The second assertion is the claim with $v_ k=u_{0,k}$.

To prove the claim, fix a test form $\varphi $ with compact support $K$ and $\varepsilon >0$. By lemma 1.4.1(1), applied to $v$ on a relatively compact open neighborhood of $K$, there is a smooth function $v_\varepsilon $ on a neighborhood of $K$ with $|v-v_\varepsilon |\le \varepsilon $ on $K$. Write

\[ v_ kS_ k-vS=(v_ k-v)S_ k+(v-v_\varepsilon )S_ k +v_\varepsilon (S_ k-S)+(v_\varepsilon -v)S. \]

Evaluated on $\varphi $, a term $fS_ k$ with a bounded Borel function $f$ is at most $C_\varphi \sup _ K|f|$ times the total variation on $K$ of the coefficient measures of $S_ k$, where $C_\varphi $ bounds the coefficients of $\varphi $; the same holds for $S$. Hence the first term tends to zero, since $v_ k\to v$ uniformly on $K$; the second and fourth terms are bounded by a constant times $\varepsilon $, uniformly in $k$; and the third tends to zero as $k\to \infty $, since $v_\varepsilon \varphi $ is a test form. Letting first $k\to \infty $ and then $\varepsilon \downarrow 0$ proves the claim. $\square$

If $u\in C^2(\Omega )$ is plurisubharmonic, its Monge–Ampère measure is given by (9.2). Indeed, the regularizations $u_\varepsilon $ are smooth and plurisubharmonic on $\Omega _\varepsilon $ (proposition 8.2.2) and converge to $u$ locally uniformly (lemma 1.4.1(1)), and their second derivatives are the regularizations of the continuous second derivatives of $u$ (proposition 1.4.3), so they also converge locally uniformly. Hence $\det ((u_\varepsilon )_{j\bar k})\to \det (u_{j\bar k})$ locally uniformly. Applying (9.2) to $u_\varepsilon $ and proposition 9.1.4 on each $\Omega _{\varepsilon _0}$, along a sequence $\varepsilon _ k\downarrow 0$ with $\varepsilon _ k<\varepsilon _0$, we obtain the formula for $u$ on every $\Omega _{\varepsilon _0}$, hence on $\Omega $.

9.2 Decreasing continuity and symmetry

A bounded plurisubharmonic function need not be continuous (see the example after proposition 9.2.7), so in general it is not a uniform limit of continuous functions; it is, however, the limit of the decreasing sequence of its smooth regularizations. The main result of this section, theorem 9.2.5, states that the products are continuous along decreasing sequences and symmetric in their factors. Upper semicontinuity bounds every weak limit of weighted products from above (lemma 9.2.2). To prove equality, we first modify all functions so that they coincide with one fixed smooth function near the boundary of a ball, and then compare total masses by an integration by parts (lemma 9.2.1). As applications, we prove that the products are local on sets of the form $\{ u>v\} $ (proposition 9.2.7) and derive the comparison principle (theorem 9.2.8).

We use the real operator $\mathrm{d}^{\mathrm c}$ of (7.6), for which $\mathrm{d}\mathrm{d}^{\mathrm c}=\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}$. If $a$ and $b$ are smooth real functions, the $(1,1)$-part of $\mathrm{d}a\wedge \mathrm{d}^{\mathrm c}b$ is $\frac{\mathrm{i}}{4\pi }(\partial a\wedge \bar\partial b+\partial b\wedge \bar\partial a)$, which is symmetric in $a$ and $b$. Only this part survives multiplication by a current $R$ of bidegree $(n-1,n-1)$, so

\[ \mathrm{d}a\wedge \mathrm{d}^{\mathrm c}b\wedge R=\mathrm{d}b\wedge \mathrm{d}^{\mathrm c}a\wedge R. \]

Moreover, the $(2,0)$- and $(0,2)$-parts of $\mathrm{d}a\wedge \mathrm{d}^{\mathrm c}a$ are multiples of $\partial a\wedge \partial a=0$ and $\bar\partial a\wedge \bar\partial a=0$, so

9.7
\begin{equation} \mathrm{d}a\wedge \mathrm{d}^{\mathrm c}a=\frac{\mathrm{i}}{2\pi }\partial a\wedge \bar\partial a; \end{equation}

this is a positive $(1,1)$-form, because $\bar\partial a=\overline{\partial a}$ for real $a$.

If $B$ is a ball, a collar of $\partial B$ is an open neighborhood of the sphere $\partial B$.

Several proofs below make bounded plurisubharmonic functions coincide on a common collar as follows. Let $B=B(z_0,s)$, let $U$ be an open neighborhood of $\overline B$, put $\psi =|z-z_0|^2-s^2$, and let $N\ge 1$ and $\delta >0$. If $v$ is plurisubharmonic on $U$ and $-N\le v\le -1$ there, then the function

9.8
\begin{equation} \max \{ v,A\psi \} ,\qquad A=N/\delta , \end{equation}

is plurisubharmonic on $U$ (proposition 8.2.5). It equals $v$ on $\{ \psi <-\delta \} $, where $A\psi <-N$, and it equals $A\psi $ on the collar $\{ z\in U:\psi (z)>-1/A\} $ of $\partial B$, where $A\psi >-1$; on $B$, where $\psi <0$, it takes values in $[-N,0)$. Since $t\mapsto \max \{ t,A\psi (z)\} $ is increasing and continuous, the modification preserves inequalities between functions, monotone sequences, and convergence at each point.

Lemma 9.2.1. Let $B$ be a ball and $R$ a closed positive current of bidimension $(1,1)$ on a neighborhood of $\overline B$. Let $a$ be a smooth function and $b$ a bounded plurisubharmonic function on a neighborhood of $\overline B$, and suppose that $a=b$ on a collar of $\partial B$. Then

\[ \int _ B a\, \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}b\wedge R=\int _ B b\, \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}a\wedge R. \]

In particular one may take $R=S\wedge \gamma $, where $S$ is a closed positive current, $\gamma $ is a smooth closed form of pure bidegree, and $S\wedge \gamma $ is positive of bidimension $(1,1)$.

Proof. Both integrals are finite, since $a$ and $b$ are bounded on $B$ and $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}a\wedge R$ and $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}b\wedge R$ are measures of finite total variation near $\overline B$. The function $h=b-a$ is bounded and Borel, and it vanishes on a collar $V$ of $\partial B$; hence its restriction to $B$ vanishes outside the compact set $K_0=\overline B\setminus V\subset B$. On $B$ we have $bR=aR+hR$, and since $R$ is closed, $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}(aR)=\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}a\wedge R$ by (9.1); thus

\[ \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}b\wedge R=\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}a\wedge R+\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}(hR)\quad \text{on }B, \]

where $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}(hR)$ is a signed measure with support in $K_0$. Choose $\chi \in C^\infty _ c(B)$ equal to $1$ on a neighborhood of $K_0$. By the definition of $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}$ on currents,

\[ \int _ Ba\, \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}(hR)=\int \chi a\, \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}(hR)=\int h\, \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}(\chi a)\wedge R=\int _ Bh\, \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}a\wedge R. \]

Consequently

\[ \int _ Ba\, \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}b\wedge R=\int _ Ba\, \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}a\wedge R+\int _ Bh\, \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}a\wedge R=\int _ Bb\, \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}a\wedge R. \]

The last assertion holds because $S\wedge \gamma $ is then closed, by the Leibniz rule, and positive of bidimension $(1,1)$; moreover $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}b\wedge (S\wedge \gamma )=(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}b\wedge S)\wedge \gamma $ by proposition 9.1.2(4). $\square$

Lemma 9.2.2. Let $\mu _ j\to \mu $ weakly, where $\mu _ j$ and $\mu $ are positive Radon measures on $\Omega $, and let $f_ j$ be real-valued upper semicontinuous functions on $\Omega $ which decrease to a locally bounded function $f$. If $f_{j_ k}\mu _{j_ k}\to \nu $ weakly along a subsequence, then $\nu \le f\mu $.

Proof. Let $\chi \ge 0$ be a continuous function with compact support, let $N$ be a compact neighborhood of $\operatorname{Supp}\chi $, and put $W=\operatorname{Int}N$. The function $f_1$ is upper semicontinuous, hence bounded above on $N$, and $f$ is bounded below on $N$; since $f\le f_ j\le f_1$, there is $c\ge 0$ with $|f_ j|\le c$ on $N$ for all $j$. The positive Radon measures $(f_{j_ k}+c)\mu _{j_ k}$ on $W$ converge weakly to $\nu +c\mu $. By proposition 1.5.1, $\nu +c\mu $ is a positive Radon measure on $W$, and the convergence is vague. Since also $\mu _ j\to \mu $ vaguely, we obtain $\int \psi f_{j_ k}\, \mathrm d\mu _{j_ k}\to \int \psi \, \mathrm d\nu $ for every continuous function $\psi $ with compact support in $W$.

Fix $l$. By lemma 3.1.2, there are bounded continuous functions $g_ m$ on $N$ decreasing to $f_ l$. For $j_ k\ge l$ we have $f_{j_ k}\le f_ l\le g_ m$ on $N$, so

\[ \int \chi f_{j_ k}\, \mathrm{d}\mu _{j_ k}\le \int \chi g_ m\, \mathrm{d}\mu _{j_ k}. \]

Letting $k\to \infty $ gives $\int \chi \, \mathrm{d}\nu \le \int \chi g_ m\, \mathrm{d}\mu $. Since $-c\le f_ l\le g_ m\le g_1$ on $N$ and $\mu (N)<\infty $, bounded convergence as $m\to \infty $ gives $\int \chi \, \mathrm{d}\nu \le \int \chi f_ l\, \mathrm{d}\mu $, and bounded convergence as $l\to \infty $ gives $\int \chi \, \mathrm{d}\nu \le \int \chi f\, \mathrm{d}\mu $. Since $\chi \ge 0$ was arbitrary, $\nu \le f\mu $. $\square$

To smooth the functions (9.8) without changing them on a collar, we replace the maximum by a smooth convex function of two variables.

Lemma 9.2.3. There are smooth convex functions $M_\varepsilon \colon \mathbb {R}^2\to \mathbb {R}$, $\varepsilon >0$, increasing in each variable, such that $M_\varepsilon \downarrow \max $ as $\varepsilon \downarrow 0$, $\max \le M_\varepsilon \le \max +\varepsilon $, and $M_\varepsilon (s,t)=\max \{ s,t\} $ when $|s-t|>2\varepsilon $. If $a$ and $b$ are smooth plurisubharmonic functions, so is $M_\varepsilon (a,b)$.

Proof. Let $\rho _\varepsilon $ be the mollifier of section 1.4 on $\mathbb {R}^2$; it is a nonnegative even radial probability density supported in the disc of radius $\varepsilon $. Put $M_\varepsilon =\max *\rho _\varepsilon $, that is, $M_\varepsilon (x)=\int \max (x-y)\rho _\varepsilon (y)\, \mathrm dy$, where $\max (s,t)=\max \{ s,t\} $; it is smooth, since $\rho _\varepsilon $ is (section 1.4). Since $\rho _\varepsilon \ge 0$ has integral $1$, and $x\mapsto \max (x-y)$ is convex and increasing in each variable for each $y$, the function $M_\varepsilon $ is convex and increasing in each variable. Since $\rho _\varepsilon $ is even, $\int y_ i\rho _\varepsilon (y)\, \mathrm dy=0$ for $i=1,2$; integrating $\max (s-y_1,t-y_2)\ge s-y_1$ and $\max (s-y_1,t-y_2)\ge t-y_2$ against $\rho _\varepsilon $ therefore gives $M_\varepsilon (s,t)\ge s$ and $M_\varepsilon (s,t)\ge t$, that is, $M_\varepsilon \ge \max $. Since $|\max (x-y)-\max (x)|\le |y|\le \varepsilon $ on the support of $\rho _\varepsilon $, we have $M_\varepsilon \le \max +\varepsilon $. If $s-t>2\varepsilon $ and $|y|\le \varepsilon $, then $(s-y_1)-(t-y_2)\ge s-t-2\varepsilon >0$, so $M_\varepsilon (s,t)=\int (s-y_1)\rho _\varepsilon (y)\, \mathrm dy=s$; similarly $M_\varepsilon (s,t)=t$ if $t-s>2\varepsilon $.

It remains to show that $M_\varepsilon $ increases with $\varepsilon $. For fixed $x\in \mathbb R^2$, the function $g(\varepsilon )=\int \max (x-\varepsilon y)\rho _1(y)\, \mathrm{d}y$, defined for all real $\varepsilon $, equals $M_\varepsilon (x)$ for $\varepsilon >0$ and $\max (x)$ for $\varepsilon =0$; it is convex, since $\varepsilon \mapsto \max (x-\varepsilon y)$ is convex, and even, since $\rho _1$ is even. For $0\le \varepsilon _1<\varepsilon _2$, writing $\varepsilon _1=\lambda (-\varepsilon _2)+(1-\lambda )\varepsilon _2$ with $\lambda \in (0,1]$, we get $g(\varepsilon _1)\le \lambda g(-\varepsilon _2)+(1-\lambda )g(\varepsilon _2)=g(\varepsilon _2)$. This proves the monotonicity, and together with $\max \le M_\varepsilon \le \max +\varepsilon $ this gives $M_\varepsilon \downarrow \max $ as $\varepsilon \downarrow 0$.

Finally, let $a$ and $b$ be smooth and plurisubharmonic, and write $(a_1,a_2)=(a,b)$. Denoting by $\partial _ kM_\varepsilon $ and $\partial _ k\partial _ lM_\varepsilon $ the partial derivatives evaluated at $(a,b)$, the chain rule gives

\[ \mathrm{i}\partial \bar\partial M_\varepsilon (a,b) =\sum _{k=1}^2\partial _ kM_\varepsilon \, \mathrm{i}\partial \bar\partial a_ k +\mathrm{i}\sum _{k,l=1}^2\partial _ k\partial _ lM_\varepsilon \, \partial a_ k\wedge \bar\partial a_ l. \]

The first sum is positive because $\partial _ kM_\varepsilon \ge 0$, $M_\varepsilon $ being increasing in each variable, and the forms $\mathrm i\partial \bar\partial a_ k$ are positive, since the smooth plurisubharmonic functions $a_ k$ have positive semidefinite complex Hessians (proposition 8.2.2). For the second, note that the real Hessian $H=(\partial _ k\partial _ lM_\varepsilon )$ is positive semidefinite: for $v\in \mathbb R^2$, the function $t\mapsto M_\varepsilon (x+tv)$ is convex, so its second derivative $v^ tHv$ is nonnegative. Writing $H=\sum _ m\lambda _ mv_ mv_ m^ t$ with $\lambda _ m\ge 0$ and real orthonormal vectors $v_ m$, and putting $\theta _ m=\sum _ k(v_ m)_ k\partial a_ k$, we get $\mathrm i\sum _{k,l}\partial _ k\partial _ lM_\varepsilon \, \partial a_ k\wedge \bar\partial a_ l=\sum _ m\lambda _ m\mathrm i\theta _ m\wedge \bar\theta _ m$, because $\bar\partial a_ l=\overline{\partial a_ l}$ for real $a_ l$; this is a positive $(1,1)$-form. Hence the complex Hessian of $M_\varepsilon (a,b)$ is positive semidefinite, and $M_\varepsilon (a,b)$ is plurisubharmonic by proposition 8.2.2. $\square$

We also note that weak convergence of currents is a local property, so that it can be proved near each point.

Lemma 9.2.4. Let $S_ j$, $j\ge 1$, and $S$ be currents of the same degree on an open set $\Omega \subset \mathbb C^ n$. If every point of $\Omega $ has an open neighborhood $U\subset \Omega $ such that $S_ j\to S$ weakly on $U$, then $S_ j\to S$ weakly on $\Omega $.

Proof. Let $\varphi $ be a test form on $\Omega $ of complementary degree. Its support is compact, so it is covered by finitely many open sets $U_1,\ldots ,U_ m$ as in the hypothesis. By lemma 1.4.2, there are smooth functions $\eta _1,\ldots ,\eta _ m$ with compact supports in $U_1,\ldots ,U_ m$, respectively, such that $\sum _ i\eta _ i=1$ on a neighborhood of $\operatorname{Supp}\varphi $. Then $\varphi =\sum _ i\eta _ i\varphi $, where $\eta _ i\varphi $ is a test form supported in $U_ i$, and therefore $S_ j(\varphi )=\sum _ iS_ j(\eta _ i\varphi )\to \sum _ iS(\eta _ i\varphi )=S(\varphi )$. $\square$

Theorem 9.2.5 (Bedford–Taylor). Let $T$ be a closed positive current on $\Omega $ of bidimension $(r,r)$, and let $0\le q\le r$ be an integer. For $0\le l\le q$, let $(u_{l,j})_{j\ge 1}$ be a decreasing sequence of locally bounded plurisubharmonic functions on $\Omega $ whose limit $u_ l$ is locally bounded (and therefore plurisubharmonic, by theorem 8.3.1). Then

\[ \begin{aligned} \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_{1,j}\wedge \cdots \wedge \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_{q,j}\wedge T & \longrightarrow \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_1\wedge \cdots \wedge \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_ q\wedge T,\\ u_{0,j}\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_{1,j}\wedge \cdots \wedge \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_{q,j}\wedge T & \longrightarrow u_0\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_1\wedge \cdots \wedge \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_ q\wedge T \end{aligned} \]

weakly as $j\to \infty $. Moreover, the product $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_1\wedge \cdots \wedge \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_ q\wedge T$ of locally bounded plurisubharmonic functions is symmetric in $u_1,\ldots ,u_ q$.

Proof. We argue by induction on $q$. Step 1 derives the unweighted assertion and the symmetry from the weighted assertion for fewer factors. The other steps prove the weighted assertion on a ball, after a modification near its boundary: every weak limit of the weighted products is at most the expected limit (Step 4), and repeated integration by parts gives the reverse inequality for the total masses (Step 5).

Step 1: the induction. We prove the second (weighted) assertion by induction on $q$, simultaneously for all open sets $\Omega \subset \mathbb C^ n$, all $r\ge q$, all closed positive currents $T$ of bidimension $(r,r)$ on $\Omega $ and all decreasing sequences as in the statement. For $q=0$ it states that $u_{0,j}T\to u_0T$; this follows from dominated convergence with respect to the total variations of the coefficient measures of $T$, since $u_0\le u_{0,j}\le u_{0,1}$. Let $q\ge 1$ and assume the weighted assertion for fewer than $q$ factors. Applying it to the $q-1$ factors $u_{2,j},\ldots ,u_{q,j}$ with the weight $u_{1,j}$, and then applying the weakly continuous operator $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}$, we obtain the unweighted assertion for $q$ factors.

Next we prove symmetry for $q$ factors. Let $u_1,\ldots ,u_ q$ be locally bounded and plurisubharmonic, let $\varepsilon >0$, and choose $\varepsilon _ k\downarrow 0$ with $\varepsilon _ k<\varepsilon $. The regularizations $u_ l*\rho _{\varepsilon _ k}$ are smooth plurisubharmonic functions on $\Omega _\varepsilon $ decreasing to $u_ l$ (proposition 8.2.2). By the unweighted assertion, the product $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_1\wedge \cdots \wedge \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_ q\wedge T$ on $\Omega _\varepsilon $ is therefore the weak limit of exterior products of smooth forms with $T$ (proposition 9.1.2(1)), and these are symmetric in the factors because $2$-forms commute. Since the sets $\Omega _\varepsilon $ exhaust $\Omega $, products with $q$ factors are symmetric. It remains to prove the weighted assertion for $q$ factors; in doing so we may use the unweighted assertion and the symmetry for $q$ factors.

Step 2: reduction to a common collar. By lemma 9.2.4, it suffices to prove the convergence near every point. Fix a ball $B=B(a,R)$, a larger ball $B_1$ with $\overline B\subset B_1\Subset \Omega $, and a compact set $K\subset B$; we prove the convergence on a neighborhood of $K$. Since $u_ l\le u_{l,j}\le u_{l,1}$, all the functions $u_{l,j}$ and $u_ l$ are bounded on $B_1$ uniformly in $j$. Subtracting a constant from $u_{l,j}$ and $u_ l$ changes neither the Hessians nor, since the unweighted assertion is already known, the validity of the weighted assertion. We may therefore assume that $-N\le u_{l,j},u_ l\le -1$ on $B_1$ for all $l$ and $j$, where $N\ge 1$. Put $\psi =|z-a|^2-R^2$, choose $\delta >0$ with $K\subset \{ \psi <-\delta \} $, and replace every function $v$ among the $u_{l,j}$ and $u_ l$ by the function $\max \{ v,A\psi \} $ of (9.8), with $U=B_1$ and $A=N/\delta $. The new functions are plurisubharmonic on $B_1$, and the new sequences still decrease to the new limits. The functions are unchanged on the neighborhood $\{ \psi <-\delta \} $ of $K$, and by locality so are their products. From now on, $u_{l,j}$ and $u_ l$ denote the new functions; they take values in $[-N,0)$ on $B$ and coincide with $A\psi $ on the collar $V=\{ z\in B_1:\psi (z)>-1/A\} $ of $\partial B$.

Step 3: smooth approximation with the same collar. Fix $\eta >0$ such that $\{ \psi \le \eta \} \subset B_1$, and let $V'=\{ z:-1/(2A)<\psi (z)<\eta \} $; it is a collar of $\partial B$ contained in $V$. Fix $l$ and $j$, and let $\tilde u$ be the function $u_{l,j}$ before the modification of Step 2, so that $\tilde u\le -1$ on $B_1$ and $u_{l,j}=\max \{ \tilde u,A\psi \} $. For $0<\varepsilon <1/4$ smaller than the distance from $\{ \psi \le \eta \} $ to $\partial B_1$, put

\[ u^\varepsilon _{l,j}=M_\varepsilon (\tilde u*\rho _\varepsilon ,A\psi ), \]

with $M_\varepsilon $ as in lemma 9.2.3. The functions $\tilde u*\rho _\varepsilon $ are smooth and plurisubharmonic on $\{ \psi <\eta \} $ and decrease to $\tilde u$ as $\varepsilon \downarrow 0$ (proposition 8.2.2), so the $u^\varepsilon _{l,j}$ are smooth and plurisubharmonic on $\{ \psi <\eta \} $ by lemma 9.2.3. Since $\tilde u*\rho _\varepsilon \le -1$, on $V'$ we have $A\psi -\tilde u*\rho _\varepsilon >1/2>2\varepsilon $, and therefore $u^\varepsilon _{l,j}=A\psi $. Since $M_\varepsilon $ decreases to $\max $, is increasing in each variable and satisfies $\max \le M_\varepsilon \le \max +\varepsilon $, the functions $u^\varepsilon _{l,j}$ decrease to $\max \{ \tilde u,A\psi \} =u_{l,j}$. In particular $u^\varepsilon _{l,j}\ge u_{l,j}\ge u_ l$, and all the functions $u_{l,j}$, $u_ l$ and $u^\varepsilon _{l,j}$ coincide with $A\psi $ on $V'$.

Step 4: an upper bound for weak limits. Put

\[ S_ j=\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_{1,j}\wedge \cdots \wedge \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_{q,j}\wedge T,\qquad S=\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_1\wedge \cdots \wedge \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_ q\wedge T. \]

By Step 1, $S_ j\to S$ weakly. On $B$ the weights $u_{0,j}$ take values in $[-N,0)$, so the currents $-u_{0,j}S_ j$ are positive there, and by theorem 9.1.3, since $|u_{l,j}|\le N$ on $B$, their masses on compact subsets of $B$ are bounded uniformly in $j$. By the compactness part of theorem 7.2.5, every subsequence of $(u_{0,j}S_ j)$ has a further subsequence converging weakly on $B$. Let $\Theta $ be the limit of such a subsequence. Let $\varphi $ be a strongly positive test form on $B$ of complementary bidegree. The measures $\mu _ j=S_ j\wedge \varphi $ are positive and converge weakly to $S\wedge \varphi $, and along the chosen subsequence $u_{0,j}\mu _ j\to \Theta \wedge \varphi $. Hence lemma 9.2.2, applied on $B$ with the real-valued upper semicontinuous functions $f_ j=u_{0,j}$, gives $\Theta \wedge \varphi \le u_0S\wedge \varphi $. Evaluating this inequality of measures on a nonnegative test function equal to $1$ on $\operatorname{Supp}\varphi $, we get $(u_0S-\Theta )(\varphi )\ge 0$; thus $u_0S-\Theta $ is a positive current on $B$. By locality (proposition 9.1.2), all the currents $u_{0,j}S_ j$, and also $u_0S$, coincide on $V'$ with the fixed current $A\psi \, (A\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\psi )^ q\wedge T$; hence so does $\Theta $, and $u_0S-\Theta $ has compact support in $B$.

Step 5: comparison of masses. Set $\gamma =\beta ^{r-q}$ and

\[ I=\int _ Bu_0S\wedge \gamma , \qquad I_ j=\int _ Bu_{0,j}S_ j\wedge \gamma . \]

We claim that $I\le I_ j$ for every $j$. Fix $j$ and parameters $\varepsilon _0,\ldots ,\varepsilon _ q$, and abbreviate $w_ l=u^{\varepsilon _ l}_{l,j}$. For $0\le k\le q$ put

\[ J_ k=\int _ Bu_ k\, \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}w_0\wedge \cdots \wedge \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}w_{k-1}\wedge \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_{k+1}\wedge \cdots \wedge \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_ q\wedge T\wedge \gamma , \]

so that $J_0=I$. The measure in $J_ k$ is positive and $u_ k\le w_ k$, so $J_ k$ does not decrease if $u_ k$ is replaced by $w_ k$. If $k<q$, symmetry of products with $q$ factors and proposition 9.1.2(4) let us write that measure as $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_{k+1}\wedge R_ k$, where

\[ R_ k=\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}w_0\wedge \cdots \wedge \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}w_{k-1}\wedge \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_{k+2}\wedge \cdots \wedge \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_ q\wedge T\wedge \gamma \]

is a closed positive current of bidimension $(1,1)$ on $\{ \psi <\eta \} $; then lemma 9.2.1, with $a=w_ k$ and $b=u_{k+1}$, which both equal $A\psi $ on the collar $V'$, gives

\[ J_ k\le \int _ Bw_ k\, \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_{k+1}\wedge R_ k=\int _ Bu_{k+1}\, \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}w_ k\wedge R_ k=J_{k+1}. \]

Hence

\[ I=J_0\le J_1\le \cdots \le J_ q\le \int _ Bw_ q\, \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}w_0\wedge \cdots \wedge \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}w_{q-1}\wedge T\wedge \gamma . \]

Now let $\varepsilon _ q\downarrow 0$, then $\varepsilon _{q-1}\downarrow 0$, and so on down to $\varepsilon _1\downarrow 0$, keeping $\varepsilon _0$ fixed. We claim that the right-hand side of the last inequality then tends to

\[ \int _ Bu_{q,j}\, \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}w_0\wedge \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_{1,j}\wedge \cdots \wedge \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_{q-1,j}\wedge T\wedge \gamma . \]

When $\varepsilon _ q\downarrow 0$, the measure is fixed and the bounded weights $w_ q$ decrease to $u_{q,j}$, so dominated convergence applies. When $\varepsilon _ k\downarrow 0$ for some $1\le k\le q-1$, the integrand is, after moving the smooth factors next to $T$ by symmetry,

\[ u_{q,j}\, \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u^{\varepsilon _ k}_{k,j}\wedge \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_{k+1,j}\wedge \cdots \wedge \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_{q-1,j}\wedge T_ k\wedge \gamma , \]

where $T_ k=\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}w_0\wedge \cdots \wedge \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}w_{k-1}\wedge T$ is a fixed closed positive current on $\{ \psi <\eta \} $ of bidimension $(r-k,r-k)$. There are $q-k\le q-1$ Hessian factors and the fixed weight $u_{q,j}$. Along any sequence $\varepsilon _ k\downarrow 0$, the functions $u^{\varepsilon _ k}_{k,j}$ decrease to $u_{k,j}$ (Step 3), so the induction hypothesis, applied on $\{ \psi <\eta \} $ with the base current $T_ k$, constant sequences for the other factors and the constant weight $u_{q,j}$, shows that these currents converge weakly; so do their exterior products with $\gamma $.

In each case the convergence holds for the integrals over $B$, and not only against compactly supported test forms, because all the currents involved coincide on the collar $V'$ with the same fixed current: if $\chi \in C^\infty _ c(B)$ equals $1$ on $B\setminus V'$, the integrals against $\chi $ converge, and the integrals against $1-\chi $ do not depend on the parameters.

By lemma 9.2.1, with $a=w_0$ and $b=u_{q,j}$, and by symmetry of products with $q$ factors, the limit integral equals

\[ \int _ Bw_0\, \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_{q,j}\wedge \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_{1,j}\wedge \cdots \wedge \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_{q-1,j}\wedge T\wedge \gamma =\int _ Bu^{\varepsilon _0}_{0,j}\, S_ j\wedge \gamma . \]

Hence $I\le \int _ Bu^{\varepsilon _0}_{0,j}S_ j\wedge \gamma $ for every $\varepsilon _0$, and dominated convergence with respect to the finite measure $S_ j\wedge \gamma $ on $B$ gives $I\le I_ j$ as $\varepsilon _0\downarrow 0$.

Step 6: conclusion. Along the subsequence defining $\Theta $, splitting the integrals with a cutoff as in Step 5 shows that $I_ j\to \int _ B\Theta \wedge \gamma $. Therefore

\[ \int _ B(u_0S-\Theta )\wedge \gamma =I-\lim _ jI_ j\le 0. \]

Since $u_0S-\Theta $ is positive with compact support in $B$, its trace measure $(u_0S-\Theta )\wedge \gamma $ is a positive measure of total mass at most zero, hence zero; by the coefficient bounds of theorem 7.2.5, $u_0S-\Theta =0$ on $B$. Thus every subsequence of $(u_{0,j}S_ j)$ has a further subsequence converging weakly to $u_0S$ on $B$. Hence the whole sequence converges to $u_0S$ on $B$: otherwise there would be a test form $\varphi $ on $B$ and a subsequence along which $|(u_{0,j}S_ j-u_0S)(\varphi )|$ stays bounded away from zero, and no further subsequence of it could converge to $u_0S$. The replacement by maxima in Step 2 changes neither the functions nor, by locality, the weighted products on the neighborhood $\{ \psi <-\delta \} $ of $K$, and the subtraction of constants does not affect the convergence; hence the original weighted products converge on this neighborhood. As $K$ and $B$ were arbitrary, this proves the weighted assertion for $q$ factors on $\Omega $ and completes the induction. $\square$

Corollary 9.2.6. Let $P$ be a real polynomial in $m$ variables and, for $1\le i\le m$, let $(v_{i,j})_{j\ge 1}$ be a decreasing sequence of locally bounded plurisubharmonic functions on $\Omega $ with locally bounded limit $v_ i$. Then in theorem 9.2.5 the weight $u_{0,j}$ may be replaced by $P(v_{1,j},\ldots ,v_{m,j})$, and the limit is then

\[ P(v_1,\ldots ,v_ m)\, \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_1\wedge \cdots \wedge \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_ q\wedge T. \]

Moreover, the identity of lemma 9.2.1 also holds when $a$ and $b$ are both bounded plurisubharmonic functions on a neighborhood of $\overline B$ which coincide on a collar of $\partial B$.

Proof. Since weak convergence is a local property (lemma 9.2.4), it suffices to prove the convergence on every relatively compact open set $W\Subset \Omega $. On $W$ all the functions $v_{i,j}$ and $v_ i$ are bounded, because $v_ i\le v_{i,j}\le v_{i,1}$. Adding a constant $c_ i$ to each $v_{i,j}$ and to $v_ i$ replaces $P$ by the polynomial $P(x_1-c_1,\ldots ,x_ m-c_ m)$; so we may assume that all these functions are nonnegative on $W$. Since the weighted products depend linearly on the weight, it suffices to treat a monomial. A constant weight is covered by the unweighted assertion of the theorem. A monomial of degree $N\ge 1$ is a product $a_1\cdots a_ N$, where each $a_ l$ is one of the $v_ i$, repetitions being allowed; we write $a_{l,j}$ for the corresponding function $v_{i,j}$. For real numbers $x_1,\ldots ,x_ N$ we have the polarization identity

9.11
\begin{equation} N!\, x_1\cdots x_ N=\sum _{I\subset \{ 1,\ldots ,N\} }(-1)^{N-|I|}\Bigl (\sum _{l\in I}x_ l\Bigr )^ N. \end{equation}

To prove it, expand $(\sum _{l\in I}x_ l)^ N=\sum _\alpha \frac{N!}{\alpha !}x^\alpha $, the sum running over the multi-indices $\alpha \in \mathbb N^ N$ with $|\alpha |=N$ and $\alpha _ l=0$ for $l\notin I$. Fix such an $\alpha $, and let $J=\{ l:\alpha _ l>0\} $ have $k$ elements. The monomial $x^\alpha $ occurs exactly in the terms with $I\supset J$, so its total coefficient on the right-hand side is

\[ \frac{N!}{\alpha !}\sum _{I\supset J}(-1)^{N-|I|}=\frac{N!}{\alpha !}\sum _{i=0}^{N-k}\binom {N-k}{i}(-1)^{N-k-i}=\frac{N!}{\alpha !}(1-1)^{N-k}. \]

This vanishes unless $k=N$, in which case $\alpha =(1,\ldots ,1)$ and the coefficient is $N!$.

Each sum $s=\sum _{l\in I}a_ l$ is a bounded nonnegative plurisubharmonic function on $W$, and so is $s^ N$. For $N=1$ there is nothing to prove; for $N\ge 2$ and smooth $s$ this follows from

\[ \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}(s^ N)=Ns^{N-1}\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}s+N(N-1)s^{N-2}\, \mathrm{d}s\wedge \mathrm{d}^{\mathrm c}s, \]

in which both terms are positive $(1,1)$-forms, the first because $s\ge 0$ and $s$ has a positive semidefinite complex Hessian, and the second by (9.7); hence $s^ N$ is plurisubharmonic, by both directions of proposition 8.2.2. In general, the regularizations $s*\rho _\varepsilon $ are smooth, plurisubharmonic and nonnegative, and they decrease to $s$ (proposition 8.2.2); since $t\mapsto t^ N$ is increasing on $[0,\infty )$, the plurisubharmonic functions $(s*\rho _\varepsilon )^ N$ decrease to $s^ N$, which is therefore plurisubharmonic by theorem 8.3.1. Likewise the sums $s_ j=\sum _{l\in I}a_{l,j}$ are nonnegative and decrease to $s$, so the $s_ j^ N$ are bounded plurisubharmonic functions on $W$ decreasing to $s^ N$. The weighted assertion of the theorem therefore applies with the weights $s_ j^ N$, and by (9.11), with $x_ l=a_{l,j}$, it applies to the monomial weights $a_{1,j}\cdots a_{N,j}$.

For the second assertion, let $a$ and $b$ be bounded plurisubharmonic functions on a neighborhood $U$ of $\overline B$ which are equal on a collar $V$ of $\partial B$. Let $d>0$ be smaller than the distances from $\partial B$ to $\mathbb C^ n\setminus V$ and from $\overline B$ to $\mathbb C^ n\setminus U$, and put $V'=\{ z:\operatorname {dist}(z,\partial B)<d/2\} $. For $\varepsilon =\varepsilon _ k\downarrow 0$ with $\varepsilon _ k<d/2$, the regularizations $a_\varepsilon =a*\rho _\varepsilon $ and $b_\varepsilon =b*\rho _\varepsilon $ are smooth and plurisubharmonic on a neighborhood of $\overline B$ and decrease to $a$ and $b$ (proposition 8.2.2); moreover $a_\varepsilon =b_\varepsilon $ on the collar $V'$, because $a=b$ on the ball $B(z,\varepsilon )\subset V$ for $z\in V'$. By lemma 9.2.1, applied to the smooth function $a_\varepsilon $ and the bounded plurisubharmonic function $b_\varepsilon $,

\[ \int _ Ba_\varepsilon \, \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}b_\varepsilon \wedge R=\int _ Bb_\varepsilon \, \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}a_\varepsilon \wedge R. \]

Choose $\chi \in C^\infty _ c(B)$ equal to $1$ on $B\setminus V'$. On $V'$ the measures $a_\varepsilon \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}b_\varepsilon \wedge R$ and $b_\varepsilon \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}a_\varepsilon \wedge R$ coincide, by locality, so the identity is equivalent to $\int \chi a_\varepsilon \, \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}b_\varepsilon \wedge R=\int \chi b_\varepsilon \, \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}a_\varepsilon \wedge R$. By the weighted assertion of the theorem with one factor and the fixed base current $R$, this passes to the limit: $\int \chi a\, \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}b\wedge R=\int \chi b\, \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}a\wedge R$. Since $a=b$ on $V$, the measures $a\, \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}b\wedge R$ and $b\, \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}a\wedge R$ also coincide on $V'$, and adding the integrals against $1-\chi $ over $B$ gives the identity for $a$ and $b$. $\square$

Proposition 9.2.7. Let $T$ be a closed positive current on $\Omega $ of bidimension $(r,r)$, let $u$ and $v$ be locally bounded plurisubharmonic functions on $\Omega $, and let $0\le p\le r$ be an integer. Then

\[ \mathbf1_{\{ u>v\} }(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\max \{ u,v\} )^ p\wedge T =\mathbf1_{\{ u>v\} }(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u)^ p\wedge T. \]

Here the indicator function multiplies the coefficient measures of these currents of order zero, as in section 7.2; the set $\{ u>v\} $ is Borel, but in general not open (see the example following the proof).

Proof. The assertion is local, so it suffices to prove it on each $\Omega _{\varepsilon _0}$, $\varepsilon _0>0$. Let $u_ j=u*\rho _{\varepsilon _ j}$ with $\varepsilon _ j\downarrow 0$, $\varepsilon _ j<\varepsilon _0$; these are smooth plurisubharmonic functions on $\Omega _{\varepsilon _0}$ decreasing to $u$ (proposition 8.2.2). Since $u_ j$ is continuous and $v$ is upper semicontinuous, the set $\{ u_ j>v\} $ is open, and on it $w_ j=\max \{ u_ j,v\} $ equals $u_ j$; by locality, $(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}w_ j)^ p\wedge T=(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_ j)^ p\wedge T$ there. The nonnegative Borel function $f_ j=w_ j-v$ vanishes outside $\{ u_ j>v\} $, so

\[ f_ j(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}w_ j)^ p\wedge T=f_ j(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_ j)^ p\wedge T. \]

The functions $w_ j$ decrease to $w=\max \{ u,v\} $. Both sides of the last identity are differences of weighted products, for instance $f_ j(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}w_ j)^ p\wedge T=w_ j(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}w_ j)^ p\wedge T-v(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}w_ j)^ p\wedge T$. Applying theorem 9.2.5 to them, with the weights $w_ j$ and the constant weight $v$, and with the factors $w_ j$ on the left and $u_ j$ on the right, we obtain $f(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}w)^ p\wedge T=f(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u)^ p\wedge T$, where $f=w-v\ge 0$. Multiply the coefficient measures of both sides by $1/(f+\varepsilon )$ and let $\varepsilon \downarrow 0$. Since $f/(f+\varepsilon )$ increases to the indicator of $\{ f>0\} =\{ u>v\} $, dominated convergence with respect to the locally finite total variations of the coefficient measures gives the assertion. $\square$

To see that $\{ u>v\} $ need not be open, let $D=\{ z\in \mathbb C^ n:|z_1|<1/2\} $, put $a_ j=\mathrm e^{-j}$ for $j\ge 1$, and let $w=\sum _{j\ge 1}2^{-j}\log |z_1-a_ j|$ on $D$. Each term is a positive multiple of $\log |z_1-a_ j|^2$, which is plurisubharmonic by proposition 8.3.3, and it is negative on $D$, because $|z_1-a_ j|<1/2+\mathrm e^{-1}<1$. Hence the partial sums form a decreasing sequence of plurisubharmonic functions (proposition 8.2.5), and since $w(0)=-\sum _ jj2^{-j}=-2$ is finite, $w$ is plurisubharmonic on the domain $D$ by theorem 8.3.1. The function $u=\max \{ w,-4\} $ is plurisubharmonic with values in $[-4,0]$, and for the constant $v=-3$ the set $\{ u>v\} =\{ w>-3\} $ contains the origin but none of the points $(a_ j,0,\ldots ,0)$, at which $w=-\infty $; these points converge to the origin. Since $u(0)=-2$ while $u=-4$ at these points, $u$ is also an example of a bounded plurisubharmonic function which is not continuous.

Theorem 9.2.8. Let $D\subset \mathbb {C}^ n$ be a bounded open set, and let $u$ and $v$ be locally bounded plurisubharmonic functions on $D$ such that $\varliminf _{D\ni z\to x}(u(z)-v(z))\ge 0$ for every $x\in \partial D$. Then

\[ \int _{\{ u<v\} }(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}v)^ n\le \int _{\{ u<v\} }(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u)^ n, \]

where both sides may be infinite. If $(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u)^ n=0$, then $u\ge v$ on $D$.

Proof. Let $\varepsilon >0$ and $w=\max \{ u,v-\varepsilon \} $. We show that $(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}w)^ n$ and $(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u)^ n$ have the same mass near a compact set outside which $w=u$, and then identify $(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}w)^ n$ by proposition 9.2.7. Every $x\in \partial D$ has an open neighborhood $U_ x$ with $u>v-\varepsilon $ on $U_ x\cap D$, and finitely many of them cover the compact set $\partial D$; let $U$ be their union. Then $F=D\setminus U=\overline D\setminus U$ is a compact subset of $D$, it contains $\{ u\le v-\varepsilon \} $, and $w=u$ on $D\setminus F$. For $0\le l\le n-1$ put $R_ l=(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}w)^ l\wedge (\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u)^{n-1-l}$. By additivity and symmetry of the products (proposition 9.1.2, theorem 9.2.5),

\[ \begin{aligned} \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\bigl ((w-u)R_ l\bigr )& =\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}w\wedge R_ l-\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u\wedge R_ l\\ & =(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}w)^{l+1}\wedge (\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u)^{n-1-l}-(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}w)^ l\wedge (\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u)^{n-l}, \end{aligned} \]

and summing over $l$, the right-hand sides telescope:

\[ (\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}w)^ n-(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u)^ n =\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\Bigl ((w-u)\sum _{l=0}^{n-1}R_ l\Bigr ). \]

The current in parentheses vanishes outside $F$, since $w-u$ does. Hence the signed measure $(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}w)^ n-(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u)^ n$ is supported in $F$, and if $\chi \in C^\infty _ c(D)$ equals $1$ on a neighborhood of $F$, then its total mass is

\[ \int \chi \, \mathrm d\bigl ((\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}w)^ n-(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u)^ n\bigr )=\Bigl ((w-u)\sum _ lR_ l\Bigr )(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\chi )=0, \]

because $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\chi =0$ on a neighborhood of $F$. Choose a relatively compact open set $D'\subset D$ containing $F$. Both measures are finite on $D'$, and $\int _{D'}(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}w)^ n=\int _{D'}(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u)^ n$. By proposition 9.2.7, $(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}w)^ n$ coincides with $(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}v)^ n$ on $\{ u<v-\varepsilon \} $ and with $(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u)^ n$ on $\{ u>v-\varepsilon \} $; here we use that adding the constant $-\varepsilon $ does not change $(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}v)^ n$. Since $\{ u<v-\varepsilon \} $ and $D'\cap \{ u>v-\varepsilon \} $ are disjoint Borel subsets of $D'$, and $\{ u\le v-\varepsilon \} \subset F\subset D'$,

\[ \begin{aligned} \int _{\{ u<v-\varepsilon \} }(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}v)^ n & \le \int _{D'}(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}w)^ n -\int _{D'\cap \{ u>v-\varepsilon \} }(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u)^ n\\ & =\int _{\{ u\le v-\varepsilon \} }(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u)^ n \le \int _{\{ u<v\} }(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u)^ n. \end{aligned} \]

As $\varepsilon \downarrow 0$, the sets $\{ u<v-\varepsilon \} $ increase to $\{ u<v\} $, and monotone convergence proves the comparison inequality.

The last assertion is the case of corollary 9.2.9 below in which the roles of $u$ and $v$ are interchanged: if $(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u)^ n=0$, then $v\le u$ holds almost everywhere for $(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u)^ n$. The proof of that corollary uses only the comparison inequality. $\square$

Corollary 9.2.9. Let $D\subset \mathbb C^ n$ be a bounded open set, $n\geq 1$, and let $u$ and $v$ be locally bounded plurisubharmonic functions on $D$. Suppose that

\[ \varliminf _{D\ni z\to x}(v(z)-u(z))\geq 0 \quad (x\in \partial D),\qquad u\leq v\quad \text{almost everywhere for }(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}v)^ n. \]

Then $u\leq v$ everywhere on $D$.

Proof. Choose $R$ with $D\subset B(0,R)$, and for $\delta >0$ put $u_\delta =u+\delta (|z|^2-R^2)$, a locally bounded plurisubharmonic function. Since $u_\delta \le u$ on $D$, the boundary condition still holds for $v$ and $u_\delta $, and the set $A=\{ v<u_\delta \} $ is contained in $\{ v<u\} $. By additivity, positive homogeneity and symmetry of the products (proposition 9.1.2, theorem 9.2.5), $(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_\delta )^ n=\sum _{k=0}^ n\binom nk\delta ^ k(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u)^{n-k}\wedge (\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}|z|^2)^ k$ is a sum of positive measures, and its term with $k=n$ is $\delta ^ nn!\, \pi ^{-n}\, \mathrm dV_{2n}$, by (9.2). The comparison inequality of theorem 9.2.8, applied to $v$ and $u_\delta $, therefore gives

\[ \delta ^ n\frac{n!}{\pi ^ n}|A|\leq \int _ A(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_\delta )^ n\leq \int _ A(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}v)^ n=0, \]

where the last equality holds because $A\subset \{ v<u\} $ and $u\le v$ almost everywhere for $(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}v)^ n$. Thus $A$ is a Lebesgue null set, and $\max \{ u_\delta ,v\} =v$ almost everywhere. Both functions are plurisubharmonic, so the uniqueness of plurisubharmonic representatives in theorem 8.2.1, applied on each connected component of $D$, gives $\max \{ u_\delta ,v\} =v$ everywhere, that is, $u_\delta \leq v$. Letting $\delta \downarrow 0$ gives $u\le v$. $\square$

9.3 Weighted estimates and Monge–Ampère capacity

We now use the products to define a capacity adapted to plurisubharmonic functions. The Monge–Ampère capacity of a set $E$ is the supremum of the masses that the measures $(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}v)^ n$ give to $E$ when $v$ ranges over the plurisubharmonic functions with values in $[0,1]$. To control exceptional sets with it, we need integral estimates that are uniform over this whole class. The first one is a version of the Chern–Levine–Nirenberg inequality with an unbounded plurisubharmonic weight.

Theorem 9.3.1. Let $\Omega \subset \mathbb C^ n$ be open, $n\ge 1$, let $K,L\subset \Omega $ be compact sets with $K\subset \operatorname{Int}L$, and let $0\le q\le n$ be an integer. There is a constant $C_{K,L,q}$, depending only on $K$, $L$, $n$ and $q$, such that for every $V\in \mathrm{PSH}(\Omega )$ and all locally bounded plurisubharmonic functions $u_1,\ldots ,u_ q$ on $\Omega $,

\[ \int _ K |V|\, \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_1\wedge \cdots \wedge \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_ q\wedge \beta ^{n-q} \le C_{K,L,q}\| V\| _{L^1(L)} \prod _{l=1}^ q\| u_ l\| _{L^\infty (L)}. \]

In particular, the function $V$, with its values $-\infty $ on the pole set $\{ V=-\infty \} $, is locally integrable with respect to the trace measure $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_1\wedge \cdots \wedge \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_ q\wedge \beta ^{n-q}$.

Proof. For $q\le n-1$ we integrate $V$ by parts against a cutoff function on a ball, after making the factors coincide near its boundary (Steps 1–4); the case $q=n$ follows by adding one variable (Step 5).

Step 1: reductions. By theorem 8.2.1, $V$ is locally integrable, so $\| V\| _{L^1(L)}<\infty $. For $q=0$ the left-hand side equals $n!\int _ K|V|\, \mathrm{d}V_{2n}\le n!\| V\| _{L^1(L)}$, by (7.4). Let $q\ge 1$. If $\| u_ l\| _{L^\infty (L)}=0$ for some $l$, then $u_ l=0$ on $\operatorname{Int}L$, so the product vanishes on $\operatorname{Int}L\supset K$ by locality. Otherwise we replace each $u_ l$ by $u_ l/(2\| u_ l\| _{L^\infty (L)})-3/2$, which takes values in $[-2,-1]$ on $L$; by proposition 9.1.2(3) this multiplies the left-hand side by $\prod _ l(2\| u_ l\| _{L^\infty (L)})^{-1}$. For each $a\in K$ choose $R_ a>0$ with $\overline{B(a,R_ a)}\subset \operatorname{Int}L$; finitely many of the balls $B(a,R_ a/2)$ cover $K$, and this choice depends only on $K$ and $L$. Hence it suffices to prove the following: for concentric balls $B'=B(a,R')$ and $B=B(a,R)$ with $R'<R$ and $\overline B\subset \operatorname{Int}L$, there is a constant $C$, depending only on $B'$, $B$, $L$, $n$ and $q$, such that

\[ \int _{B'}|V|\, \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_1\wedge \cdots \wedge \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_ q\wedge \beta ^{n-q}\le C\| V\| _{L^1(L)} \]

whenever $u_1,\ldots ,u_ q$ are plurisubharmonic on $\Omega $ with values in $[-2,-1]$ on $L$.

Step 2: a common collar. Put $\psi =|z-a|^2-R^2$ and $b=\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\psi =\beta /\pi $, and choose $\delta >0$ with $\overline{B'}\subset \{ \psi <-\delta \} $. Replace each $u_ l$ by $\max \{ u_ l,A\psi \} $, where $A=2/\delta $; this function is plurisubharmonic on $\Omega $ (proposition 8.2.5). By (9.8), with $N=2$ and $U=\operatorname{Int}L$, it is unchanged on $\{ \psi <-\delta \} $, it equals $A\psi $ on the collar $V_0=\{ z\in \operatorname{Int}L:\psi (z)>-\delta /2\} $ of $\partial B$, and it takes values in $[-2,0)$ on $B$. Let $S=\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_1\wedge \cdots \wedge \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_ q$ be the product of the modified functions. Put $\tau =\delta /4$ and $B_\tau =\{ \psi <-\tau \} $. Then $\overline{B'}\subset B_\tau $, and on the neighborhood $V_0$ of $\overline B\setminus B_\tau $ we have $S=(A\, \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\psi )^ q=A^ qb^ q$ by proposition 9.1.2(1),(2). By lemma 1.4.1(4) there is $\theta \in C^\infty _ c(B)$ with $0\le \theta \le 1$ and $\theta =1$ on a neighborhood of $\overline{B_\tau }$; the function $\chi =-\psi \theta $ is smooth, nonnegative, has compact support in $B$, and equals $-\psi $ on a neighborhood of $\overline{B_\tau }$. All these choices depend only on $B'$, $B$ and $L$.

Step 3: smooth $V$ and $q\le n-1$. Suppose that $q\le n-1$ and that $V$ is smooth and plurisubharmonic on a neighborhood of $\overline B$ with $V\le 0$ on $B_\tau $. The signed measure $S\wedge b^{n-q-1}\wedge \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\chi $ has compact support in $B$. Splitting its integral against $V$ over $B_\tau $, where $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\chi =-b$, and over $B\setminus B_\tau \subset V_0$, where $S=A^ qb^ q$, we obtain

\[ \begin{aligned} \int _{B_\tau }(-V)S\wedge b^{n-q} & =\int _ B V S\wedge b^{n-q-1}\wedge \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\chi -A^ q\int _{B\setminus B_\tau }V b^{n-1}\wedge \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\chi \\ & =\int _ B\chi \, \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}V\wedge S\wedge b^{n-q-1} -A^ q\int _{B\setminus B_\tau }V b^{n-1}\wedge \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\chi . \end{aligned} \]

The second equality holds because the first integral is $(VS\wedge b^{n-q-1})(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\chi )=\bigl (\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}(VS\wedge b^{n-q-1})\bigr )(\chi )$, and $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}(VS\wedge b^{n-q-1})=\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}V\wedge S\wedge b^{n-q-1}$ by (9.1), the current $S\wedge b^{n-q-1}$ being closed. The smooth form $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}V$ is closed, and it is strongly positive, since its coefficient matrix is positive semidefinite (proposition 8.2.2) and weak and strong positivity agree in bidegree $(1,1)$ (section 7.2); so by proposition 9.1.2(4), with $T=1$ and $\gamma =\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}V$, the current $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}V\wedge S$ is the product $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_1\wedge \cdots \wedge \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_ q\wedge \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}V$ with the closed positive base current $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}V$ of bidimension $(n-1,n-1)$.

Choose a compact set $L'\subset B$ whose interior contains $\operatorname{Supp}\chi $. The first term is at most $\pi ^{q+1-n}\sup \chi $ times the mass of the trace measure of $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_1\wedge \cdots \wedge \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_ q\wedge \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}V$ on $\operatorname{Supp}\chi $. Since the modified $u_ l$ take values in $[-2,0)$ on $B$, theorem 9.1.3, applied to $\operatorname{Supp}\chi \subset \operatorname{Int}L'$, shows that the first term is at most $C\| \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}V\| _{L'}$, with $C$ depending only on $\chi $, $L'$, $n$ and $q$. Let $\chi '\in C^\infty _ c(B)$ satisfy $0\le \chi '\le 1$ and $\chi '=1$ on $L'$. Since $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}V\wedge \beta ^{n-1}\ge 0$,

\[ \| \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}V\| _{L'}\le \int \chi '\, \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}V\wedge \frac{\beta ^{n-1}}{(n-1)!}=\int V\, \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\chi '\wedge \frac{\beta ^{n-1}}{(n-1)!}\le C\int _ B|V|\, \mathrm{d}V_{2n}, \]

where $C$ bounds the coefficient of the smooth $(n,n)$-form $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\chi '\wedge \beta ^{n-1}/(n-1)!$. The collar term is bounded by the same quantity, because $b^{n-1}\wedge \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\chi $ has bounded smooth coefficients. Hence

\[ \int _{B_\tau }(-V)S\wedge b^{n-q}\le C\int _ B|V|\, \mathrm{d}V_{2n}. \]

Step 4: general $V$ and $q\le n-1$. Let $V\in \mathrm{PSH}(\Omega )$, and choose $r_0>0$ with $2r_0<\operatorname {dist}(\overline B,\mathbb C^ n\setminus \operatorname{Int}L)$. The function $V$ is subharmonic (theorem 8.2.1). If $x$ lies within distance $r_0$ of $\overline B$, then $\overline{B(x,r_0)}\subset \operatorname{Int}L$, and the ball submean inequality of theorem 3.1.3(2) gives

\[ V(x)\le \frac1{|B(x,r_0)|}\int _{B(x,r_0)}V\, \mathrm dV_{2n}\le c_0\| V\| _{L^1(L)},\qquad c_0=|B(0,r_0)|^{-1}; \]

put $c=c_0\| V\| _{L^1(L)}$. For $0<\varepsilon <r_0$ the regularization $V_\varepsilon =V*\rho _\varepsilon $ is smooth and plurisubharmonic on the neighborhood $\{ z:\operatorname {dist}(z,\overline B)<r_0\} $ of $\overline B$ (proposition 8.2.2), since this neighborhood lies in $\Omega _\varepsilon $. It satisfies $V_\varepsilon \le c$ on $\overline B$, since $V_\varepsilon (x)$ is an average of values of $V$ at points within distance $\varepsilon $ of $x$, and $\int _ B|V_\varepsilon |\, \mathrm{d}V_{2n}\le \int \rho _\varepsilon (y)\int _ B|V(x-y)|\, \mathrm dV_{2n}(x)\, \mathrm dV_{2n}(y)\le \| V\| _{L^1(L)}$ by Tonelli's theorem, since $B-y\subset L$ for $|y|\le \varepsilon $. Applying Step 3 to $V_\varepsilon -c$ gives

\[ \int _{B_\tau }(c-V_\varepsilon )\, S\wedge b^{n-q}\le C\bigl (\| V\| _{L^1(L)}+c|B|\bigr )\le C'\| V\| _{L^1(L)}. \]

As $\varepsilon \downarrow 0$, the nonnegative functions $c-V_\varepsilon $ increase to $c-V$, by (3.3), so monotone convergence gives the same bound for $\int _{B_\tau }(c-V)\, S\wedge b^{n-q}$. Since $V\le c$ on $B_\tau $, we have $|V|\le (c-V)+c$ there, and the mass of $S\wedge b^{n-q}$ on $B_\tau $ is bounded by a constant depending only on $B'$, $B$, $n$ and $q$, by theorem 9.1.3 applied to $\overline{B_\tau }\subset B=\operatorname{Int}\overline B$, since the modified $u_ l$ take values in $[-2,0]$ on $\overline B$. Since $S$ coincides with the original product on $B'\subset B_\tau $, by locality, this proves the estimate of Step 1 for $q\le n-1$. By the finite cover and the normalizations of Step 1, the theorem holds for $q\le n-1$, in every dimension.

Step 5: the case $q=n$. Consider $\mathbb C^{n+1}$ with coordinates $(z,w)$, the unit disc $\Delta \subset \mathbb C$, and the projection $p(z,w)=z$; let $\beta _ z$, $\beta _ w$ be the forms (7.4) in the variables $z$ and $w$, so that $\beta _ z+\beta _ w$ is the form $\beta $ of $\mathbb C^{n+1}$, and $\beta _ w=\mathrm dV_2(w)$. By proposition 8.2.3, the functions $u_ l\circ p$ are locally bounded and plurisubharmonic on $\Omega \times \Delta $, and $V\circ p$ is plurisubharmonic there, since it is not identically $-\infty $ on any connected component $\Omega _0\times \Delta $ of $\Omega \times \Delta $. Let $\mu =\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_1\wedge \cdots \wedge \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_ n$, a positive measure on $\Omega $. We claim that

\[ \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}(u_1\circ p)\wedge \cdots \wedge \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}(u_ n\circ p)\wedge (\beta _ z+\beta _ w)=\mu \otimes \mathrm{d}V_2. \]

If the $u_ l$ are smooth, then $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}(u_ l\circ p)=p^*\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_ l$, so the product of these $n$ factors is the pullback $p^*\mu $ of the $(n,n)$-form $\mu =g\, \mathrm dV_{2n}$ (proposition 9.1.2(1)). Its exterior product with $\beta _ z$ is $p^*(\mu \wedge \beta )=0$, since $\mu \wedge \beta $ has bidegree $(n+1,n+1)$ on $\mathbb C^ n$, and its exterior product with $\beta _ w$ is $g(z)\, \mathrm dV_{2n}(z)\wedge \mathrm dV_2(w)$, the density of $\mu \otimes \mathrm dV_2$.

In general, let $u_{l,\varepsilon }=u_ l*\rho _\varepsilon $, which are smooth plurisubharmonic functions on $\Omega _{\varepsilon _0}$ for $\varepsilon <\varepsilon _0$, decreasing to $u_ l$ as $\varepsilon \downarrow 0$ (proposition 8.2.2). By theorem 9.2.5, applied on $\Omega _{\varepsilon _0}\times \Delta $ to the functions $u_{l,\varepsilon }\circ p$, which decrease to $u_ l\circ p$, the left-hand sides for $u_{l,\varepsilon }$ converge weakly to the left-hand side for $u_ l$. By the same theorem on $\Omega _{\varepsilon _0}$, the measures $\mu _\varepsilon =\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_{1,\varepsilon }\wedge \cdots \wedge \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_{n,\varepsilon }$ converge weakly to $\mu $; hence $\mu _\varepsilon \otimes \mathrm dV_2\to \mu \otimes \mathrm dV_2$, because for a test function $\varphi $ on $\Omega _{\varepsilon _0}\times \Delta $ we have $\int \varphi \, \mathrm d(\mu _\varepsilon \otimes \mathrm dV_2)=\int \Phi \, \mathrm d\mu _\varepsilon $ with $\Phi (z)=\int _\Delta \varphi (z,w)\, \mathrm dV_2(w)$, a test function on $\Omega _{\varepsilon _0}$ (differentiate under the integral sign). Since $\varepsilon _0$ is arbitrary, the claim follows from the smooth case.

Now apply the case already proved in dimension $n+1$, with $q=n\le (n+1)-1$, to the compact sets $\widetilde K=K\times \{ |w|\le 1/2\} $ and $\widetilde L=L\times \{ |w|\le 3/4\} $ in $\Omega \times \Delta $; note that $\widetilde K\subset \operatorname{Int}\widetilde L$. By the claim and Fubini's theorem, the left-hand side of the estimate for $V\circ p$ and the $u_ l\circ p$ is $\frac\pi 4\int _ K|V|\, \mathrm d\mu $, while $\| V\circ p\| _{L^1(\widetilde L)}=\frac{9\pi }{16}\| V\| _{L^1(L)}$ and $\| u_ l\circ p\| _{L^\infty (\widetilde L)}=\| u_ l\| _{L^\infty (L)}$. Hence the estimate holds in $\mathbb C^ n$ with the constant $\frac94C_{\widetilde K,\widetilde L,n}$, which depends only on $K$, $L$ and $n$. This proves the case $q=n$. $\square$

The base current in theorem 9.3.1 is $T=1$, and for a general closed positive current $T$ no such estimate holds, already for $q=0$. Let $n\ge 1$, let $H=\{ z_1=0\} \subset \mathbb C^ n$, and let $T=[H]$ be the current of integration of example 7.2.7, a closed positive current of bidimension $(n-1,n-1)$. In the coordinates $z_2,\ldots ,z_ n$ of $H$, the restriction of $\beta $ to $H$ is the form (7.4) of $\mathbb C^{n-1}$, so $T\wedge \beta ^{n-1}/(n-1)!$ is Lebesgue measure on $H$. The function $V=\log |z_1|$ is plurisubharmonic (proposition 8.3.3), hence $\| V\| _{L^1(L)}<\infty $ for every compact $L$, but $V=-\infty $ on $H$. Therefore $\int _ K|V|\, T\wedge \beta ^{n-1}=\infty $ for the closed unit ball $K$, which meets $H$ in a set of positive Lebesgue measure on $H$.

Definition 9.3.2. Let $D\subset \mathbb {C}^ n$, $n\ge 1$, be a bounded open set. The Monge–Ampère capacity of a Borel set $E\subset D$ is

\[ \operatorname {Cap}_ D(E)=\sup \left\{ \int _ E(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}v)^ n: v\in \mathrm{PSH}(D),\ 0\le v\le 1\right\} . \]

The functions $v$ in this supremum are called competitors for $\operatorname {Cap}_ D$. The outer capacity of an arbitrary set $A\subset D$ is

\[ \operatorname {Cap}_ D^*(A)=\inf \{ \operatorname {Cap}_ D(G):A\subset G\subset D, \ G\text{ open}\} . \]
On a complex manifold we use these capacities in coordinate charts, taking for $D$ a bounded open set, usually a ball, which is relatively compact in the chart.

Proposition 9.3.3. Let $D\subset \mathbb C^ n$ be a bounded open set. The set functions $\operatorname {Cap}_ D$ and $\operatorname {Cap}^*_ D$ are increasing with respect to inclusion and countably subadditive. Moreover:

  1. $\operatorname {Cap}_ D(K)<\infty $ for every compact $K\subset D$, and $\operatorname {Cap}_ D(E)=\sup \{ \operatorname {Cap}_ D(K):K\subset E\text{ compact}\} $ for every Borel set $E\subset D$;

  2. if $E_ j\subset D$ are Borel sets increasing to $E$, then $\operatorname {Cap}_ D(E_ j)\to \operatorname {Cap}_ D(E)$;

  3. there is a constant $C_ D$ such that $|E|\le C_ D\operatorname {Cap}_ D(E)$ for every Borel set $E\subset D$, where $|E|$ is Lebesgue measure;

  4. if $B\Subset D$ is a ball and $K\subset B$ is compact, there is a constant $C$ such that, for every Borel set $E\subset K$,

    \[ \operatorname {Cap}_ D(E)\le \operatorname {Cap}_ B(E) \le C\operatorname {Cap}_ D(E), \]

    and the same inequalities hold for the outer capacities of arbitrary subsets of $K$;

  5. both capacities are invariant under biholomorphisms between bounded open sets: if $F\colon D\to D'$ is biholomorphic, then $\operatorname {Cap}_{D'}(F(E))=\operatorname {Cap}_ D(E)$ for Borel sets $E\subset D$ and $\operatorname {Cap}^*_{D'}(F(A))=\operatorname {Cap}^*_ D(A)$ for arbitrary sets $A\subset D$.

Proof. For each competitor $v$, the measure $(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}v)^ n$ is increasing and countably subadditive on Borel sets: $\int _{\bigcup _ jE_ j}(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}v)^ n\le \sum _ j\int _{E_ j}(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}v)^ n\le \sum _ j\operatorname {Cap}_ D(E_ j)$. Taking the supremum over $v$ gives the same properties for $\operatorname {Cap}_ D$. Outer capacity is increasing by definition. For countable subadditivity, given sets $A_ j$ with $\sum _ j\operatorname {Cap}^*_ D(A_ j)<\infty $ and $\varepsilon >0$, choose open sets $G_ j\supset A_ j$ with $\operatorname {Cap}_ D(G_ j)<\operatorname {Cap}^*_ D(A_ j)+\varepsilon 2^{-j}$; then $\operatorname {Cap}^*_ D(\bigcup _ jA_ j)\le \operatorname {Cap}_ D(\bigcup _ jG_ j)\le \sum _ j\operatorname {Cap}^*_ D(A_ j)+\varepsilon $.

Finiteness on a compact set $K$ follows from theorem 9.1.3 with $T=1$, applied to $K$ and a compact neighborhood $L$ of $K$ in $D$, since $\| v\| _{L^\infty (L)}\le 1$ for every competitor. Inner regularity of each Radon measure $(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}v)^ n$ (definition 1.1.1) gives $\int _ E(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}v)^ n=\sup _ K\int _ K(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}v)^ n\le \sup _ K\operatorname {Cap}_ D(K)$ over compact $K\subset E$, and taking the supremum over $v$ gives (1). For (2), $\operatorname {Cap}_ D(E_ j)\le \operatorname {Cap}_ D(E)$ by monotonicity, and for every competitor $v$ monotone convergence gives $\int _ E(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}v)^ n=\lim _ j\int _{E_ j}(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}v)^ n\le \lim _ j\operatorname {Cap}_ D(E_ j)$; taking the supremum over $v$ gives (2). For (3), choose $a\in D$ and $d>0$ with $D\subset B(a,d)$; the function $|z-a|^2/d^2$ is a competitor, and by (9.2) and proposition 9.1.2(3) its Monge–Ampère measure is $n!\pi ^{-n}d^{-2n}\, \mathrm dV_{2n}$. Hence (3) holds with $C_ D=\pi ^ nd^{2n}/n!$.

For (4), the first inequality holds because the restriction to $B$ of a competitor for $D$ is a competitor for $B$, with the same Monge–Ampère measure on $B$. For the second, write $B=B(a,r)$ and choose $\lambda >0$ such that $\rho =\lambda (|z-a|^2-r^2)<-1$ on a neighborhood of $K$. Given a competitor $v$ for $B$, let $w$ be the function equal to $\max \{ v-1,\rho \} $ on $B$ and to $\rho $ on $D\setminus B$. Since $v-1\le 0$ on $B$ and $\rho =0$ on $\partial B$, proposition 8.2.5 shows that $w$ is plurisubharmonic on $D$. It equals $v-1$ near $K$, satisfies $w\ge -1$ on $D$, and is bounded above by $\Lambda =\max \{ 0,\sup _ D\rho \} $, which does not depend on $v$. Thus $(w+1)/(\Lambda +1)$ is a competitor for $D$, and by locality its Monge–Ampère measure near $K$ is $(\Lambda +1)^{-n}(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}v)^ n$. This gives the second inequality with $C=(\Lambda +1)^ n$; the first inequality holds for all Borel subsets of $B$.

For the outer capacities in (4), let $K'$ be a compact neighborhood of $K$ in $B$; the constant obtained for $K'$ also serves for $K$, and we use it. If $A\subset K$ and $G$ is an open subset of $D$ or of $B$ containing $A$, then $G\cap \operatorname{Int}K'$ is open in both $B$ and $D$, contains $A$, lies in $K'$, and has no larger capacity than $G$. Applying the inequalities to these sets and taking infima over $G$ gives them for the outer capacities of $A$.

For (5), let $F\colon D\to D'$ be biholomorphic. By proposition 8.2.3, applied to $F$ and to $F^{-1}$, the map $v\mapsto v\circ F$ is a bijection from the competitors for $D'$ onto the competitors for $D$, and by proposition 9.1.2(5), $(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}(v\circ F))^ n=F^*\bigl ((\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}v)^ n\bigr )$. We claim that for every positive Radon measure $\mu $ on $D'$, regarded as a current of bidegree $(n,n)$, the pullback $F^*\mu $ is the image of $\mu $ under $F^{-1}$, that is, $\int \psi \, \mathrm d(F^*\mu )=\int \psi \circ F^{-1}\, \mathrm d\mu $ for every test function $\psi $ on $D$.
