If $\mu =g\, \mathrm dV_{2n}$ with $g$ smooth, then $F^*\mu =(g\circ F)\, |\det F'|^2\, \mathrm dV_{2n}$, because $F^*(\mathrm dz_1\wedge \cdots \wedge \mathrm dz_ n)=\det F'\, \mathrm dz_1\wedge \cdots \wedge \mathrm dz_ n$ and similarly for the conjugate forms; since $|\det F'|^2$ is the Jacobian determinant of $F$ as a map of $\mathbb R^{2n}$, the change of variables formula gives the claim. Both sides of the claimed identity are weakly continuous in $\mu $ (proposition 7.5.2), and on relatively compact open subsets of $D'$ every $\mu $ is the weak limit of its smooth regularizations, so the claim holds in general. Consequently $(F^*\mu )(F^{-1}(E'))=\mu (E')$ for Borel sets $E'\subset D'$, since two Radon measures that agree on test functions agree on Borel sets (proposition 1.4.3(4)). Applying this to $\mu =(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}v)^ n$ and $E'=F(E)$ and taking the supremum over $v$ gives $\operatorname {Cap}_ D(E)=\operatorname {Cap}_{D'}(F(E))$. Since $F$ is a homeomorphism, it maps the open sets containing a set $A$ bijectively onto the open sets containing $F(A)$, so the outer capacities agree as well. $\square$

Proposition 9.3.4. Let $D\subset \mathbb C^ n$ be a bounded open set, let $K,L\subset D$ be compact sets with $K\subset \operatorname{Int}L$, and let $V\in \mathrm{PSH}(D)$. There is a constant $C_{K,L}$, depending only on $K$, $L$ and $n$, such that

\[ \operatorname {Cap}_ D^*(K\cap \{ V<-s\} ) \le \frac{C_{K,L}}{s}\| V\| _{L^1(L)},\qquad s>0. \]

Moreover, let $E\subset D$ be a set locally contained in pole sets of plurisubharmonic functions, that is, every point of $D$ has an open neighborhood $W\subset D$ and a plurisubharmonic function $V_ W$ on $W$ with $E\cap W\subset \{ V_ W=-\infty \} $. (Such sets are called pluripolar in chapter 10.) Then $\operatorname {Cap}^*_ D(E\cap K)=0$ for every compact set $K\subset D$, and $E$ is contained in a Borel set which has measure zero for every measure $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_1\wedge \cdots \wedge \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_ n$ with locally bounded plurisubharmonic functions $u_1,\ldots ,u_ n$ on $D$.

Proof. Choose an open neighborhood $U$ of $K$ with compact closure in $\operatorname{Int}L$. For every competitor $v$, theorem 9.3.1, applied to $\overline U\subset \operatorname{Int}L$ with $q=n$ and $u_1=\cdots =u_ n=v$, gives

\[ \int _{U\cap \{ V<-s\} }(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}v)^ n\le s^{-1}\int _{\overline U}|V|\, (\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}v)^ n \le C s^{-1}\| V\| _{L^1(L)}. \]

Since $V$ is upper semicontinuous, $U\cap \{ V<-s\} $ is an open set containing $K\cap \{ V<-s\} $; taking the supremum over $v$ proves the estimate. Since $V$ is locally integrable (theorem 8.2.1), $\| V\| _{L^1(L)}<\infty $, and letting $s\to \infty $ gives $\operatorname {Cap}^*_ D(K\cap \{ V=-\infty \} )=0$.

Now let $E$ be as in the statement. Let $x\in D$, let $W$ and $V_ W$ be as in the hypothesis, and choose $r>0$ with $\overline{B(x,3r)}\subset W$; put $B_ x=B(x,3r)$, $K_ x=\overline{B(x,r)}$ and $L_ x=\overline{B(x,2r)}$. Then $E\cap B_ x\subset \{ V_ W=-\infty \} $, and the restriction of $V_ W$ to $B_ x$ is plurisubharmonic. The estimate just proved, applied in $B_ x$ to this restriction and to $K_ x\subset \operatorname{Int}L_ x$, gives $\operatorname {Cap}^*_{B_ x}(E\cap K_ x)=0$; since every open set in $B_ x$ is open in $D$, the first inequality of proposition 9.3.3(4) gives $\operatorname {Cap}^*_ D(E\cap K_ x)=0$. A compact set $K\subset D$ is covered by finitely many interiors $\operatorname{Int}K_ x$, and subadditivity gives $\operatorname {Cap}^*_ D(E\cap K)=0$. Finally, by theorem 9.3.1, applied on $B_ x$ to $K_ x\subset \operatorname{Int}L_ x$ with $q=n$, the function $V_ W$ is integrable on $K_ x$ for every measure $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_1\wedge \cdots \wedge \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_ n$ as in the statement, so the Borel set $K_ x\cap \{ V_ W=-\infty \} $ has measure zero for all these measures. Since $D$ has a countable basis, countably many sets $\operatorname{Int}K_ x$ cover $D$, and the union of the corresponding sets $K_ x\cap \{ V_ W=-\infty \} $ is a Borel set with the required property. $\square$

The next estimate controls, uniformly over all competitors, the set where two bounded plurisubharmonic functions with the same boundary values differ.

Proposition 9.3.5 (Błocki). Let $B$ be a ball, and let $u$ and $h$ be bounded plurisubharmonic functions on a neighborhood of $\overline B$ with $u\le h$ on $B$ and $u=h$ on a collar of $\partial B$. Let $v$ be a bounded plurisubharmonic function on a neighborhood of $\overline B$, and put $M=\sup _ Bv-\inf _ Bv$. Then

\[ \int _ B(h-u)^{n+1}(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}v)^ n \le (n+1)!M^ n\int _ B(h-u)(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u)^ n. \]

Proof. First suppose that $u$, $h$ and $v$ are smooth; then $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u$, $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}h$ and $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}v$ are positive $(1,1)$-forms, by proposition 8.2.2. Subtracting $\sup _ Bv$ from $v$, we may assume $-M\le v\le 0$ on $B$. Put $w=h-u\ge 0$; it vanishes on a collar of $\partial B$. Let $p\ge 2$, and let $R$ be a smooth closed strongly positive form of bidegree $(n-1,n-1)$; the exterior product of $R$ with a positive $(1,1)$-form is then a nonnegative multiple of the volume form, by proposition 7.2.1, since positive $(1,1)$-forms are strongly positive. Since $\mathrm{d}w\wedge \mathrm{d}^{\mathrm c}w$ is a positive $(1,1)$-form by (9.7) and $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}h\wedge R\ge 0$, the chain rule $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}(w^ p)=pw^{p-1}\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}w+p(p-1)w^{p-2}\mathrm dw\wedge \mathrm d^{\mathrm c}w$ gives

\[ \begin{aligned} \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}(w^ p)\wedge R& =\bigl (p w^{p-1}(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}h-\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u) +p(p-1)w^{p-2}\mathrm{d}w\wedge \mathrm{d}^{\mathrm c}w\bigr )\wedge R\\ & \ge -pw^{p-1}\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u\wedge R. \end{aligned} \]

Since $w^ p$ has compact support in $B$ and $R$ is closed, (9.1) gives $\int _ Bw^ p\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}v\wedge R=(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}(vR))(w^ p)=(vR)(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}(w^ p))$. As $v\le 0$ and $-v\le M$, the preceding inequality therefore yields

\[ \int _ Bw^ p\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}v\wedge R =\int _ Bv\, \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}(w^ p)\wedge R \le pM\int _ Bw^{p-1}\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u\wedge R. \]

For $k=1,\ldots ,n$, apply this with $p=n+2-k$ and $R=(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}v)^{n-k}\wedge (\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u)^{k-1}$, a product of positive $(1,1)$-forms, hence strongly positive (proposition 7.2.1) and closed. This gives

\[ \int _ Bw^{n+2-k}(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}v)^{n+1-k}\wedge (\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u)^{k-1}\le (n+2-k)M\int _ Bw^{n+1-k}(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}v)^{n-k}\wedge (\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u)^ k, \]

and chaining these $n$ inequalities from $k=1$ to $k=n$ gives the asserted inequality, since $(n+1)n\cdots 2=(n+1)!$.

In general, write $B=B(a,r)$ and let $V$ be a collar of $\partial B$ on which $u=h$. Since $\partial B$ is compact, there is $r''<r$ with $\{ z:r''\le |z-a|\le r\} \subset V$; choose $r'\in (r'',r)$ and put $B'=B(a,r')$. Then $h-u$ vanishes on $B\setminus \overline{B(a,r'')}$. Convolve $u$, $h$ and $v$ with the same radial kernel $\rho _\varepsilon $, with $\varepsilon <\min \{ (r'-r'')/2,r-r'\} $ so small that the regularizations are defined, smooth and plurisubharmonic on a neighborhood of $\overline{B'}$ (proposition 8.2.2). At points of $\overline{B'}$ they are averages over balls of radius $\varepsilon $ contained in $B$, where $u\le h$; hence $u_\varepsilon \le h_\varepsilon $ on $\overline{B'}$. Moreover $h_\varepsilon -u_\varepsilon $ vanishes on the collar $\{ z:r''+\varepsilon <|z-a|<r-\varepsilon \} $ of $\partial B'$ and outside $\overline{B(a,r''+\varepsilon )}$ in $B'$, hence outside the compact set $\overline{B(a,(r'+r'')/2)}\subset B'$. The values of $v_\varepsilon $ on $B'$ are averages of values of $v$ on $B$, so the oscillation of $v_\varepsilon $ on $B'$ is at most $M$.

Apply the smooth case on $B'$ and let $\varepsilon =\varepsilon _ k\downarrow 0$. The weights $(h_\varepsilon -u_\varepsilon )^{n+1}$ and $h_\varepsilon -u_\varepsilon $ are polynomials in decreasing sequences of bounded plurisubharmonic functions, and they, as well as their limits $(h-u)^{n+1}$ and $h-u$, vanish in $B'$ outside the fixed compact set $\overline{B(a,(r'+r'')/2)}$. Testing against a function $\chi \in C^\infty _ c(B')$ equal to $1$ on this set, corollary 9.2.6 lets both sides pass to the limit. Since $h-u$ vanishes on $B\setminus B'$, the limiting inequality on $B'$ is the asserted inequality on $B$. $\square$

In the next two sections we use the proposition through a capacity bound. Let $u$ and $h$ be as in proposition 9.3.5, and let $D\subset \mathbb C^ n$ be a bounded open set with $\overline B\subset D$. Every competitor $v$ for $\operatorname {Cap}_ D$ has oscillation at most $1$ on $B$. Hence Chebyshev's inequality and the proposition give, for every $\delta >0$,

9.20
\begin{equation} \begin{aligned} \operatorname {Cap}_ D\bigl (B\cap \{ h-u>\delta \} \bigr ) & \le \sup _ v\frac{1}{\delta ^{n+1}}\int _ B(h-u)^{n+1}(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}v)^ n\\ & \le \frac{(n+1)!}{\delta ^{n+1}}\int _ B(h-u)(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u)^ n, \end{aligned} \end{equation}

where the supremum is taken over all competitors $v$.

9.4 Quasi-continuity and convergence in capacity

Throughout this section and the next, $D\subset \mathbb {C}^ n$, $n\ge 1$, is a bounded open set. By theorem 4.5.2, locally integrable subharmonic functions are quasi-continuous in the sense of 1: they are continuous outside open sets of arbitrarily small relative capacity. We now define quasi-continuity with respect to the Monge–Ampère capacity instead, which is the meaning of the term in the rest of the book, prove that plurisubharmonic functions are quasi-continuous in this sense, and then show that the products are continuous under convergence in capacity.

Definition 9.4.1. A Borel function $f\colon D\to [-\infty ,\infty ]$ is quasi-continuous (for $\operatorname {Cap}_ D$) if for every compact set $K\subset D$ and every $\varepsilon >0$ there is an open set $G\subset D$ with $\operatorname {Cap}_ D(G)<\varepsilon $ such that the restriction of $f$ to $K\setminus G$ is continuous. Here $[-\infty ,\infty ]$ carries its order topology.

The same definition applies to a function defined on an open subset $W\subset D$, with compact sets $K\subset W$ and still with $\operatorname {Cap}_ D$. The sum $f_1+f_2$ and the difference $f_1-f_2$ of two real-valued quasi-continuous functions $f_1,f_2$ are quasi-continuous, and so is $\Phi \circ f_1$ for every continuous function $\Phi \colon \mathbb R\to \mathbb R$. Indeed, these functions are Borel. Given $K$ and $\varepsilon $, choose open sets $G_ i\subset D$ with $\operatorname {Cap}_ D(G_ i)<\varepsilon /2$ such that $f_ i$ is continuous on $K\setminus G_ i$, $i=1,2$. Then $G=G_1\cup G_2$ satisfies $\operatorname {Cap}_ D(G)<\varepsilon $ by subadditivity (proposition 9.3.3), and $f_1+f_2$ and $f_1-f_2$ are continuous on $K\setminus G$, as sums and differences of continuous real functions there; similarly, $\Phi \circ f_1$ is continuous on $K\setminus G_1$.

Theorem 9.4.2. Let $u\in \mathrm{PSH}(D)$. For every $\varepsilon >0$ there is an open set $G\subset D$ with $\operatorname {Cap}_ D(G)<\varepsilon $ which contains the pole set $\{ u=-\infty \} $ and such that the restriction of $u$ to $D\setminus G$ is finite and continuous. In particular $u$ is quasi-continuous.

Proof. Step 1: locally bounded $u$. Let $u$ be locally bounded, $K\subset D$ compact and $\varepsilon >0$; we find an open set $G\subset D$ with $\operatorname {Cap}_ D(G)<\varepsilon $ such that $u$ is continuous on $K\setminus G$. For each $x\in K$ choose $r_ x>0$ with $\overline{B(x,3r_ x)}\subset D$; finitely many balls $B(x_ i,r_{x_ i})$, $1\le i\le m$, cover $K$. Suppose that for each $i$ we find an open set $G_ i$ with $\operatorname {Cap}_ D(G_ i)<\varepsilon /m$ such that $u$ is continuous on $\overline{B(x_ i,r_{x_ i})}\setminus G_ i$. Then $G=\bigcup _ iG_ i$ satisfies $\operatorname {Cap}_ D(G)<\varepsilon $, and $u$ is continuous on $K\setminus G$, because the finitely many sets $(K\setminus G)\cap \overline{B(x_ i,r_{x_ i})}$ are closed in $K\setminus G$ and cover it, and $u$ is continuous on each of them. We may therefore assume that $K=\overline{B'}$, where $B'=B(x,r)$, $B=B(x,2r)$ and $B_1=B(x,3r)$ satisfy $\overline{B_1}\subset D$.

Subtracting a constant, we may assume that $-N\le u\le -1$ on $B_1$. Let $j>2/r$. Since $U=B(x,5r/2)$, a neighborhood of $\overline B$, lies at distance $r/2>1/j$ from $\mathbb C^ n\setminus B_1$, the regularization $u*\rho _{1/j}$ is smooth and plurisubharmonic on $U$ and takes values in $[-N,-1]$ there; moreover, $u*\rho _{1/j}$ decreases to $u$ as $j\to \infty $ (proposition 8.2.2). Put $\psi =|z-x|^2-4r^2$, choose $\delta >0$ with $\overline{B'}\subset \{ \psi <-\delta \} $, and let $g=\max \{ u,A\psi \} $ and $g_ j=\max \{ u*\rho _{1/j},A\psi \} $, with $A=N/\delta $, be the functions (9.8) on $U$. The $g_ j$ are continuous and plurisubharmonic on $U$ and decrease to $g$; moreover, $g=u$ on $\{ \psi <-\delta \} \supset \overline{B'}$, and $g_ j=g=A\psi $ on a collar of $\partial B$.

For $\delta '>0$ put $G_{j,\delta '}=\{ z\in B:g_ j(z)-g(z)>\delta '\} $; it is open, because $g_ j$ is continuous and $g$ is upper semicontinuous. By (9.20), applied with $h=g_ j$, $u=g$ and $\delta '$ in place of $\delta $,

\[ \operatorname {Cap}_ D(G_{j,\delta '})\le \frac{(n+1)!}{\delta '^{n+1}}\int _ B(g_ j-g)(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}g)^ n. \]

The right-hand side tends to zero as $j\to \infty $ by dominated convergence: $(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}g)^ n$ has finite mass on $B$, since $g$ is bounded and plurisubharmonic near $\overline B$, and the functions $g_ j-g\ge 0$ are bounded on $B$ uniformly in $j$ and decrease to zero. Choose $j_1<j_2<\cdots $ such that $G_ l=G_{j_ l,2^{-l}}$ satisfies $\operatorname {Cap}_ D(G_ l)<\varepsilon 2^{-l}$, and let $G=\bigcup _ lG_ l$. Then $\operatorname {Cap}_ D(G)<\varepsilon $, and on $B\setminus G$ we have $0\le g_{j_ l}-g\le 2^{-l}$ for every $l$. Thus $g$ is a uniform limit of continuous functions on $B\setminus G$, hence continuous there; in particular $u=g$ is continuous on $\overline{B'}\setminus G$.

Step 2: arbitrary $u$. Let $K\subset D$ be compact and $\varepsilon >0$. By proposition 9.3.4, applied to $K$ and a compact neighborhood $L$ of $K$ in $D$, there is $s>0$ with $\operatorname {Cap}^*_ D(K\cap \{ u<-s\} )<\varepsilon /2$, and hence an open set $G_1\supset K\cap \{ u<-s\} $ with $\operatorname {Cap}_ D(G_1)<\varepsilon /2$. By Step 1, applied to the locally bounded plurisubharmonic function $\max \{ u,-s\} $, there is an open set $G_2$ with $\operatorname {Cap}_ D(G_2)<\varepsilon /2$ such that $\max \{ u,-s\} $ is continuous on $K\setminus G_2$. On $K\setminus (G_1\cup G_2)$ we have $u\ge -s$, hence $u=\max \{ u,-s\} $. Thus $G=G_1\cup G_2$ contains $K\cap \{ u=-\infty \} $, $\operatorname {Cap}_ D(G)<\varepsilon $, and $u$ is finite and continuous on $K\setminus G$.

Step 3: a single exceptional set. Choose compact sets $K_ l$ with $K_ l\subset \operatorname{Int}K_{l+1}$ and $\bigcup _ lK_ l=D$. By Step 2 there are open sets $G^{(l)}\supset K_ l\cap \{ u=-\infty \} $ with $\operatorname {Cap}_ D(G^{(l)})<\varepsilon 2^{-l}$ such that $u$ is finite and continuous on $K_ l\setminus G^{(l)}$. Put $G=\bigcup _ lG^{(l)}$. Then $\operatorname {Cap}_ D(G)<\varepsilon $, and $G$ contains the pole set of $u$. Every $x\in D\setminus G$ lies in some $\operatorname{Int}K_ l$, and on the neighborhood $\operatorname{Int}K_ l\setminus G$ of $x$ in $D\setminus G$ the function $u$ is a restriction of the continuous function $u|_{K_ l\setminus G^{(l)}}$. Hence $u|_{D\setminus G}$ is finite and continuous. $\square$

In the proofs below we extend continuous functions from compact sets, using the following elementary case of Tietze's extension theorem.

Lemma 9.4.3. Let $(X,d)$ be a metric space, let $F\subset X$ be compact, and let $f\colon F\to \mathbb R$ be continuous with $|f|\le a$. Then $f$ has a continuous extension $g\colon X\to \mathbb R$ with $|g|\le a$.

Proof. If $F=\varnothing $ or $a=0$, take $g=0$. Otherwise, for $L>0$ put

\[ f_ L(x)=\max \Bigl \{ -a,\min \bigl \{ a,\inf _{y\in F}(f(y)+L\, d(x,y))\bigr \} \Bigr \} ,\qquad x\in X. \]

The infimum is finite, since $f\ge -a$, and as an infimum of functions that are Lipschitz with constant $L$ it is Lipschitz with constant $L$; hence $f_ L$ is continuous and $|f_ L|\le a$. We claim that $f_ L\to f$ uniformly on $F$ as $L\to \infty $. Taking $y=x$ shows $f_ L\le f$ on $F$. Given $\eta >0$, uniform continuity of $f$ on the compact set $F$ gives $r>0$ with $|f(y)-f(x)|<\eta $ for $x,y\in F$ with $d(x,y)<r$. Let $Lr>2a$ and $x\in F$. Points $y\in F$ with $d(x,y)\ge r$ give $f(y)+L\, d(x,y)\ge -a+Lr>a\ge f(x)$, and the other points give $f(y)+L\, d(x,y)>f(x)-\eta $. Hence the infimum is at least $f(x)-\eta $, and so is $f_ L(x)$, because $a\ge f(x)>f(x)-\eta $ and $-a\le f(x)$. This proves the claim. Consequently there is a continuous $g_1$ on $X$ with $|g_1|\le a$ and $|f-g_1|\le a/2$ on $F$. Applying the same construction to $f-g_1$ on $F$, we find a continuous $g_2$ with $|g_2|\le a/2$ and $|f-g_1-g_2|\le a/4$ on $F$, and inductively continuous $g_ k$ with $|g_ k|\le 2^{1-k}a$ and $|f-g_1-\cdots -g_ k|\le 2^{-k}a$ on $F$. The series $\sum _ kg_ k$ converges uniformly on $X$; its sum is continuous and equals $f$ on $F$, and clamping it to $[-a,a]$, that is, replacing it by $\max \{ -a,\min \{ a,\sum _ kg_ k\} \} $, does not change its values on $F$. $\square$

Lemma 9.4.4. Let $K,L\subset D$ be compact sets with $K\subset \operatorname{Int}L$, and let $f$ be a real Borel function which is defined and quasi-continuous on a neighborhood of $L$. Then, for every $\delta >0$,

\[ \operatorname {Cap}_ D^*(K\cap \{ |f|>\delta \} ) \le \operatorname {Cap}_ D(L\cap \{ |f|>\delta /2\} ). \]

Proof. Let $\eta >0$. By quasi-continuity, applied to the compact set $L$, there is an open set $G\subset D$ with $\operatorname {Cap}_ D(G)<\eta $ such that $f$ is continuous on $L\setminus G$; let $g$ be a continuous extension of $f|_{L\setminus G}$ to $\mathbb {C}^ n$ (lemma 9.4.3); here $f|_{L\setminus G}$ is bounded, being continuous on a compact set. The open set $G\cup (\operatorname{Int}L\cap \{ |g|>\delta /2\} )$ contains $K\cap \{ |f|>\delta \} $, since $K\subset \operatorname{Int}L$ and $g=f$ on $K\setminus G$. Its part outside $G$ is a Borel set contained in $L\cap \{ |f|>\delta /2\} $, because $g=f$ on $L\setminus G$. By monotonicity and subadditivity,

\[ \operatorname {Cap}_ D^*(K\cap \{ |f|>\delta \} )\le \eta +\operatorname {Cap}_ D(L\cap \{ |f|>\delta /2\} ). \]

Let $\eta \downarrow 0$. $\square$

Definition 9.4.5. A net $(f_ a)$ of Borel functions $f_ a\colon D\to [-\infty ,+\infty ]$ converges locally in capacity to a Borel function $f\colon D\to [-\infty ,+\infty ]$ if, for every compact set $K\subset D$ and every $\delta >0$,

\[ \operatorname {Cap}_ D^*(K\cap \{ f_ a>f+\delta \} )\longrightarrow 0, \qquad \operatorname {Cap}_ D^*(K\cap \{ f_ a<f-\delta \} )\longrightarrow 0. \]

Here $\pm \infty +\delta =\pm \infty $ and $\pm \infty -\delta =\pm \infty $.

For finite functions, $K\cap \{ |f_ a-f|>\delta \} $ is the union of the two sets in the definition, so by monotonicity and subadditivity of outer capacity the two conditions together are equivalent to $\operatorname {Cap}_ D^*(K\cap \{ |f_ a-f|>\delta \} )\to 0$. Convergence locally in capacity is a local property: it holds on $D$ if and only if, for every ball $B\Subset D$, it holds on $B$ with $\operatorname {Cap}_ B$ in place of $\operatorname {Cap}_ D$. Indeed, if it holds on $D$ and $K\subset B$ is compact, then proposition 9.3.3(4) gives $\operatorname {Cap}^*_ B(A)\le C\operatorname {Cap}^*_ D(A)$ for all $A\subset K$. Conversely, if it holds on every ball $B\Subset D$, cover a compact set $K\subset D$ by finitely many balls $B(x_ i,r_ i)$ with $\overline{B(x_ i,2r_ i)}\subset D$; for $A\subset K_ i=K\cap \overline{B(x_ i,r_ i)}$, proposition 9.3.3(4) gives $\operatorname {Cap}^*_ D(A)\le \operatorname {Cap}^*_{B(x_ i,2r_ i)}(A)$, and subadditivity over $i$ gives the condition on $D$. In particular, for functions on an open set $W\subset D$, convergence locally in capacity with $\operatorname {Cap}_ W$ is equivalent to the same condition, for compact subsets of $W$, with $\operatorname {Cap}_ D$, since both are equivalent to the condition on balls $B\Subset W$.

Proposition 9.4.6. Let $u_ j$ be locally bounded plurisubharmonic functions on $D$ decreasing to a locally bounded function $u$. Then $u_ j\to u$ locally in capacity. Moreover, a net of real Borel functions on $D$ which is uniformly bounded on each compact subset of $D$ and converges locally in capacity to a locally bounded real Borel function also converges in $L^1_{\mathrm{loc}}(D)$.

Proof. Let $K\subset D$ be compact and $\delta >0$. Since $u_ j\ge u$, the sets $\{ u_ j<u-\delta \} $ are empty, and we must show that $\operatorname {Cap}^*_ D(K\cap \{ u_ j-u>\delta \} )\to 0$. By theorem 8.3.1, $u$ is plurisubharmonic. Covering $K$ by finitely many balls $B(x,r)$ with $\overline{B(x,4r)}\subset D$ and using subadditivity of outer capacity, we may assume that $K\subset \overline{B(x,r)}$ for such a ball; put $L=\overline{B(x,3r/2)}$, $B=B(x,3r)$ and $B_1=B(x,4r)$.

We first make the functions coincide near $\partial B$. Since $u\le u_ j\le u_1$, all these functions are bounded on $B_1$ uniformly in $j$, so there are constants $c$ and $N\ge 1$ with $-N\le u_ j-c,\, u-c\le -1$ on $B_1$. Put $\psi =|z-x|^2-9r^2$, choose $\delta _0>0$ with $L\subset \{ \psi <-\delta _0\} $, and let $\hat u_ j=\max \{ u_ j-c,A\psi \} $ and $\hat u=\max \{ u-c,A\psi \} $, with $A=N/\delta _0$, be the functions (9.8) on $B_1$, with $\delta _0$ in place of $\delta $. They are bounded and plurisubharmonic on $B_1$, $\hat u_ j\downarrow \hat u$, $\hat u_ j-\hat u=u_ j-u$ on $L$, and $\hat u_ j=\hat u$ on a collar of $\partial B$. By (9.20), applied with $u=\hat u$ and $h=\hat u_ j$,

\[ \operatorname {Cap}_ D\bigl (B\cap \{ \hat u_ j-\hat u>\delta /2\} \bigr ) \le (n+1)!\, (2/\delta )^{n+1}\int _ B(\hat u_ j-\hat u)(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\hat u)^ n, \]

and the right-hand side tends to zero by dominated convergence, since $(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\hat u)^ n$ has finite mass on $B$ and $0\le \hat u_ j-\hat u\le \hat u_1-\hat u$ decreases to zero. The real function $w_ j=u_ j-u$ is quasi-continuous on $D$, as a difference of two real quasi-continuous functions (theorem 9.4.2 and the remark following definition 9.4.1), and $w_ j=\hat u_ j-\hat u$ on $L$. Hence lemma 9.4.4 gives

\[ \operatorname {Cap}^*_ D(K\cap \{ w_ j>\delta \} )\le \operatorname {Cap}_ D(L\cap \{ w_ j>\delta /2\} )\le \operatorname {Cap}_ D\bigl (B\cap \{ \hat u_ j-\hat u>\delta /2\} \bigr )\longrightarrow 0. \]

For the second assertion, let $f_ a\to f$ be as in the statement, let $K\subset D$ be compact, and let $M$ bound $|f_ a|$ and $|f|$ on $K$. Splitting $K$ into the Borel set where $|f_ a-f|>\delta $ and its complement, and using proposition 9.3.3(3) together with $\operatorname {Cap}_ D\le \operatorname {Cap}^*_ D$ on Borel sets, we get

\[ \int _ K|f_ a-f|\, \mathrm{d}V_{2n} \le 2MC_ D\operatorname {Cap}_ D^*(K\cap \{ |f_ a-f|>\delta \} ) +\delta |K|. \]

Taking the limit along the net and then letting $\delta \downarrow 0$ proves $L^1$ convergence on $K$. $\square$

Lemma 9.4.7. Let $B\subset \mathbb C^ n$ be a ball, and let $\mathcal U$ be a family of plurisubharmonic functions on $B$ which is uniformly bounded on each compact subset of $B$.

  1. For every compact set $K\subset B$ there is a constant $C$ such that, for all integers $0\le q\le n$, all $u_1,\ldots ,u_ q\in \mathcal U$ and all Borel sets $E\subset K$, the trace measure $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_1\wedge \cdots \wedge \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_ q\wedge \beta ^{n-q}$, and the total variation of every coefficient measure of $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_1\wedge \cdots \wedge \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_ q$, give $E$ mass at most $C\operatorname {Cap}_ B(E)$.

  2. Let $S_ j=\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_{1,j}\wedge \cdots \wedge \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_{q,j}$ with $u_{l,j}\in \mathcal U$, and suppose that $S_ j\to S$ weakly. Let $f_ j$ be real Borel functions on $B$, uniformly bounded on each compact subset of $B$, which converge locally in capacity (for $\operatorname {Cap}_ B$) to a real Borel function $f$ that is locally bounded and quasi-continuous. Then $f_ jS_ j\to fS$ weakly, and the same holds for the trace measures of $S_ j$ and $S$. This applies in particular to a fixed weight $f_ j=f$.

Proof. (1) Let $B=B(a,R)$, fix $K$, and choose $R'<R$ with $K\subset B'=B(a,R')$. Let $M\ge 1$ be a common bound for $|u|$ on $B'$, $u\in \mathcal U$. For $u_1,\ldots ,u_ q\in \mathcal U$ put $v_ l=(u_ l+M)/(2M)$ and $\phi =|z-a|^2/R'^2$; these functions take values in $[0,1]$ on $B'$, and so does

\[ F=\frac1n\bigl (v_1+\cdots +v_ q+(n-q)\phi \bigr ), \]

which is therefore a competitor for $\operatorname {Cap}_{B'}$. Expand $(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}F)^ n$ by additivity, positive homogeneity and symmetry (proposition 9.1.2, theorem 9.2.5). All terms are positive measures, and those in which each $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}v_ l$ occurs once and $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\phi $ occurs $n-q$ times add up to $c_{n,q}\, \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}v_1\wedge \cdots \wedge \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}v_ q\wedge (\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\phi )^{n-q}$, where $c_{n,q}=n^{-n}\, n!\, (n-q)^{n-q}/(n-q)!>0$ (with $0^0=1$) is the corresponding multinomial coefficient. Hence $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}v_1\wedge \cdots \wedge \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}v_ q\wedge (\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\phi )^{n-q}\le c_{n,q}^{-1}(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}F)^ n$. Since $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\phi =\beta /(\pi R'^2)$ and $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_ l=2M\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}v_ l$, it follows that $\int _ E\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_1\wedge \cdots \wedge \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_ q\wedge \beta ^{n-q}\le C\operatorname {Cap}_{B'}(E)$ for Borel $E\subset B'$. The bound for the coefficient measures follows from theorem 7.2.5, and proposition 9.3.3(4), applied to $B'\Subset B$ and $K$, replaces $\operatorname {Cap}_{B'}$ by $\operatorname {Cap}_ B$ on subsets of $K$.

(2) The weak limit $S$ is a positive current (theorem 7.2.5). Let $\sigma _ j$ be either the trace measure or a coefficient measure of $S_ j$, and $\sigma $ the corresponding measure of $S$; then $\int \psi \, \mathrm d\sigma _ j\to \int \psi \, \mathrm d\sigma $ for every test function $\psi $, since this integral is the value of $S_ j$ on a test form. Fix a continuous function $\chi $ with compact support in $B$ and a compact set $K\subset B$ with $\operatorname{Supp}\chi \subset \operatorname{Int}K$, and let $C$ be the constant of (1) for $K$; in particular $|\sigma _ j|(K)\le C\operatorname {Cap}_ B(K)$, which is finite by proposition 9.3.3(1). A similar bound holds for $\sigma $ on open subsets $U\subset \operatorname{Int}K$: for the trace measure, which is the vague limit of the trace measures of $S_ j$ (section 7.2), proposition 1.5.2 gives $\sigma (U)\le \varliminf _ j\sigma _ j(U)\le C\operatorname {Cap}_ B(U)$, and the total variations of the coefficient measures of the positive current $S$ are bounded by a constant multiple of its trace measure.

Since the total variations $|\sigma _ j|(K)$ are bounded, the convergence $\int \psi \, \mathrm d\sigma _ j\to \int \psi \, \mathrm d\sigma $ extends to continuous functions $\psi $ with support in $\operatorname{Int}K$: approximating $\psi $ uniformly by test functions supported in a fixed compact subset of $\operatorname{Int}K$ (lemma 1.4.1(5)) changes the integrals by at most $\sup |\psi -\psi _\varepsilon |$ times a bound for $|\sigma _ j|(K)$ and $|\sigma |(K)$.

Consider first the fixed weight $f$, and let $\eta >0$. Since $f$ is quasi-continuous, there is an open set $G\subset B$ with $\operatorname {Cap}_ B(G)<\eta $ such that $f$ is continuous on $K\setminus G$; let $g$ be a continuous extension of $f|_{K\setminus G}$ to $\mathbb C^ n$ with $\sup |g|\le \sup _ K|f|$ (lemma 9.4.3). Then

\[ \Bigl |\int \chi f\, \mathrm{d}\sigma _ j-\int \chi g\, \mathrm{d}\sigma _ j\Bigr |\le 2\sup _ K|f|\sup |\chi |\, |\sigma _ j|(G\cap \operatorname{Supp}\chi )\le C'\eta , \]

and similarly for $\sigma $, using the open set $G\cap \operatorname{Int}K$. Since $\chi g$ is continuous with support in $\operatorname{Int}K$, we have $\int \chi g\, \mathrm{d}\sigma _ j\to \int \chi g\, \mathrm{d}\sigma $. Hence $\varlimsup _ j|\int \chi f\, \mathrm{d}\sigma _ j-\int \chi f\, \mathrm{d}\sigma |\le 2C'\eta $ for every $\eta >0$, which proves $f\sigma _ j\to f\sigma $.

For varying weights, let $M_ K$ bound $|f_ j|$ and $|f|$ on $K$, and let $\delta >0$. By (1),

\[ \Bigl |\int \chi (f_ j-f)\, \mathrm{d}\sigma _ j\Bigr | \le \sup |\chi |\Bigl (\delta \, C\operatorname {Cap}_ B(K)+2M_ KC\operatorname {Cap}^*_ B\bigl (K\cap \{ |f_ j-f|>\delta \} \bigr )\Bigr ). \]

As $j\to \infty $, the second term tends to zero by convergence in capacity; then we let $\delta \downarrow 0$. Together with the fixed-weight case, this proves $f_ j\sigma _ j\to f\sigma $, and hence $f_ jS_ j\to fS$. $\square$

Theorem 9.4.8. Let $0\le q\le n$ be an integer. For $1\le l\le q$, let $(u_{l,j})_{j\ge 1}$ be a sequence of plurisubharmonic functions on $D$, uniformly bounded on each compact subset of $D$, which converges locally in capacity to a locally bounded plurisubharmonic function $u_ l$. Then

\[ \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_{1,j}\wedge \cdots \wedge \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_{q,j} \longrightarrow \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_1\wedge \cdots \wedge \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_ q \]

weakly. More generally, if $f_ j$ are real Borel functions on $D$, uniformly bounded on each compact subset of $D$, which converge locally in capacity to a locally bounded, quasi-continuous real Borel function $f$, then $f_ j\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_{1,j}\wedge \cdots \wedge \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_{q,j}\to f\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_1\wedge \cdots \wedge \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_ q$ weakly.

Proof. Weak convergence is local (lemma 9.2.4), so it suffices to prove the convergence on every ball $B\Subset D$. By the remark following definition 9.4.5, the convergence in capacity holds with $\operatorname {Cap}_ B$ in place of $\operatorname {Cap}_ D$ on every ball $B\Subset D$. The limit $f$ is quasi-continuous for $\operatorname {Cap}_ B$ as well: if $K\subset B$ is compact, $K'$ is a compact neighborhood of $K$ in $B$, and $G\subset D$ is open with $f$ continuous on $K'\setminus G$, then $G\cap \operatorname{Int}K'$ is open in $B$, $f$ is continuous on $K\setminus (G\cap \operatorname{Int}K')=K\setminus G$, and $\operatorname {Cap}_ B(G\cap \operatorname{Int}K')\le C\operatorname {Cap}_ D(G)$ by proposition 9.3.3(4) applied to $K'$. Finally, the functions $u_{l,j}$ and $u_ l$, restricted to $B$, form a family which is uniformly bounded on compact subsets of $B$. We may therefore work on such a ball and use lemma 9.4.7.

We argue by induction on $q$. For $q=0$ the product is $1$, and the weighted assertion is the second part of the lemma with $S_ j=1$. Let $q\ge 1$, and assume that $S_ j=\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_{2,j}\wedge \cdots \wedge \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_{q,j}$ converges weakly to $S=\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_2\wedge \cdots \wedge \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_ q$. The functions $u_{1,j}$ converge in capacity to $u_1$, which is quasi-continuous by theorem 9.4.2; hence the second part of the lemma gives $u_{1,j}S_ j\to u_1S$, and applying $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}$ proves the unweighted assertion for $q$ factors. The weighted assertion then follows from the second part of the lemma once more. $\square$

Unlike theorem 9.2.5, theorem 9.4.8 concerns only products with the base current $T=1$.

9.5 Increasing limits and exceptional envelopes

The supremum of an increasing sequence of plurisubharmonic functions need not be upper semicontinuous, and it may differ from its upper semicontinuous regularization on an exceptional set. For instance, on the unit ball of $\mathbb C^ n$ the functions $\max \{ j^{-1}\log |z_1|,-1\} $ are plurisubharmonic (proposition 8.3.3, proposition 8.2.5) and increase with $j$, since $\log |z_1|<0$ there; their supremum equals $0$ where $z_1\ne 0$ and $-1$ on the hyperplane $\{ z_1=0\} $, while its upper semicontinuous regularization is $0$. We show that the products are nevertheless continuous along increasing sequences. The proof follows the pattern of the decreasing case: the inequality $u_{0,j}\le u_0$ bounds weak limits from above, while the quasi-continuity of the fixed weights, through lemma 9.4.7, supplies the lower bound for the masses. As a consequence, the exceptional set of an upper envelope has outer capacity zero.

Theorem 9.5.1. Let $0\le q\le n$ be an integer. For $0\le l\le q$, let $(u_{l,j})_{j\ge 1}$ be an increasing sequence of locally bounded plurisubharmonic functions on $D$ which converges almost everywhere to a locally bounded plurisubharmonic function $u_ l$. Then

\[ \begin{aligned} \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_{1,j}\wedge \cdots \wedge \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_{q,j} & \longrightarrow \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_1\wedge \cdots \wedge \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_ q,\\ u_{0,j}\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_{1,j}\wedge \cdots \wedge \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_{q,j} & \longrightarrow u_0\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_1\wedge \cdots \wedge \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_ q \end{aligned} \]

weakly. Moreover $u_{l,j}\to u_ l$ locally in capacity for every $l$.

Proof. We first note that $u_{l,j}\le u_ l$ everywhere. Indeed, $u_{l,j}\le u_ l$ almost everywhere, so the plurisubharmonic functions $\max \{ u_{l,j},u_ l\} $ and $u_ l$ agree almost everywhere, and hence everywhere, by the uniqueness of plurisubharmonic representatives in theorem 8.2.1, applied on each connected component of $D$. Consequently $u_{l,1}\le u_{l,j}\le u_ l$, and the sequences are uniformly bounded on each compact subset of $D$.

As in the proof of theorem 9.2.5, we prove the weighted assertion by induction on $q$, for all bounded open sets $D$ and all sequences as in the statement; convergence in capacity is proved at the end. For $q=0$ it follows from dominated convergence with respect to Lebesgue measure. Let $q\ge 1$ and assume the weighted assertion for $q-1$ factors; applying $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}$ to it, with the weight $u_{1,j}$, gives the unweighted assertion for $q$ factors. Put $S_ j=\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_{1,j}\wedge \cdots \wedge \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_{q,j}$ and $S=\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_1\wedge \cdots \wedge \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_ q$, so that $S_ j\to S$ weakly.

Weak convergence is a local property (lemma 9.2.4). Fix $x\in D$ and $r>0$ with $\overline{B(x,4r)}\subset D$, and put $B=B(x,3r)$ and $B_1=B(x,4r)$; we prove the convergence on $B(x,2r)$. Subtracting constants does not affect the assertions, since the unweighted assertion for $q$ factors is known, so we may assume that $-N\le u_{l,j},u_ l\le -1$ on $B_1$ for all $l$ and $j$, where $N\ge 1$. Put $\psi =|z-x|^2-9r^2$, choose $\delta _0>0$ such that $\overline{B(x,2r)}\subset \{ \psi <-\delta _0\} $, and replace each function $v$ among the $u_{l,j}$ and $u_ l$ by the function $\max \{ v,A\psi \} $ of (9.8), with $U=B_1$, $\delta _0$ in place of $\delta $ and $A=N/\delta _0$. The new functions are plurisubharmonic on $B_1$ and take values in $[-N,0)$ on $B$; the new sequences still increase, satisfy $u_{l,j}\le u_ l$ and converge almost everywhere. The functions do not change on $B(x,2r)$, and by locality neither do the products. On the collar $V=\{ z\in B_1:\psi (z)>-1/A\} $ of $\partial B$ the new functions coincide with $A\psi $.

By locality, on $V$ all the currents considered below coincide with fixed currents, independent of $j$ and $k$. Hence, if $\chi \in C^\infty _ c(B)$ equals $1$ on $B\setminus V$, the integral over $B$ of each of these currents splits into its integral against $\chi $, to which weak convergence applies, and its integral against $(1-\chi )\mathbf1_ B$, which only involves values on $V$ and therefore does not change; we use this without further comment. The currents $-u_{0,j}S_ j$ are positive on $B$, and their masses on compact subsets of $B$ are bounded uniformly in $j$ by theorem 9.1.3; by the compactness part of theorem 7.2.5, every subsequence of $(u_{0,j}S_ j)$ has a further subsequence converging weakly on $B$. Let $\Theta $ be the limit of such a subsequence.

Since $u_{0,j}\le u_0$, the currents $(u_0-u_{0,j})S_ j$ are positive. Let $\varphi $ be a strongly positive test form on $B$ of complementary bidegree. The positive measures $-u_0S_ j\wedge \varphi $ have bounded masses on compact sets, so by theorem 1.5.5 a further subsequence of $u_0S_ j\wedge \varphi $ converges weakly to some $\nu $, and lemma 9.2.2, applied to the constant sequence $f_ j=u_0$ and the measures $S_ j\wedge \varphi \to S\wedge \varphi $, gives $\nu \le u_0S\wedge \varphi $. The measures $(u_0-u_{0,j})S_ j\wedge \varphi $ are positive and converge along this subsequence to $\nu -\Theta \wedge \varphi $, which is therefore positive. Hence $\Theta \wedge \varphi \le u_0S\wedge \varphi $. Evaluating this inequality of measures on a nonnegative test function equal to $1$ on $\operatorname{Supp}\varphi $, we get $(u_0S-\Theta )(\varphi )\ge 0$. Thus $u_0S-\Theta $ is a positive current on $B$, and it vanishes on $V$, since all the currents $u_{0,j}S_ j$ and $u_0S$ coincide there with $A\psi (A\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\psi )^ q$. Write $\gamma =\beta ^{n-q}$.

For fixed $k$ and all $j\ge k$, positivity of $S_ j\wedge \gamma $ and $u_{0,j}\ge u_{0,k}$ give

\[ \int _ Bu_{0,j}S_ j\wedge \gamma \ge \int _ Bu_{0,k}S_ j\wedge \gamma . \]

Let $j\to \infty $ along the subsequence defining $\Theta $. The left-hand side tends to $\int _ B\Theta \wedge \gamma $. The fixed weight $u_{0,k}$ is quasi-continuous for $\operatorname {Cap}_ B$ by theorem 9.4.2, and the functions $u_{l,j}$, $u_ l$ form a family which is uniformly bounded on $B$; so by lemma 9.4.7(2) the right-hand side tends to $\int _ Bu_{0,k}S\wedge \gamma $. By the second assertion of corollary 9.2.6, applied with $a=u_{0,k}$, $b=u_1$ and $R=\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_2\wedge \cdots \wedge \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_ q\wedge \gamma $, and by symmetry,

\[ \int _ Bu_{0,k}S\wedge \gamma =\int _ Bu_1\, \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_{0,k}\wedge \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_2\wedge \cdots \wedge \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_ q\wedge \gamma . \]

As $k\to \infty $, the unweighted assertion for $q$ factors, already established, gives $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_{0,k}\wedge \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_2\wedge \cdots \wedge \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_ q\to \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_0\wedge \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_2\wedge \cdots \wedge \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_ q$ weakly, and lemma 9.4.7, with the fixed quasi-continuous weight $u_1$, passes the integral to the limit. A second application of corollary 9.2.6 identifies the limit with $\int _ Bu_0\, \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_1\wedge \cdots \wedge \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_ q\wedge \gamma =\int _ Bu_0S\wedge \gamma $. We have shown that $\int _ B\Theta \wedge \gamma \ge \int _ Bu_0S\wedge \gamma $.

Since $u_0S-\Theta $ is positive with compact support in $B$, its trace measure has total mass $\int _ B(u_0S-\Theta )\wedge \gamma \le 0$ and therefore vanishes, and $\Theta =u_0S$ by theorem 7.2.5. Thus every subsequence of $(u_{0,j}S_ j)$ has a further subsequence converging weakly to $u_0S$ on $B$. Hence the whole sequence converges to $u_0S$ on $B$: otherwise there would be a test form $\varphi $ on $B$ and a subsequence along which $|(u_{0,j}S_ j-u_0S)(\varphi )|$ stays bounded away from zero, and no further subsequence of it could converge to $u_0S$. Since the modifications change neither the functions nor the weighted products on $B(x,2r)$, the original weighted products converge on $B(x,2r)$; this completes the induction.

Now that both assertions are known for every $q\le n$, we prove convergence in capacity. Fix $l$, a compact set $K\subset D$ and $\delta >0$. Since $u_{l,j}\le u_ l$, the sets $\{ u_{l,j}>u_ l+\delta \} $ are empty. Covering $K$ by finitely many balls and using subadditivity of outer capacity, we may assume that $K\subset \overline{B(x,r)}$ with $\overline{B(x,4r)}\subset D$; put $L=\overline{B(x,3r/2)}$. Let $\hat u_{l,j}$ and $\hat u_ l$ be the functions obtained by the modification above, on $B=B(x,3r)$; on $\{ \psi <-\delta _0\} \supset L$ we have $\hat u_ l-\hat u_{l,j}=u_ l-u_{l,j}$. These functions are bounded and plurisubharmonic on $B_1$, they satisfy $\hat u_{l,j}\le \hat u_ l$, and both coincide with $A\psi $ on the collar $V$ of $\partial B$. By (9.20), applied with $u=\hat u_{l,j}$ and $h=\hat u_ l$,

\[ \operatorname {Cap}_ D\bigl (B\cap \{ \hat u_ l-\hat u_{l,j}>\delta /2\} \bigr )\le \frac{(n+1)!\, 2^{n+1}}{\delta ^{n+1}}\int _ B(\hat u_ l-\hat u_{l,j})(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\hat u_{l,j})^ n. \]

The right-hand side tends to zero: $\int _ B\hat u_ l(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\hat u_{l,j})^ n\to \int _ B\hat u_ l(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\hat u_ l)^ n$ by the unweighted assertion and lemma 9.4.7(2) with the fixed quasi-continuous weight $\hat u_ l$, and $\int _ B\hat u_{l,j}(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\hat u_{l,j})^ n\to \int _ B\hat u_ l(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\hat u_ l)^ n$ by the weighted assertion with all factors and the weight equal to $\hat u_{l,j}$; as before, the integrals over $B$ are split by a cutoff. The real function $u_ l-u_{l,j}$ is quasi-continuous on $D$ (theorem 9.4.2 and the remark following definition 9.4.1), so lemma 9.4.4 gives

\[ \begin{aligned} \operatorname {Cap}^*_ D\bigl (K\cap \{ u_ l-u_{l,j}>\delta \} \bigr )& \le \operatorname {Cap}_ D\bigl (L\cap \{ u_ l-u_{l,j}>\delta /2\} \bigr )\\ & \le \operatorname {Cap}_ D\bigl (B\cap \{ \hat u_ l-\hat u_{l,j}>\delta /2\} \bigr )\longrightarrow 0. \end{aligned} \]
$\square$

Corollary 9.5.2. Let $\mathcal F$ be a nonempty family of plurisubharmonic functions on $D$ which is locally uniformly bounded above, and let $F=\sup _{v\in \mathcal F}v$ be its pointwise supremum and $F^*$ its upper semicontinuous regularization. Then $\operatorname {Cap}^*_ D(K\cap \{ F<F^*\} )=0$ for every compact set $K\subset D$.

Proof. By theorem 8.3.2, $u=F^*$ is plurisubharmonic, and there is a countable subfamily $\{ w_1,w_2,\ldots \} \subset \mathcal F$ with $(\sup _ kw_ k)^*=u$. The functions $v_ j=\max \{ w_1,\ldots ,w_ j\} $ are plurisubharmonic (proposition 8.2.5) and increase to $G=\sup _ kw_ k$, and $G=G^*=u$ almost everywhere by theorem 8.3.2, applied to the countable family. Fix an integer $m\ge 1$. The functions $\max \{ v_ j,-m\} $ are locally bounded and plurisubharmonic on $D$, and they increase almost everywhere to the locally bounded plurisubharmonic function $\max \{ u,-m\} $, so by theorem 9.5.1 they converge to it locally in capacity. For rational $\delta >0$, the set where $\sup _ j\max \{ v_ j,-m\} <\max \{ u,-m\} -\delta $ is contained in $\{ \max \{ v_ k,-m\} <\max \{ u,-m\} -\delta \} $ for every $k$, and the outer capacities of the intersections of the latter sets with a compact set tend to zero as $k\to \infty $. Taking the union over rational $\delta $, countable subadditivity shows that the set

\[ E_ m=\Bigl \{ \sup _ j\max \{ v_ j,-m\} <\max \{ u,-m\} \Bigr \} \]

meets every compact set in a set of outer capacity zero.

If $\sup _ jv_ j(x)<u(x)$, then $u(x)>-\infty $, so $u(x)>-m$ for some integer $m$, and then $x\in E_ m$. Hence $\{ \sup _ jv_ j<u\} \subset \bigcup _ mE_ m$ also meets every compact set in a set of outer capacity zero. Finally, $\sup _ jv_ j\le F\le u$, so $\{ F<F^*\} \subset \{ \sup _ jv_ j<u\} $. (Since outer capacity is defined for all sets, we need not know whether $\{ F<F^*\} $ is Borel.) $\square$

9.6 The homogeneous Dirichlet problem and balayage

A plurisubharmonic function is maximal if it cannot be increased on a compact set without losing plurisubharmonicity (definition 9.6.5). We show in theorem 9.6.6 that, for locally bounded functions, maximality is equivalent to $(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u)^ n=0$. The proof replaces $u$ on a ball by a solution $H$ of the homogeneous equation $(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}H)^ n=0$ with the same boundary values (corollary 9.6.4); in chapter 10 the same replacement shows that relative extremal functions have vanishing Monge–Ampère measure away from the sets on which they are constrained. We construct $H$ as an upper envelope. To show that it solves the equation, we bound its second differences by composing with automorphisms of the ball (lemma 9.6.1), which makes it twice differentiable almost everywhere (lemma 9.6.2).

Lemma 9.6.1. Let $B\subset \mathbb C^ n$ be the unit ball. For $a\in B$ and $h\in \mathbb C^ n$ with $|h|$ small, depending on $a$, there are biholomorphic automorphisms $S_{a,h}$ of $B$ such that $S_{a,0}$ is the identity and $S_{a,h}(a)=a+h$. Each $S_{a,h}$ extends to a holomorphic map on a neighborhood of $\overline B$ which maps $\partial B$ onto $\partial B$. For every compact set $K\subset B$ there is $h_0>0$ such that the map $(a,h,z)\mapsto S_{a,h}(z)$ is defined and smooth on an open subset of $\mathbb C^ n\times \mathbb C^ n\times \mathbb C^ n$ containing $K\times \overline{B(0,h_0)}\times \overline B$, and holomorphic in $z$ there. In particular, there is a constant $C$ such that, for $a\in K$, $|h|\le h_0$ and $z\in \overline B$, the first and second derivatives of $S_{a,h}(z)$ with respect to the real variable $h\in \mathbb C^ n=\mathbb R^{2n}$ are bounded by $C$.

Proof. Use the Hermitian product $\langle z,a\rangle =\sum z_ j\bar a_ j$, put $s_ a=\sqrt{1-|a|^2}$, and define

\[ T_ a(z)=\frac{s_ a z+\langle z,a\rangle a/(1+s_ a)-a}{1-\langle z,a\rangle }. \]

This expression depends smoothly on $(a,z)$ in the open set $\{ (a,z):|a|<1,\ |a||z|<1\} $, where the denominator does not vanish, and it is holomorphic in $z$; moreover $T_0$ is the identity. If $U$ is a unitary matrix, then $\langle Uz,Ua\rangle =\langle z,a\rangle $ and $s_{Ua}=s_ a$, so $T_{Ua}(Uz)=UT_ a(z)$. Given $a\ne 0$, choose $U$ with $Ua=(t,0,\ldots ,0)$, $t=|a|$; it therefore suffices to verify the following identities for $a=(t,0,\ldots ,0)$ with $0\le t<1$, and also for $-a=(-t,0,\ldots ,0)$. Put $s=s_ a=\sqrt{1-t^2}$. Then $\langle z,a\rangle =tz_1$, and the first component of the numerator of $T_ a(z)$ is $sz_1+t^2z_1/(1+s)-t=z_1-t$, because $s(1+s)+t^2=s+s^2+t^2=1+s$. Hence

\[ \begin{aligned} T_ a(z)& =\left(\frac{z_1-t}{1-tz_1}, \frac{s\, z_2}{1-tz_1},\ldots , \frac{s\, z_ n}{1-tz_1}\right),\\ T_{-a}(w)& =\left(\frac{w_1+t}{1+tw_1}, \frac{s\, w_2}{1+tw_1},\ldots , \frac{s\, w_ n}{1+tw_1}\right), \end{aligned} \]

the second formula by the same computation with $t$ replaced by $-t$. In particular $T_ a(a)=0$ and $T_{-a}(0)=a$. If $w=T_ a(z)$, then

\[ w_1+t=\frac{(1-t^2)z_1}{1-tz_1},\qquad 1+tw_1=\frac{1-t^2}{1-tz_1}, \]

so the first component of $T_{-a}(w)$ is $z_1$ and the others are $s\cdot sz_ k/(1-tz_1)\cdot (1-tz_1)/(1-t^2)=z_ k$; thus $T_{-a}\circ T_ a=\mathrm{id}$, and by symmetry $T_ a\circ T_{-a}=\mathrm{id}$. Finally,

\[ |1-tz_1|^2-|z_1-t|^2-(1-t^2)\sum _{k\ge 2}|z_ k|^2=(1-t^2)(1-|z_1|^2)-(1-t^2)\sum _{k\ge 2}|z_ k|^2, \]

because the terms $\mp 2t\operatorname{Re}z_1$ cancel; dividing by $|1-tz_1|^2$ gives

\[ 1-|T_ a(z)|^2=\frac{(1-|a|^2)(1-|z|^2)}{|1-\langle z,a\rangle |^2}. \]

Hence $T_ a$ maps $B$ into $B$ and the sphere $\partial B$ into itself, and the same holds for $T_{-a}$; since $T_{-a}$ is inverse to $T_ a$, the map $T_ a$ is a biholomorphic automorphism of $B$ which maps $\partial B$ onto itself.

Set $S_{a,h}=T_{-(a+h)}\circ T_ a$ for $|a+h|<1$. It maps $a$ to $T_{-(a+h)}(0)=a+h$ and is the identity for $h=0$. The map $T_ a$ is holomorphic on the neighborhood $\{ z:|a||z|<1\} $ of $\overline B$, and, as we have just seen, it maps $\overline B$ into $\overline B$ and $\partial B$ onto $\partial B$; the same holds for $T_{-(a+h)}$. Hence $S_{a,h}$ is holomorphic on the neighborhood $\{ z:|a||z|<1,\ |a+h||T_ a(z)|<1\} $ of $\overline B$, and it maps $\partial B$ onto $\partial B$. Let $K\subset B$ be compact (we may assume $K\neq \varnothing $), put $t_ K=\max _ K|a|<1$, and choose $h_0>0$ with $t_ K+3h_0<1$. On the open set $O$ of all $(a,h,z)$ with $|a|<t_ K+h_0$, $|h|<2h_0$, $|a||z|<1$ and $(t_ K+3h_0)|T_ a(z)|<1$, we have $|a+h|<1$ and $|a+h||T_ a(z)|<1$, so $(a,h,z)\mapsto S_{a,h}(z)$ is defined and smooth on $O$, and holomorphic in $z$. Since $|T_ a(z)|\le 1$ for $|z|\le 1$, the set $O$ contains the compact set $K\times \overline{B(0,h_0)}\times \overline B$, on which the continuous first and second derivatives of $S_{a,h}(z)$ with respect to $h$ are therefore bounded by a constant $C$. $\square$

A plurisubharmonic function whose second differences are bounded above is locally of class $C^{1,1}$, and its Monge–Ampère measure is then given by the determinant of its complex Hessian.

Lemma 9.6.2. Let $U\subset \mathbb C^ n$ be open, $n\ge 1$, and let $H$ be a locally bounded plurisubharmonic function on $U$. Suppose that for every compact set $K\subset U$ there are constants $C_ K\ge 0$ and $h_ K>0$ such that, for all $a\in K$ and all $h\in \mathbb C^ n$ with $|h|\le h_ K$, the points $a\pm h$ lie in $U$ and

9.29
\begin{equation} H(a+h)+H(a-h)-2H(a)\le 2C_ K|h|^2\qquad (a\in K,\ |h|\le h_ K). \end{equation}

Then $H$ is continuous, $H\in C^{1,1}_{\mathrm{loc}}(U)$, and the distributional real Hessian of $H$ is a matrix $A=(A_{ab})$ of measurable functions which are bounded on compact subsets of $U$. Almost every $x\in U$ satisfies

\[ H(x+h)=H(x)+\nabla H(x)\cdot h+\tfrac 12h^ tA(x)h+o(|h|^2)\qquad (h\to 0). \]

Moreover, let $H_{j\bar k}$ be the coefficients of the distributional complex Hessian of $H$, which are fixed complex linear combinations of the $A_{ab}$. Then the matrix $(H_{j\bar k})$ is positive semidefinite almost everywhere, and

\[ (\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}H)^ n=\frac{n!}{\pi ^ n}\det (H_{j\bar k})\, \mathrm dV_{2n}. \]

Proof. Let $H_\varepsilon =H*\rho _\varepsilon $. Fix $a_0\in U$ and $r>0$ with $\overline{B(a_0,4r)}\subset U$, put $K=\overline{B(a_0,3r)}$, and let $M_0=\sup _ K|H|$, which is finite because $H$ is locally bounded. For $0<\varepsilon <r$, the regularization $H_\varepsilon $ is smooth and plurisubharmonic on a neighborhood of $\overline{B(a_0,2r)}$ (proposition 8.2.2), and $|H_\varepsilon |\le M_0$ on $\overline{B(a_0,2r)}$, since its values there are averages of values of $H$ on $K$. For $x\in \overline{B(a_0,2r)}$ and $|h|\le h_ K$,

\[ \begin{aligned} & H_\varepsilon (x+h)+H_\varepsilon (x-h)-2H_\varepsilon (x)\\ & \qquad =\int \bigl (H(x-y+h)+H(x-y-h)-2H(x-y)\bigr )\rho _\varepsilon (y)\, \mathrm dy\le 2C_ K|h|^2, \end{aligned} \]

by (9.29), because $x-y\in K$ for $|y|\le \varepsilon $. Replacing $h$ by $th$ with $0<t\le 1$, dividing by $t^2$ and letting $t\to 0$, we get $D^2H_\varepsilon (x)[h,h]\le 2C_ K|h|^2$ for $|h|\le h_ K$, and hence, by homogeneity, for all $h\in \mathbb C^ n=\mathbb R^{2n}$. On the other hand, the complex Hessian of the smooth plurisubharmonic function $H_\varepsilon $ is positive semidefinite (proposition 8.2.2), and $D^2H_\varepsilon [h,h]+D^2H_\varepsilon [\mathrm ih,\mathrm ih]=4\sum _{j,k}(H_\varepsilon )_{j\bar k}h_ j\bar h_ k\ge 0$; hence $D^2H_\varepsilon (x)[h,h]\ge -2C_ K|h|^2$ as well. Thus the second derivatives of the $H_\varepsilon $ are bounded on $\overline{B(a_0,2r)}$ by a constant $\Lambda _0$ independent of $\varepsilon $. For $x\in \overline{B(a_0,r)}$ and a unit vector $e$, Taylor's formula

\[ H_\varepsilon (x+re)=H_\varepsilon (x)+r\, \partial _ eH_\varepsilon (x)+\int _0^ r(r-t)D^2H_\varepsilon (x+te)[e,e]\, \mathrm dt \]

gives $|\partial _ eH_\varepsilon (x)|\le 2M_0/r+\Lambda _0r=\Lambda $. By the mean value theorem on the convex set $B(a_0,r)$, $|H_\varepsilon (x)-H_\varepsilon (y)|\le \Lambda |x-y|$ for $x,y\in B(a_0,r)$. Since $H_\varepsilon \to H$ pointwise as $\varepsilon \downarrow 0$, by (3.3), the same inequality holds for $H$. Hence $H$ is continuous on $B(a_0,r)$, and, as $a_0$ was arbitrary, on $U$.

Moreover, $H_\varepsilon \to H$ in $L^1$ on compact subsets of $U$, by dominated convergence, and hence in the sense of distributions; and every ball $B_0\Subset U$ is covered by finitely many balls $B(a_0,2r)$ as above, so the second derivatives of the $H_\varepsilon $ are bounded on $B_0$ uniformly in small $\varepsilon $. By the converse part of proposition 1.7.5, the distributional real Hessian of $H$ has locally bounded measurable coefficients $A_{ab}$; hence $H\in C^{1,1}_{\mathrm{loc}}(U)$, and almost every $x\in U$ satisfies the second-order expansion of the statement.

The functions $H_{j\bar k}$ are bounded measurable functions on compact subsets of $U$. By proposition 1.4.3, the complex Hessian of $H_\varepsilon $ has the coefficients $(H_{j\bar k})_\varepsilon $; these are bounded on compact sets uniformly in small $\varepsilon $, and they converge to $H_{j\bar k}$ at every Lebesgue point of $H_{j\bar k}$, hence almost everywhere (theorem 1.7.3). In particular, the matrix $(H_{j\bar k})$ is positive semidefinite almost everywhere, as an almost everywhere limit of the positive semidefinite complex Hessians of the smooth plurisubharmonic functions $H_\varepsilon $. Since $H$ is continuous, $H_\varepsilon \to H$ locally uniformly (lemma 1.4.1(1)), so $(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}H_\varepsilon )^ n\to (\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}H)^ n$ weakly on every ball $B_0\Subset U$ as $\varepsilon \downarrow 0$ along a sequence, by proposition 9.1.4 applied on $B_0$. By (9.2), $(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}H_\varepsilon )^ n=n!\pi ^{-n}\det ((H_{j\bar k})_\varepsilon )\, \mathrm dV_{2n}$, and the determinants converge almost everywhere and are bounded on compact sets uniformly in small $\varepsilon $. Hence, for every test function $\chi $ supported in such a ball $B_0$, dominated convergence gives

\[ \int \chi \, (\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}H)^ n=\frac{n!}{\pi ^ n}\int \chi \det (H_{j\bar k})\, \mathrm dV_{2n}. \]

By lemma 1.4.2, every test function on $U$ is a finite sum of test functions supported in balls $B_0\Subset U$, so this identity holds for all test functions on $U$, and the two Radon measures coincide by proposition 1.4.3(4). $\square$

Theorem 9.6.3. Let $B\subset \mathbb C^ n$ be a ball and $\varphi $ a real continuous function on $\partial B$. There is a unique $H\in \mathrm{PSH}(B)\cap C(\overline B)$ such that

\[ (\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}H)^ n=0\quad \text{on }B,\qquad H|_{\partial B}=\varphi . \]

It is the upper envelope of the class of all $v\in \mathrm{PSH}(B)$ with $\varlimsup _{B\ni z\to x}v(z)\le \varphi (x)$ for every $x\in \partial B$. If $\varphi $ is the restriction of a $C^2$ function on a neighborhood of $\partial B$, then $H\in C^{1,1}_{\mathrm{loc}}(B)$.

Proof. After an affine change of coordinates $z\mapsto z_0+Rz$ with $R>0$, we may assume that $B$ is the unit ball; plurisubharmonicity, the boundary conditions, $C^2$ data and the vanishing of the Monge–Ampère measure are preserved by such a change, by proposition 8.2.3 and proposition 9.1.2(5). We call the functions $v$ in the statement the subsolutions for $\varphi $ of the homogeneous equation. If $v$ is a subsolution for $\varphi $ and $\varphi \le \varphi '+c$ on $\partial B$ with a constant $c$, then $v-c$ is a subsolution for $\varphi '$. Every subsolution is subharmonic on $B$ (theorem 8.2.1), so the comparison principle (theorem 3.1.3 and the remark following it), applied with the harmonic function $P\varphi $, the Poisson integral of $\varphi $ given by theorem 2.3.1, gives $v\le P\varphi $, and $P\varphi \le \max _{\partial B}\varphi $ because the Poisson kernel is positive with integral $1$. Hence, as soon as the class of subsolutions is nonempty, its upper envelope $H$ is defined, and $H\le P\varphi $.

Step 1: the envelope for $C^2$ data. Suppose that $\varphi $ is the restriction of a $C^2$ function on a neighborhood of $\partial B$. Multiplying this function by a cutoff equal to $1$ near $\partial B$, we obtain a $C^2$ function $\tilde\varphi $ with compact support in $\mathbb {C}^ n$ and $\tilde\varphi =\varphi $ on $\partial B$. Its complex Hessian is continuous with compact support, so there is $\lambda >0$ with $\sum _{j,k}\tilde\varphi _{j\bar k}\xi _ j\bar\xi _ k\ge -\lambda |\xi |^2$ everywhere. The function $s=\tilde\varphi +\lambda (|z|^2-1)$ is then of class $C^2$ with positive semidefinite complex Hessian, so it is plurisubharmonic by proposition 8.2.2. As $s=\varphi $ on $\partial B$, it is a subsolution. Hence $s\le H\le P\varphi $. By theorem 8.3.2, the upper semicontinuous regularization $H^*$ is plurisubharmonic, and $s\le H^*\le P\varphi $ since $P\varphi $ is continuous. As $s$ and $P\varphi $ are continuous on $\overline B$ with boundary values $\varphi $, we have $H^*(z)\to \varphi (x)$ as $z\to x\in \partial B$; so $H^*$ is a subsolution, $H^*\le H$, and $H=H^*$ is plurisubharmonic. Moreover $H(z)\to \varphi (x)$ as $z\to x\in \partial B$, and $H$ is bounded.

Step 2: second differences. Let $K\subset B$ be compact, and let $S_{a,h}$, $h_0$ and $C$ be as in lemma 9.6.1. For $x\in \partial B$ and $a\in K$, the function $g(h)=\tilde\varphi (S_{a,h}(x))$, $|h|\le h_0$, is of class $C^2$, and by the chain rule its second derivatives are bounded by a constant $2C_ K$ independent of $a$ and $x$, since the first and second derivatives of $\tilde\varphi $ are bounded and those of $S_{a,h}(x)$ with respect to $h$ are bounded by $C$. Since $g(0)=\varphi (x)$, Taylor's formula with integral remainder gives $g(h)+g(-h)-2g(0)=\int _0^1(1-t)\bigl (D^2g(th)[h,h]+D^2g(-th)[h,h]\bigr )\, \mathrm dt\le 2C_ K|h|^2$. As $S_{a,\pm h}$ maps $\partial B$ to $\partial B$, where $\tilde\varphi =\varphi $, this reads

\[ \tfrac 12\bigl (\varphi \circ S_{a,h}+\varphi \circ S_{a,-h}\bigr ) \le \varphi +C_ K|h|^2\quad \text{on }\partial B\qquad (a\in K,\ |h|\le h_0). \]

The function $w=\frac12(H\circ S_{a,h}+H\circ S_{a,-h})-C_ K|h|^2$ is plurisubharmonic on $B$, by proposition 8.2.3, proposition 8.2.5, since $S_{a,\pm h}$ are automorphisms of $B$ and $H$ is bounded. If $y\in \partial B$ and $z\to y$ in $B$, then $S_{a,\pm h}(z)\to S_{a,\pm h}(y)\in \partial B$, so $H(S_{a,\pm h}(z))\to \varphi (S_{a,\pm h}(y))$ by Step 1, and the preceding inequality gives $\varlimsup _{z\to y}w(z)\le \varphi (y)$. Thus $w$ is a subsolution, so $w\le H$. Evaluating at $z=a$, where $S_{a,\pm h}(a)=a\pm h$, we obtain

\[ H(a+h)+H(a-h)-2H(a)\le 2C_ K|h|^2\qquad (a\in K,\ |h|\le h_0). \]

Since $K$ was arbitrary and the points $a\pm h=S_{a,\pm h}(a)$ lie in $B$, lemma 9.6.2 applies on $U=B$. Thus $H$ is continuous on $B$ and belongs to $C^{1,1}_{\mathrm{loc}}(B)$, and its distributional real Hessian $A$ and complex Hessian $(H_{j\bar k})$ have the properties stated there: the second-order expansion holds almost everywhere, $(H_{j\bar k})$ is positive semidefinite almost everywhere, and $(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}H)^ n=n!\, \pi ^{-n}\det (H_{j\bar k})\, \mathrm dV_{2n}$.

Step 3: the equation. Suppose that $\det (H_{j\bar k})>0$ on a set of positive measure, and choose a point $a$ of this set at which $(H_{j\bar k}(a))$ is positive semidefinite and the second-order expansion holds. The Taylor polynomial $Q(z)=H(a)+\nabla H(a)\cdot (z-a)+\frac12(z-a)^ tA(a)(z-a)$ is a real quadratic polynomial with real Hessian $A(a)$, so its complex Hessian is the constant matrix $(H_{j\bar k}(a))$; this matrix is positive semidefinite with positive determinant, hence positive definite. Let $\eta >0$ be smaller than its smallest eigenvalue; then $Q-\eta |z-a|^2$ has positive semidefinite complex Hessian and is plurisubharmonic (proposition 8.2.2). For sufficiently small $r>0$ we have $\overline{B(a,r)}\subset B$ and, by the expansion, $|H-Q|<\eta r^2/2$ on the sphere $|z-a|=r$. The polynomial $\tilde Q=Q-\eta |z-a|^2+\eta r^2/4$ is then strictly smaller than $H$ on that sphere, since there $\tilde Q<H+\eta r^2/2-3\eta r^2/4$, and $\tilde Q(a)=H(a)+\eta r^2/4>H(a)$. By proposition 8.2.5, applied with $D=B(a,r)$ and $v=\tilde Q$, whose boundary inequality holds because $\tilde Q$ and $H$ are continuous, the function equal to $\max \{ H,\tilde Q\} $ on $B(a,r)$ and to $H$ elsewhere is plurisubharmonic on $B$. It coincides with $H$ near $\partial B$, so it is a subsolution, and it exceeds $H$ at $a$, a contradiction. Hence $\det (H_{j\bar k})=0$ almost everywhere, and $(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}H)^ n=0$. This proves the theorem for $C^2$ data, including the $C^{1,1}$ regularity, except for uniqueness.

Step 4: continuous data and uniqueness. For continuous $\varphi $, the function $z\mapsto \varphi (z/|z|)$ is continuous on the annulus $\{ 1/2<|z|<2\} $, and its mollifications $\varphi _ j$, $j\ge 4$, are smooth on the neighborhood $\{ 3/4<|z|<3/2\} $ of $\partial B$ and converge to $\varphi $ uniformly on $\partial B$ (lemma 1.4.1(1)). Let $H_ j$ be the envelope for $\varphi _ j|_{\partial B}$; by Steps 1–3 it is continuous on $\overline B$ with boundary values $\varphi _ j$, plurisubharmonic, and $(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}H_ j)^ n=0$. Put $c_ j=\max _{\partial B}|\varphi _ j-\varphi |$. Since $H_ j-c_ j$ is a subsolution for $\varphi $, the class of subsolutions for $\varphi $ is nonempty, and its envelope $H$ satisfies $H_ j-c_ j\le H$; symmetrically, $H-c_ j\le H_ j$. Therefore $\sup _ B|H_ j-H|\le c_ j\to 0$, so $H$ is a uniform limit of continuous functions on $B$; it is continuous, extends continuously to $\overline B$ with the boundary values $\varphi $, and is plurisubharmonic, being equal to its regularization $H^*$, which is plurisubharmonic by theorem 8.3.2. By proposition 9.1.4, $(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}H)^ n=\lim _ j(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}H_ j)^ n=0$. Finally, if $H'$ and $H''$ are two solutions, then $H'-H''\to 0$ at $\partial B$, and the last assertion of theorem 9.2.8, applied in both directions, gives $H'=H''$. $\square$

Corollary 9.6.4. Let $u$ be a locally bounded plurisubharmonic function on an open set $\Omega \subset \mathbb C^ n$, and let $B\Subset \Omega $ be a ball. There is a locally bounded plurisubharmonic function $\widehat u$ on $\Omega $ which equals $u$ outside $B$, satisfies $\widehat u\ge u$ on $B$, and $(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\widehat u)^ n=0$ on $B$. The function $\widehat u$ is increasing in $u$, and its restriction to $B$ is the largest plurisubharmonic function $v$ on $B$ with $\varlimsup _{B\ni z\to x}v(z)\le u(x)$ for every $x\in \partial B$.

Proof. Let $u_ j=u*\rho _{\varepsilon _ j}$, with $\varepsilon _ j\downarrow 0$ smaller than the distance from $\overline B$ to $\mathbb C^ n\setminus \Omega $, be radial regularizations of $u$; they are smooth plurisubharmonic functions on a neighborhood of $\overline B$ decreasing to $u$ (proposition 8.2.2). Let $H_ j$ be the solution of theorem 9.6.3 with boundary values $u_ j|_{\partial B}$, that is, the upper envelope of the subsolutions for $u_ j|_{\partial B}$. Since $u_ j$ is such a subsolution, $u\le u_ j\le H_ j$ on $B$. Since $u_{j+1}\le u_ j$ on $\partial B$, every subsolution for $u_{j+1}|_{\partial B}$ is one for $u_ j|_{\partial B}$, so the envelopes decrease: $H_{j+1}\le H_ j$. Let $H=\lim _ jH_ j$. The function $H_1$ is subharmonic on $B$ and continuous on $\overline B$ with boundary values $u_1$, so $H_1\le \max _{\partial B}u_1$ by the comparison principle (theorem 3.1.3). Hence $\inf _{\overline B}u\le H\le \max _{\partial B}u_1$, so $H$ is bounded; it is plurisubharmonic by theorem 8.3.1, and theorem 9.2.5 gives $(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}H)^ n=\lim _ j(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}H_ j)^ n=0$. For $x\in \partial B$, since $H\le H_ j$ and $H_ j$ is continuous on $\overline B$ with $H_ j=u_ j$ on $\partial B$,

\[ \varlimsup _{B\ni z\to x}H(z)\le u_ j(x)\quad \text{for every }j, \qquad \text{hence}\quad \varlimsup _{B\ni z\to x}H(z)\le u(x). \]

By proposition 8.2.5, the function $\widehat u$ equal to $\max \{ u,H\} =H$ on $B$ and to $u$ outside $B$ is plurisubharmonic on $\Omega $. It is locally bounded, since $H$ is bounded and $u$ is locally bounded; it equals $u$ outside $B$, satisfies $\widehat u\ge u$ on $B$, and $(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\widehat u)^ n=(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}H)^ n=0$ on $B$ by locality.

Let $v$ be plurisubharmonic on $B$ with $\varlimsup _{B\ni z\to x}v(z)\le u(x)$ for $x\in \partial B$. Since $u\le u_ j$, the function $v$ is a subsolution for the boundary values $u_ j|_{\partial B}$, so $v\le H_ j$ by theorem 9.6.3. Letting $j\to \infty $ gives $v\le H$. Conversely, $H$ belongs to this class by the displayed estimate, so $H$ is its largest member. If $u\le u'$, the class for $u$ is contained in the class for $u'$, which proves that $\widehat u\le \widehat{u'}$. $\square$

Definition 9.6.5. Let $\Omega \subset \mathbb C^ n$ be open. A plurisubharmonic function $u$ on $\Omega $ is maximal if every plurisubharmonic function $v$ on $\Omega $ with $v\le u$ outside a compact subset of $\Omega $ satisfies $v\le u$ everywhere.

Theorem 9.6.6. A locally bounded plurisubharmonic function $u$ on an open set $\Omega \subset \mathbb C^ n$ is maximal if and only if $(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u)^ n=0$. In particular, maximality is a local property for locally bounded plurisubharmonic functions.

Proof. Suppose that $(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u)^ n=0$, and let $v$ be plurisubharmonic on $\Omega $ with $v\le u$ outside a compact set $K$. Choose a bounded open set $D$ with $K\subset D\Subset \Omega $. By upper semicontinuity, $v$ is bounded above on $\overline D$; since $u$ is bounded on $\overline D$, we may choose $M$ with $-M<\inf _ Du$. The function $\max \{ v,-M\} $ is bounded and plurisubharmonic on $D$, and it is at most $u$ on $D\setminus K$; since every point of $D$ near $\partial D$ lies in $D\setminus K$, we get $\varliminf _{D\ni z\to x}(u(z)-\max \{ v(z),-M\} )\ge 0$ for $x\in \partial D$. The last assertion of theorem 9.2.8 therefore gives $\max \{ v,-M\} \le u$ on $D$. Hence $v\le u$ everywhere, and $u$ is maximal.

Conversely, suppose that $u$ is maximal, and let $B\Subset \Omega $ be a ball. The function $\widehat u$ of corollary 9.6.4 is plurisubharmonic, satisfies $\widehat u\ge u$, and equals $u$ outside the compact set $\overline B$. Maximality gives $\widehat u\le u$, so $\widehat u=u$ and $(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u)^ n=(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\widehat u)^ n=0$ on $B$. Since the balls $B\Subset \Omega $ cover $\Omega $, and a distribution vanishing on each member of a family of open sets vanishes on their union (lemma 1.4.2), $(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u)^ n=0$ on $\Omega $. For the last assertion, let every point of $\Omega $ have an open neighborhood $U$ such that $u|_ U$ is maximal on $U$. By the first part applied on $U$, and by locality of the products (proposition 9.1.2), $(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u)^ n$ vanishes on each such $U$, hence on $\Omega $, and $u$ is maximal on $\Omega $. Conversely, if $u$ is maximal on $\Omega $, then $(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u)^ n=0$ on every open set $U\subset \Omega $, so $u|_ U$ is maximal on $U$. $\square$

9.7 Density lower bounds and mixed products

We now turn to the equation $(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u)^ n=\mu $ with a prescribed measure $\mu $ on a ball, and to the lower bounds that mixed products inherit from lower bounds of the pure Monge–Ampère measures. From here to the end of section 9.11, $\Omega \subset \mathbb C^ n$ is open, $n\ge 1$, and

\[ \eta =\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}|z|^2=\beta /\pi ,\qquad \text{so that}\qquad \eta ^ n=\frac{n!}{\pi ^ n}\, \mathrm dV_{2n} \]

by (9.2). Densities of Monge–Ampère measures are taken with respect to $\eta ^ n$; thus, by (9.2) and the remark following proposition 9.1.4, a plurisubharmonic function $u\in C^2(\Omega )$ satisfies $(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u)^ n=\det (u_{j\bar k})\, \eta ^ n$.

A lower bound $(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u)^ n\ge f\eta ^ n$ is a nonlinear condition on $u$, and it is not evident that it is preserved by convolution. We show that, for a continuous density $f$, it can be tested pointwise with $C^2$ functions touching $u$ from above, and that it is equivalent to a family of linear elliptic inequalities, one for each positive definite Hermitian matrix of determinant one (proposition 9.7.3). Two lemmas prepare it: an expression of $(\det A)^{1/n}$ as an infimum of traces, and a passage from test functions to distributions. Since linear inequalities are preserved by convolution, the determinant inequalities for mixed products of smooth functions then pass to locally bounded potentials (corollary 9.7.4, corollary 9.7.6). The same characterization will make subsolutions of the Dirichlet problem stable under the operations used in section 9.8.

Let $u$ be a real function on an open subset of $\mathbb R^ d$. A real $C^2$ function $q$ defined near a point $x$ touches $u$ from above at $x$ if $q(x)=u(x)$ and $q\ge u$ on a neighborhood of $x$; we then call $q$ an upper test function for $u$ at $x$.

Lemma 9.7.1. Let $A$ be a positive semidefinite Hermitian $n\times n$ matrix. Then

\[ (\det A)^{1/n}=\inf \left\{ \frac{\operatorname {tr}(BA)}n:\ B\text{ positive definite Hermitian},\ \det B=1\right\} . \]

If $C$ is another positive semidefinite Hermitian $n\times n$ matrix, define the numbers $M_ k(A,C)$, $0\le k\le n$, by

\[ \det (tA+sC)=\sum _{k=0}^ n\binom nk M_ k(A,C)\, t^ ks^{n-k}\qquad (t,s\in \mathbb R). \]

Then $M_ n(A,C)=\det A$, $M_0(A,C)=\det C$, and for each integer $1\le k\le n-1$,

\[ M_ k(A,C)\ge (\det A)^{k/n}(\det C)^{(n-k)/n}. \]

Proof. Let $B$ be positive definite with $\det B=1$. The positive semidefinite matrix $B^{1/2}AB^{1/2}$ has trace $\operatorname {tr}(BA)$ and determinant $\det A$, so the arithmetic–geometric mean inequality for its eigenvalues gives $\operatorname {tr}(BA)/n\ge (\det A)^{1/n}$. If $A$ is positive definite, equality holds for $B=(\det A)^{1/n}A^{-1}$. If $A$ is singular, the matrices $B_\varepsilon =\det (A+\varepsilon I)^{1/n}(A+\varepsilon I)^{-1}$, $\varepsilon >0$, are admissible and

\[ 0\le \operatorname {tr}(B_\varepsilon A)\le \operatorname {tr}\bigl (B_\varepsilon (A+\varepsilon I)\bigr )=n\det (A+\varepsilon I)^{1/n}\longrightarrow 0 \]

as $\varepsilon \downarrow 0$.

The formulas for $M_ n$ and $M_0$ follow from $\det (tA)=t^ n\det A$ and $\det (sC)=s^ n\det C$. Suppose first that $A$ and $C$ are positive definite, and let $\lambda _1,\ldots ,\lambda _ n>0$ be the eigenvalues of $A^{-1/2}CA^{-1/2}$. Since $\det (tA+sC)=\det A\prod _{i=1}^ n(t+s\lambda _ i)$, comparison of coefficients gives

\[ M_ k(A,C)=\frac{\det A}{\binom nk}\sum _{|J|=n-k}\prod _{j\in J}\lambda _ j, \]

the sum being over the subsets $J\subset \{ 1,\ldots ,n\} $ with $n-k$ elements. Each eigenvalue occurs in exactly $\binom {n-1}{n-k-1}$ of these $\binom n{n-k}$ products, so the geometric mean of the products is $(\lambda _1\cdots \lambda _ n)^{(n-k)/n}$. Since $\lambda _1\cdots \lambda _ n=\det C/\det A$, the arithmetic–geometric mean inequality gives

\[ M_ k(A,C)\ge \det A\left(\frac{\det C}{\det A}\right)^{(n-k)/n}=(\det A)^{k/n}(\det C)^{(n-k)/n}. \]

For semidefinite $A$ and $C$, apply this to $A+\varepsilon I$ and $C+\varepsilon I$ and let $\varepsilon \downarrow 0$; both sides depend continuously on the matrices. $\square$

Lemma 9.7.2. Let $d\ge 1$, let $(c_{kl})$ be a real symmetric positive definite $d\times d$ matrix, and put $L=\sum _{k,l=1}^ dc_{kl}\partial _ k\partial _ l$. Let $U\subset \mathbb R^ d$ be open, let $w\colon U\to \mathbb R$ be upper semicontinuous, and let $F\colon U\to \mathbb R$ be continuous. Suppose that, for every $x\in U$, every upper test function $q$ for $w$ at $x$ satisfies $Lq(x)\ge F(x)$. Then $w$ is locally integrable, and $Lw\ge F$ in the sense of distributions on $U$.

Proof. We reduce to the Laplacian, treat the case $F=0$ by a maximum principle argument, and reduce the general case to it.

Step 1. Reduction to $L=\Delta $. Let $C=(c_{kl})$, let $P$ be the positive definite square root of $C^{-1}$, so that $PCP=I$, and put $\tilde U=P(U)$, $\tilde w=w\circ P^{-1}$ and $\tilde F=F\circ P^{-1}$. If $q$ is a $C^2$ function near $x\in U$ and $\tilde q=q\circ P^{-1}$, then $\partial _ k\partial _ lq(x)=\sum _{a,b}P_{ak}P_{bl}(\partial _ a\partial _ b\tilde q)(Px)$ by the chain rule, and hence

\[ (Lq)(x)=\sum _{a,b}(PCP)_{ab}(\partial _ a\partial _ b\tilde q)(Px)=(\Delta \tilde q)(Px). \]

The map $q\mapsto \tilde q$ is a bijection from the upper test functions for $w$ at $x$ onto those for $\tilde w$ at $Px$, so $\tilde w$, $\Delta $ and $\tilde F$ satisfy the hypothesis of the lemma on $\tilde U$. Suppose that the lemma holds for them. Then $w=\tilde w\circ P$ is locally integrable, and for every nonnegative test function $\chi $ on $U$ the change of variables $y=Px$, with $\tilde\chi =\chi \circ P^{-1}$ and $L\chi =(\Delta \tilde\chi )\circ P$, gives

\[ \langle Lw,\chi \rangle =\int _ Uw\, L\chi \, \mathrm dx =\frac1{|\det P|}\int _{\tilde U}\tilde w\, \Delta \tilde\chi \, \mathrm dy \ge \frac1{|\det P|}\int _{\tilde U}\tilde F\tilde\chi \, \mathrm dy=\int _ UF\chi \, \mathrm dx; \]

the first equality is the definition of distributional derivatives. We may therefore assume that $L=\Delta $.

Step 2. The case $F=0$. We show that $w$ satisfies assertion (1) of theorem 3.1.3 for every ball $D=B(y,r)$ with $\overline{B(y,r)}\subset U$; by the converse part of that theorem, $w$ is then subharmonic on $U$. Let $h$ be a real function which is harmonic on $B(y,r)$ and continuous on $\overline{B(y,r)}$, with $\varlimsup _{B(y,r)\ni x\to \zeta }w(x)\le h(\zeta )$ for every $\zeta \in \partial B(y,r)$, and suppose that $w(x_1)>h(x_1)$ for some $x_1\in B(y,r)$. Choose $\varepsilon >0$ so small that the upper semicontinuous function $g(x)=w(x)-h(x)-\varepsilon (r^2-|x-y|^2)$ on $B(y,r)$ satisfies $g(x_1)>0$.

Since $h$ is continuous on the closed ball, $\varlimsup _{B(y,r)\ni x\to \zeta }g(x)\le 0$ for every $\zeta \in \partial B(y,r)$. Hence every $\zeta \in \partial B(y,r)$ has an open neighborhood $N_\zeta $ with $g<g(x_1)/2$ on $N_\zeta \cap B(y,r)$; finitely many of these neighborhoods cover the sphere, and the complement $K$ of their union in $\overline{B(y,r)}$ is a compact subset of $B(y,r)$ containing $x_1$. The upper semicontinuous function $g$ attains its maximum over $K$ at a point $x_0$, and $c=g(x_0)\ge g(x_1)>0$; since $g<g(x_1)/2$ outside $K$, we have $g\le c$ on $B(y,r)$. The function $q=h+\varepsilon (r^2-|x-y|^2)+c$ is smooth on $B(y,r)$, satisfies $q-w=c-g\ge 0$ there and $q(x_0)=w(x_0)$, so it is an upper test function for $w$ at $x_0$. But $\Delta q=-2d\varepsilon <0$, which contradicts the hypothesis. Hence $w\le h$ on $B(y,r)$, and $w$ is subharmonic on $U$. Being real-valued, it is not identically $-\infty $ on any connected component of $U$, so theorem 3.2.2(1) shows that $w$ is locally integrable and $\Delta w\ge 0$.

Step 3. The general case. Let $V\Subset U$ be open. Every compact subset of $U$ lies in such a set $V$, so it suffices to show that $w$ is locally integrable on $V$ and that $\Delta w\ge F$ on $V$. Choose an open set $V'$ with $\overline V\subset V'\Subset U$. By lemma 1.4.1(1), the mollifications of $F$ converge to $F$ uniformly on $\overline{V'}$; subtracting from each of them its maximal deviation from $F$ on $\overline{V'}$, we obtain smooth functions $F_ j$ on a neighborhood of $\overline{V'}$ such that $F_ j\le F$ on $\overline{V'}$ and $F_ j\to F$ uniformly there. Let $\vartheta \in C^\infty _ c(V')$ be equal to one on an open set $W\supset \overline V$ (lemma 1.4.1(4)). By proposition 2.2.1, the convolution $h_ j=E_ d*(\vartheta F_ j)$, where $\vartheta F_ j$ is extended by zero, is a smooth function on $\mathbb R^ d$ with $\Delta h_ j=\vartheta F_ j$; in particular $\Delta h_ j=F_ j$ on $W$.

If $q$ is an upper test function for $w-h_ j$ at $x\in V$, then $q+h_ j$ is an upper test function for $w$ at $x$, so that $\Delta q(x)+F_ j(x)\ge F(x)$, that is, $\Delta q(x)\ge F(x)-F_ j(x)\ge 0$. Step 2, applied to the real upper semicontinuous function $w-h_ j$ on $V$, shows that $w-h_ j$ is locally integrable on $V$ and $\Delta (w-h_ j)\ge 0$ there. Hence $w$ is locally integrable on $V$ and $\Delta w\ge F_ j$ on $V$. Testing against a nonnegative test function on $V$ and letting $j\to \infty $, we obtain $\Delta w\ge F$ on $V$. $\square$

For a real $C^2$ function $q$ on an open subset of $\mathbb C^ n$, we write $\mathcal H(q)=(q_{j\bar k})$ for its complex Hessian matrix, normalized as in (7.5). For a positive definite Hermitian matrix $B$ with $\det B=1$, put

\[ L_ Bq=\operatorname {tr}\bigl (B\mathcal H(q)\bigr ). \]

This is a real operator with constant coefficients, since $B$ and $\mathcal H(q)$ are Hermitian, and it is elliptic. Indeed, write $B=V\operatorname {diag}(b_1,\ldots ,b_ n)V^*$ with $V$ unitary and $b_1,\ldots ,b_ n>0$, and introduce coordinates $\zeta $ by $z=\bar V\zeta $. By the chain rule, the complex Hessian of $\tilde q(\zeta )=q(\bar V\zeta )$ is $V^*\mathcal H(q)V$, so that

\[ L_ Bq=\operatorname {tr}\bigl (\operatorname {diag}(b_1,\ldots ,b_ n)\, V^*\mathcal H(q)V\bigr )=\sum _{j=1}^ nb_ j\tilde q_{j\bar j}=\frac14\sum _{j=1}^ nb_ j\Delta _ j\tilde q \]

by (7.5), where $\Delta _ j$ is the Laplacian in the variable $\zeta _ j$. The real-linear map $z\mapsto \zeta $ is orthogonal on $\mathbb C^ n=\mathbb R^{2n}$; let $O$ be its matrix. By the chain rule, in the real coordinates of $\mathbb C^ n$ the operator $L_ B$ takes the form $\sum _{k,l}c_{kl}\partial _ k\partial _ l$ with $(c_{kl})=O^ t\Lambda O$, where $\Lambda $ is the diagonal matrix with entries $b_1/4,b_1/4,\ldots ,b_ n/4,b_ n/4$. This matrix is symmetric and positive definite, so lemma 9.7.2 applies to $L_ B$.

Proposition 9.7.3. Let $u$ be a locally bounded plurisubharmonic function on $\Omega $, and let $f\ge 0$ be a continuous function on $\Omega $. The following conditions are equivalent:

  1. $(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u)^ n\ge f\eta ^ n$ as measures on $\Omega $;

  2. $\det \mathcal H(q)(x)\ge f(x)$ for every $x\in \Omega $ and every upper test function $q$ for $u$ at $x$;

  3. $L_ Bu\ge nf^{1/n}$ in the sense of distributions on $\Omega $, for every positive definite Hermitian matrix $B$ with $\det B=1$.

If these conditions hold, then for every $\varepsilon >0$ the mollification $u_\varepsilon =u*\rho _\varepsilon $ satisfies $\det \mathcal H(u_\varepsilon )\ge (f^{1/n}*\rho _\varepsilon )^ n$ on $\Omega _\varepsilon $.

Proof. We first observe that the complex Hessian $\mathcal H(q)(x)$ of an upper test function $q$ for $u$ at $x$ is positive semidefinite. Indeed, let $\xi \in \mathbb C^ n\setminus \{ 0\} $, and let $r>0$ be so small that the closed disc $\{ x+\zeta \xi :|\zeta |\le r\} $ lies in the neighborhood of $x$ on which $q\ge u$. The function $\zeta \mapsto u(x+\zeta \xi )$ is subharmonic near this disc and finite at $\zeta =0$, so theorem 3.1.3(2) gives

\[ q(x)=u(x)\le \frac1{2\pi }\int _0^{2\pi }u(x+r\mathrm e^{\mathrm i\theta }\xi )\, \mathrm d\theta \le \frac1{2\pi }\int _0^{2\pi }q(x+r\mathrm e^{\mathrm i\theta }\xi )\, \mathrm d\theta . \]

Write $q_ j=\partial _{z_ j}q$ and $q_{jk}=\partial _{z_ j}\partial _{z_ k}q$. Since $q$ is real and of class $C^2$, Taylor's formula gives

\[ q(x+h)=q(x)+2\operatorname{Re}\sum _ jq_ j(x)h_ j+\operatorname{Re}\sum _{j,k}q_{jk}(x)h_ jh_ k+\sum _{j,k}q_{j\bar k}(x)h_ j\bar h_ k+o(|h|^2). \]
