For $h=r\mathrm e^{\mathrm i\theta }\xi $ the terms containing $\mathrm e^{\mathrm i\theta }$ and $\mathrm e^{2\mathrm i\theta }$ have mean zero over $\theta \in [0,2\pi ]$, so the right-hand side of the preceding display equals $q(x)+r^2\sum _{j,k}q_{j\bar k}(x)\xi _ j\bar\xi _ k+o(r^2)$. Dividing by $r^2$ and letting $r\downarrow 0$, we obtain $\sum _{j,k}q_{j\bar k}(x)\xi _ j\bar\xi _ k\ge 0$.

(1) implies (2). Assume (1), let $q$ be an upper test function for $u$ at $x$, and suppose that $\det \mathcal H(q)(x)<f(x)$. Since $\mathcal H(q)(x)$ is positive semidefinite, we can choose $\delta >0$ so small that $q_\delta =q+\delta |z-x|^2$ satisfies $\mathcal H(q_\delta )(x)=\mathcal H(q)(x)+\delta I>0$ and still $\det \mathcal H(q_\delta )(x)<f(x)$. Since $q$ is of class $C^2$ and $f$ is continuous, there is $r>0$ such that $\overline{B(x,r)}\subset \Omega $, $q\ge u$ on $\overline{B(x,r)}$, the complex Hessian $\mathcal H(q_\delta )$ is positive definite on $\overline{B(x,r)}$, and $\det \mathcal H(q_\delta )<f$ on $\overline{B(x,r)}$; in particular, $q_\delta $ is plurisubharmonic on $B(x,r)$ by proposition 8.2.2. Put $b=q_\delta -\delta r^2/2$. Then $b=q+\delta r^2/2>u$ on the sphere $\partial B(x,r)$, while $b(x)=u(x)-\delta r^2/2<u(x)$.

The Borel set $S=\{ z\in B(x,r):b(z)<u(z)\} $ has positive Lebesgue measure. Otherwise $\max \{ u,b\} =b$ almost everywhere on $B(x,r)$; since both functions are plurisubharmonic, the uniqueness assertion of theorem 8.2.1 gives $\max \{ u,b\} =b$ everywhere, contradicting $b(x)<u(x)$. The function $b-u$ is lower semicontinuous and positive on $\partial B(x,r)$, so $\varliminf _{z\to \zeta }(b(z)-u(z))>0$ for every $\zeta \in \partial B(x,r)$. The comparison principle theorem 9.2.8, applied on $B(x,r)$ with the bounded plurisubharmonic functions $b$ and $u$ in place of $u$ and $v$, therefore gives

\[ \int _ S f\eta ^ n\le \int _ S(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u)^ n\le \int _ S(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}b)^ n=\int _ S\det \mathcal H(q_\delta )\, \eta ^ n. \]

Here $(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}b)^ n=\det \mathcal H(q_\delta )\, \eta ^ n$ on $B(x,r)$ because $b$ is a plurisubharmonic $C^2$ function there (see the beginning of this section), and the integrals are finite because $(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u)^ n$ is a Radon measure on $\Omega $ and $\overline{B(x,r)}$ is a compact subset of $\Omega $. Since $\det \mathcal H(q_\delta )<f$ on $S$ and $S$ has positive measure, the right side is strictly smaller than the left side, a contradiction. Thus (2) holds.

(2) implies (3). Let $q$ be an upper test function for $u$ at $x$, and let $B$ be positive definite with $\det B=1$. Since $\mathcal H(q)(x)$ is positive semidefinite, the infimum formula of lemma 9.7.1 and (2) give

\[ L_ Bq(x)=\operatorname {tr}\bigl (B\mathcal H(q)(x)\bigr )\ge n\bigl (\det \mathcal H(q)(x)\bigr )^{1/n}\ge nf(x)^{1/n}. \]

Since $u$ is real-valued and upper semicontinuous, lemma 9.7.2, applied to $L_ B$ and the continuous function $nf^{1/n}$, gives (3).

(3) implies (1). The mollifications $u_\varepsilon =u*\rho _\varepsilon $ of section 1.4 are smooth plurisubharmonic functions on $\Omega _\varepsilon $ which decrease to $u$ as $\varepsilon \downarrow 0$ (proposition 8.2.2). Fix $B$ as in (3). The distribution $L_ Bu-nf^{1/n}$ is positive, so its mollification is a nonnegative function on $\Omega _\varepsilon $ (proposition 1.4.3(3)). Since $L_ B$ has constant coefficients, proposition 1.4.3(1) identifies the mollification of $L_ Bu$ with $L_ Bu_\varepsilon $, and we obtain the pointwise inequality

\[ \operatorname {tr}\bigl (B\mathcal H(u_\varepsilon )\bigr )\ge n\, (f^{1/n}*\rho _\varepsilon )\quad \text{on }\Omega _\varepsilon \qquad (B>0,\ \det B=1). \]

Since $\mathcal H(u_\varepsilon )$ is positive semidefinite (proposition 8.2.2), taking the infimum over $B$ and using lemma 9.7.1, we obtain $\det \mathcal H(u_\varepsilon )\ge (f^{1/n}*\rho _\varepsilon )^ n$, which is the last assertion of the proposition. By the formula recalled at the beginning of this section, it reads

\[ (\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_\varepsilon )^ n\ge (f^{1/n}*\rho _\varepsilon )^ n\eta ^ n\quad \text{on }\Omega _\varepsilon . \]

As $\varepsilon \downarrow 0$, the density on the right converges locally uniformly to $f$ by lemma 1.4.1(1), and $(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_\varepsilon )^ n\to (\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u)^ n$ weakly by theorem 9.2.5, applied along any sequence $\varepsilon _ j\downarrow 0$. Testing against nonnegative functions in $C_ c(\Omega )$ gives (1). $\square$

Corollary 9.7.4. Let $u,v$ be locally bounded plurisubharmonic functions on $\Omega $, and let $f,g\ge 0$ be continuous functions on $\Omega $ such that $(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u)^ n\ge f\eta ^ n$ and $(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}v)^ n\ge g\eta ^ n$. Then, for each integer $1\le k\le n-1$,

\[ (\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u)^ k\wedge (\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}v)^{n-k}\ge f^{k/n}g^{(n-k)/n}\eta ^ n. \]

For $k=0$ and $k=n$ the same inequality is the hypothesis.

Proof. Let $u_\varepsilon =u*\rho _\varepsilon $ and $v_\varepsilon =v*\rho _\varepsilon $, with the same mollifiers for both functions. By the last assertion of proposition 9.7.3, applied to $u$ with the density $f$ and to $v$ with the density $g$,

\[ \det \mathcal H(u_\varepsilon )\ge (f^{1/n}*\rho _\varepsilon )^ n, \qquad \det \mathcal H(v_\varepsilon )\ge (g^{1/n}*\rho _\varepsilon )^ n. \]

For real $t$ and $s$, (9.2), applied to the smooth function $tu_\varepsilon +sv_\varepsilon $, gives $(t\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_\varepsilon +s\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}v_\varepsilon )^ n=\det \bigl (t\mathcal H(u_\varepsilon )+s\mathcal H(v_\varepsilon )\bigr )\eta ^ n$ for the exterior powers of smooth forms. Since $2$-forms commute, the left side can be expanded by the binomial formula. Comparing the coefficients of $\binom nkt^ ks^{n-k}$, and recalling that Bedford–Taylor products of smooth functions are exterior products of smooth forms (proposition 9.1.2(1)), we find

\[ (\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_\varepsilon )^ k\wedge (\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}v_\varepsilon )^{n-k}=M_ k\bigl (\mathcal H(u_\varepsilon ),\mathcal H(v_\varepsilon )\bigr )\eta ^ n\ge (f^{1/n}*\rho _\varepsilon )^ k(g^{1/n}*\rho _\varepsilon )^{n-k}\eta ^ n, \]

where the inequality is the second assertion of lemma 9.7.1, applied to the matrices $\mathcal H(u_\varepsilon )$ and $\mathcal H(v_\varepsilon )$, which are positive semidefinite by proposition 8.2.2. As $\varepsilon \downarrow 0$, the density on the right converges locally uniformly to $f^{k/n}g^{(n-k)/n}$, while the left side converges weakly to $(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u)^ k\wedge (\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}v)^{n-k}$ by theorem 9.2.5, since $u_\varepsilon $ and $v_\varepsilon $ are smooth plurisubharmonic functions decreasing to $u$ and $v$ (proposition 8.2.2). $\square$

9.7.1 Several independent factors

Corollary 9.7.4 concerns products in which only two Hessians occur, each repeated several times. For a product $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_1\wedge \cdots \wedge \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_ n$ of $n$ arbitrary potentials, the coefficients $M_ k$ of lemma 9.7.1 are replaced by the mixed discriminant $D(A_1,\ldots ,A_ n)$ of Hermitian matrices. Recall from the paragraph before lemma 7.2.2 that $D(A_1,\ldots ,A_ n)$ is $1/n!$ times the coefficient of $t_1\cdots t_ n$ in $\det (\sum _ jt_ jA_ j)$; it is symmetric and real multilinear, and $D(A,\ldots ,A)=\det A$ (lemma 7.2.2(1)). We prove the geometric-mean inequality for $D$ by means of a reverse Cauchy–Schwarz inequality, which follows from the signature of $D$ computed in lemma 7.2.3. Convolution then extends it to locally bounded potentials as before.

Lemma 9.7.5. Let $n\geq 1$. For positive semidefinite Hermitian $n\times n$ matrices $A_1,\ldots ,A_ n$,

\[ D(A_1,\ldots ,A_ n)\geq \prod _{i=1}^ n(\det A_ i)^{1/n}. \]

Proof. For $n=1$ both sides equal $A_1$. Let $n\geq 2$, and assume first that all $A_ i$ are positive definite. By lemma 7.2.2(3), $D$ is positive on positive definite matrices.

We first derive the reverse Cauchy–Schwarz inequality. Fix positive definite matrices $A_3,\ldots ,A_ n$, and consider the symmetric bilinear form

\[ q(A,B)=D(A,B,A_3,\ldots ,A_ n) \]

on Hermitian matrices. Let $A,B$ be positive definite, put $c=q(A,B)/q(B,B)$ and $M=A-cB$. Then $q(M,B)=0$, so $q(M,M)\leq 0$ by lemma 7.2.3. Since $q(M,M)=q(A,A)-q(A,B)^2/q(B,B)$, we obtain

9.32
\begin{equation} q(A,B)^2\geq q(A,A)q(B,B). \end{equation}

Put $\widetilde A_ i=(\det A_ i)^{-1/n}A_ i$, so that $\det \widetilde A_ i=1$. By multilinearity it suffices to prove that $D(\widetilde A_1,\ldots ,\widetilde A_ n)\geq 1$. Consider the finitely many ordered $n$-tuples whose entries belong to the set $\{ \widetilde A_1,\ldots ,\widetilde A_ n\} $, and let $F>0$ be the smallest mixed discriminant of such a tuple. A tuple whose entries all equal $\widetilde A_ i$ has discriminant $\det \widetilde A_ i=1$, so $F\leq 1$. The multiplicity of a matrix in a tuple is the number of entries equal to it. Among the tuples with discriminant $F$, choose one for which the sum of the squares of the multiplicities is largest.

Suppose that this tuple contains two different matrices $A$ and $B$. By symmetry of $D$ we may place them in the first two slots, so that $F=q(A,B)$, where $q$ is formed with the remaining entries of the tuple. Replacing the pair $(A,B)$ by $(A,A)$ or by $(B,B)$ gives tuples with discriminants $q(A,A)\geq F$ and $q(B,B)\geq F$, whereas (9.32) gives $q(A,A)q(B,B)\leq F^2$. Hence $q(A,A)=q(B,B)=F$, and both new tuples are minimizing. If $A$ and $B$ have multiplicities $r$ and $s$, the two replacements change the sum of the squared multiplicities by $2(r-s)+2$ and $2(s-r)+2$, respectively. These two numbers add up to $4$, so one of them is positive, which contradicts the choice of the tuple. Therefore all entries of the chosen tuple are equal, and $F=1$. In particular $D(\widetilde A_1,\ldots ,\widetilde A_ n)\geq 1$.

Finally, for positive semidefinite $A_ i$, apply the inequality to $A_ i+\varepsilon I$ and let $\varepsilon \downarrow 0$. Both sides are continuous: $D$ is a polynomial in the matrix entries, and $(\det A)^{1/n}$ is continuous on positive semidefinite matrices. $\square$

Corollary 9.7.6. Let $u_1,\ldots ,u_ n$ be locally bounded plurisubharmonic functions on an open set $\Omega \subset \mathbb C^ n$, $n\geq 1$, and let $f_1,\ldots ,f_ n\geq 0$ be continuous functions on $\Omega $ such that

\[ (\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_ i)^ n\geq f_ i\eta ^ n\quad (1\leq i\leq n), \qquad \eta =\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}|z|^2. \]

Then

\[ \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_1\wedge \cdots \wedge \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_ n \geq \left(\prod _{i=1}^ n f_ i^{1/n}\right)\eta ^ n. \]

Proof. For $n=1$ this is the hypothesis, so let $n\geq 2$. Put $u_{i,\varepsilon }=u_ i*\rho _\varepsilon $, with the same radial mollifiers for all $i$. By the last assertion of proposition 9.7.3, applied to each $u_ i$ with the density $f_ i$,

\[ \det \mathcal H(u_{i,\varepsilon })\geq (f_ i^{1/n}*\rho _\varepsilon )^ n\qquad (1\leq i\leq n). \]

By (9.2), applied to the smooth function $\sum _ it_ iu_{i,\varepsilon }$, the exterior powers of smooth forms satisfy

\[ \Bigl (\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\sum _{i=1}^ nt_ iu_{i,\varepsilon }\Bigr )^ n=\det \Bigl (\sum _{i=1}^ nt_ i\mathcal H(u_{i,\varepsilon })\Bigr )\eta ^ n\qquad (t_1,\ldots ,t_ n\in \mathbb R). \]

Both sides are polynomials in $t_1,\ldots ,t_ n$. Since $2$-forms commute, the coefficient of $t_1\cdots t_ n$ on the left is $n!\, \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_{1,\varepsilon }\wedge \cdots \wedge \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_{n,\varepsilon }$, and on the right it is $n!\, D(\mathcal H(u_{1,\varepsilon }),\ldots ,\mathcal H(u_{n,\varepsilon }))\eta ^ n$. Since the Bedford–Taylor product of smooth functions is the exterior product of the forms $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_{i,\varepsilon }$ (proposition 9.1.2(1)), we obtain

\[ \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_{1,\varepsilon }\wedge \cdots \wedge \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_{n,\varepsilon } =D\bigl (\mathcal H(u_{1,\varepsilon }),\ldots ,\mathcal H(u_{n,\varepsilon })\bigr )\eta ^ n \geq \prod _{i=1}^ n(f_ i^{1/n}*\rho _\varepsilon )\, \eta ^ n, \]

where the inequality is lemma 9.7.5, applied to the complex Hessians of the smooth plurisubharmonic functions $u_{i,\varepsilon }$, which are positive semidefinite (proposition 8.2.2). As $\varepsilon \downarrow 0$, the density on the right converges locally uniformly to $\prod _ if_ i^{1/n}$, and the left side converges weakly to $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_1\wedge \cdots \wedge \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_ n$ by theorem 9.2.5, since $u_{i,\varepsilon }\downarrow u_ i$ for each $i$ (proposition 8.2.2). $\square$

9.8 The ball Dirichlet problem with continuous density

We now solve the Dirichlet problem

\[ (\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u)^ n=f\eta ^ n\quad \text{on }B,\qquad u=\varphi \quad \text{on }\partial B, \]

on a ball $B$, for a continuous density $f\ge 0$ and continuous boundary values $\varphi $. As for the homogeneous equation in theorem 9.6.3, the solution is the upper envelope of subsolutions. By the linear characterization of density lower bounds in proposition 9.7.3, subsolutions are stable under maxima, convex combinations and the addition of multiples of $|z|^2$ (lemma 9.8.1). Stability under maxima makes the envelope a subsolution, and stability under averages allows us to compose with the automorphisms of lemma 9.6.1 once more to bound its second differences; since a biholomorphism multiplies the density by the squared modulus of its Jacobian determinant, this factor now enters the estimate.

Lemma 9.8.1. Let $u,v$ be locally bounded plurisubharmonic functions on $\Omega $, and let $F,G\ge 0$ be continuous functions on $\Omega $ with $(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u)^ n\ge F^ n\eta ^ n$ and $(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}v)^ n\ge G^ n\eta ^ n$. Then, for $0\le t\le 1$ and $c\ge 0$,

\[ \bigl (\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}((1-t)u+tv+c|z|^2)\bigr )^ n \ge \bigl ((1-t)F+tG+c\bigr )^ n\eta ^ n. \]

Moreover, $(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\max \{ u,v\} )^ n\ge \min \{ F,G\} ^ n\eta ^ n$.

Proof. Let $B$ be a positive definite Hermitian matrix with $\det B=1$. By the implication from (1) to (3) in proposition 9.7.3, $L_ Bu\ge nF$ and $L_ Bv\ge nG$ in the sense of distributions. Moreover $L_ B|z|^2=\operatorname {tr}B\ge n(\det B)^{1/n}=n$ by the arithmetic–geometric mean inequality for the eigenvalues of $B$. Hence

\[ L_ B\bigl ((1-t)u+tv+c|z|^2\bigr ) \ge n\bigl ((1-t)F+tG\bigr )+c\operatorname {tr}B \ge n\bigl ((1-t)F+tG+c\bigr ), \]

and the implication from (3) to (1), applied with the continuous density $((1-t)F+tG+c)^ n$, gives the first assertion.

For the second, let $q$ be an upper test function for $\max \{ u,v\} $ at a point $x$. If $u(x)\ge v(x)$, then $q(x)=u(x)$ and $q\ge \max \{ u,v\} \ge u$ near $x$, so $q$ is an upper test function for $u$ at $x$, and the implication from (1) to (2) gives $\det \mathcal H(q)(x)\ge F(x)^ n\ge \min \{ F,G\} (x)^ n$. The case $v(x)\ge u(x)$ is symmetric. The implication from (2) to (1) now gives the assertion. $\square$

Theorem 9.8.2. Let $B=B(a,R)\subset \mathbb C^ n$ with $n\ge 1$ and $R>0$. Let $f\in C(\overline B)$ be nonnegative, and let $\varphi \in C(\partial B)$ be real. There is a unique $H_{f,\varphi }\in \mathrm{PSH}(B)\cap C(\overline B)$ such that

\[ (\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}H_{f,\varphi })^ n=f\eta ^ n\quad \text{on }B, \qquad H_{f,\varphi }|_{\partial B}=\varphi . \]

It is the pointwise supremum of the bounded plurisubharmonic functions $v$ on $B$ such that $(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}v)^ n\ge f\eta ^ n$ and $\varlimsup _{B\ni z\to \zeta }v(z)\le \varphi (\zeta )$ for every $\zeta \in \partial B$. If $g\in C(\overline B)$ is another nonnegative density and $\chi \in C(\partial B)$ another real boundary function, then

9.33
\begin{equation} \| H_{f,\varphi }-H_{g,\chi }\| _{L^\infty (B)} \le \| \varphi -\chi \| _{L^\infty (\partial B)} +R^2\| f^{1/n}-g^{1/n}\| _{L^\infty (B)}. \end{equation}

If $f^{1/n}$ is the restriction of a real $C^2$ function on a neighborhood of $\overline B$ and $\varphi $ that of a real $C^2$ function on a neighborhood of $\partial B$, then $H_{f,\varphi }\in C^{1,1}_{\mathrm{loc}}(B)$.

Proof. For data of class $C^2$ (defined below) we show that the envelope of subsolutions is a locally $C^{1,1}$ solution (Steps 1–3). A stability estimate then gives a solution for continuous data (Step 4), and a comparison argument identifies every continuous solution with the envelope (Step 5).

The affine map $\tau (\xi )=a+R\xi $ carries the unit ball onto $B$. Since $|\tau (\xi )|^2-R^2|\xi |^2=2R\operatorname{Re}\sum _ j\xi _ j\bar a_ j+|a|^2$ is the real part of a holomorphic polynomial plus a constant, its complex Hessian vanishes, so $\tau $ pulls $\eta =\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}|z|^2$ back to $R^2\eta $ and $\eta ^ n$ back to $R^{2n}\eta ^ n$. Since Bedford–Taylor products are invariant under biholomorphisms (proposition 9.1.2(5)), a function $u$ on $B$ satisfies $(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u)^ n\ge f\eta ^ n$ if and only if $\xi \mapsto u(a+R\xi )$ satisfies the same inequality with the density $R^{2n}f(a+R\xi )$, whose $n$th root is $R^2f^{1/n}(a+R\xi )$; the same holds with equality. It therefore suffices to prove the theorem for the unit ball $B=B(0,1)$, with $R=1$ in (9.33). We write $z$ again for the variable and put

\[ \psi =|z|^2-1,\qquad F=f^{1/n}. \]

In this proof a subsolution for $(f,\varphi )$ is a bounded plurisubharmonic function $v$ on $B$ with $(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}v)^ n\ge f\eta ^ n$ and $\varlimsup _{B\ni z\to \zeta }v(z)\le \varphi (\zeta )$ for all $\zeta \in \partial B$. We say that the data $(f,\varphi )$ are of class $C^2$ if $F$ and $\varphi $ are restrictions of real $C^2$ functions on neighborhoods of $\overline B$ and $\partial B$, respectively.

Step 1. The envelope of subsolutions for data of class $C^2$. Assume that $(f,\varphi )$ is of class $C^2$. We first construct a subsolution with boundary values $\varphi $. Multiplying a $C^2$ extension of $\varphi $ by a smooth cutoff function for $\partial B$ in its domain which equals one near $\partial B$ (lemma 1.4.1(4)), we obtain a real $C^2$ function $b$ on $\mathbb C^ n$ with compact support and $b=\varphi $ on $\partial B$. Let $\lambda \ge 0$ bound the operator norm of the complex Hessian of $b$, and choose $c_0>\lambda $ with $(c_0-\lambda )^ n\ge \max _{\overline B}f$. The complex Hessian of $s=b+c_0\psi $ is at least $(c_0-\lambda )I$, so $s$ is plurisubharmonic (proposition 8.2.2) and $\det (s_{j\bar k})\ge (c_0-\lambda )^ n\ge f$ on $B$; by the formula for plurisubharmonic $C^2$ functions at the beginning of section 9.7, $(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}s)^ n\ge f\eta ^ n$ on $B$. Moreover $s$ is continuous on $\overline B$ and equals $\varphi $ on $\partial B$; in particular, $s$ is a subsolution. On the other hand, every subsolution is subharmonic (theorem 8.2.1) with boundary upper limits at most $\varphi $, so the comparison principle (theorem 3.1.3 and the remark following it) bounds it above by the harmonic extension $P\varphi $ of $\varphi $, its Poisson integral (theorem 2.3.1).

Let $H$ be the pointwise supremum of all subsolutions, so that $s\le H\le P\varphi $. By lemma 9.8.1, the maximum of finitely many subsolutions is a subsolution. By theorem 8.3.2, some countable family of subsolutions has the same upper semicontinuous regularization $H^*$ as the whole family, and its supremum equals $H^*$ almost everywhere. Taking finite maxima of $s$ and the members of this family, we obtain subsolutions $v_ j$ which increase to a function equal to $H^*$ almost everywhere, and $s\le v_ j\le P\varphi $. By theorem 9.5.1, $(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}v_ j)^ n\to (\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}H^*)^ n$ weakly, and hence $(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}H^*)^ n\ge f\eta ^ n$. Since $P\varphi $ is continuous, $s\le H\le H^*\le P\varphi $; as $s$ and $P\varphi $ are continuous on $\overline B$ and equal $\varphi $ on $\partial B$, the function $H^*$ has boundary limits $\varphi $. Thus $H^*$ is a subsolution, so $H^*\le H$ and $H=H^*$. In particular, $H$ is a subsolution with $\lim _{B\ni z\to \zeta }H(z)=\varphi (\zeta )$ for all $\zeta \in \partial B$.

Step 2. Second differences for data of class $C^2$. We show that $H$ satisfies the hypothesis of lemma 9.6.2 on $B$. Fix a compact set $K\subset B$, and let $h_0>0$ and the automorphisms $S_{a,h}$, $a\in K$, $|h|\le h_0$, be as in lemma 9.6.1. Thus $(a,h,z)\mapsto S_{a,h}(z)$ is smooth, and holomorphic in $z$, on an open set $O$ containing the compact set $L=K\times \overline{B(0,h_0)}\times \overline B$, and so is the complex Jacobian determinant $J_{a,h}(z)=\det _{\mathbb C}DS_{a,h}(z)$ of $z\mapsto S_{a,h}(z)$. Since $S_{a,0}$ is the identity, $J_{a,0}=1$ on $B$, and hence on $\overline B$, as $z\mapsto J_{a,0}(z)$ is continuous. The open set $W=\{ (a,h,z)\in O:|J_{a,h}(z)-1|<1/2\} $ therefore contains the compact set $K\times \{ 0\} \times \overline B$, and hence all points within some positive distance of it; decreasing $h_0$, we may assume that $L\subset W$. Then $|J_{a,h}|^{2/n}=(J_{a,h}\overline{J_{a,h}})^{1/n}$ is smooth and positive on the neighborhood $W$ of $L$.

Since $S_{a,h}$ is holomorphic, $S_{a,h}^*(\mathrm dz_1\wedge \cdots \wedge \mathrm dz_ n)=J_{a,h}\, \mathrm dz_1\wedge \cdots \wedge \mathrm dz_ n$, and the conjugate identity holds for $\mathrm d\bar z_1\wedge \cdots \wedge \mathrm d\bar z_ n$. As $\eta ^ n$ is a constant multiple of $\mathrm dz_1\wedge \cdots \wedge \mathrm dz_ n\wedge \mathrm d\bar z_1\wedge \cdots \wedge \mathrm d\bar z_ n$, it follows that $S_{a,h}^*\eta ^ n=|J_{a,h}|^2\eta ^ n$. Pulling back the inequality $(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}H)^ n\ge F^ n\eta ^ n$ by $S_{a,h}$, and using again the invariance of Bedford–Taylor products under biholomorphisms, we obtain

\[ \bigl (\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}(H\circ S_{a,h})\bigr )^ n \ge (F\circ S_{a,h})^ n|J_{a,h}|^2\eta ^ n =\bigl ((F\circ S_{a,h})|J_{a,h}|^{2/n}\bigr )^ n\eta ^ n. \]

Put

\[ \Phi _{a,h}=\tfrac 12\bigl ((F\circ S_{a,h})|J_{a,h}|^{2/n} +(F\circ S_{a,-h})|J_{a,-h}|^{2/n}\bigr ), \]

a nonnegative continuous function on $\overline B$. We claim that there are constants $C_1,C_2\ge 0$ such that, for all $a\in K$ and $|h|\le h_0$,

\[ \begin{aligned} \Phi _{a,h}& \ge F-C_1|h|^2 & & \text{on }\overline B,\\ \tfrac 12(\varphi \circ S_{a,h}+\varphi \circ S_{a,-h}) & \le \varphi +C_2|h|^2 & & \text{on }\partial B. \end{aligned} \]

To prove the first inequality, fix $a\in K$ and $z\in \overline B$, and consider $\gamma (h)=F(S_{a,h}(z))|J_{a,h}(z)|^{2/n}$ as a function of the real variable $h\in \mathbb C^ n=\mathbb R^{2n}$, $|h|\le h_0$. Since $S_{a,h}(\overline B)=\overline B$, $F$ is of class $C^2$ on a neighborhood of $\overline B$, and the maps $(a,h,z)\mapsto S_{a,h}(z)$ and $(a,h,z)\mapsto |J_{a,h}(z)|^{2/n}$ are smooth near the compact set $L$, the second derivatives of $\gamma $ are bounded by a constant $2C_1$ which depends neither on $a$ nor on $z$. Taylor's formula with integral remainder, applied at $h$ and at $-h$, gives $|\gamma (h)+\gamma (-h)-2\gamma (0)|\le 2C_1|h|^2$, since the first-order terms cancel. As $\gamma (0)=F(z)$, it follows that $\Phi _{a,h}(z)=\frac12(\gamma (h)+\gamma (-h))\ge F(z)-C_1|h|^2$. The second inequality follows in the same way from a $C^2$ extension of $\varphi $ to a neighborhood of $\partial B$, since $S_{a,\pm h}$ map $\partial B$ onto itself and $S_{a,0}$ is the identity. The constants depend only on $K$, $h_0$, $n$ and the $C^2$ extensions of $F$ and $\varphi $.

Now consider

\[ V_{a,h}=\tfrac 12(H\circ S_{a,h}+H\circ S_{a,-h}) +C_1|h|^2\psi -C_2|h|^2. \]

Since additive constants do not change Monge–Ampère measures, lemma 9.8.1, applied with $t=1/2$ and the quadratic term $C_1|h|^2|z|^2$, gives

\[ (\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}V_{a,h})^ n\ge \bigl (\Phi _{a,h}+C_1|h|^2\bigr )^ n\eta ^ n\ge F^ n\eta ^ n=f\eta ^ n. \]

Since $S_{a,\pm h}$ preserve $\partial B$ and $H$ has boundary limits $\varphi $, the boundary limits of $V_{a,h}$ are $\frac12(\varphi \circ S_{a,h}+\varphi \circ S_{a,-h})-C_2|h|^2\le \varphi $. Thus $V_{a,h}$ is a subsolution, and $V_{a,h}\le H$. Evaluating at $a$, where $S_{a,\pm h}(a)=a\pm h$, and using $-1\le \psi (a)\le 0$, we obtain

\[ H(a+h)+H(a-h)-2H(a)\le 2(C_1+C_2)|h|^2 \]

for all $a\in K$ and $|h|\le h_0$; here the points $a\pm h=S_{a,\pm h}(a)$ lie in $B$.

Step 3. The equation for data of class $C^2$. Since $K$ was arbitrary and $H$ is bounded and plurisubharmonic, lemma 9.6.2 applies on $B$. Thus $H$ is continuous and locally $C^{1,1}$ on $B$, and its distributional real Hessian is a locally bounded measurable matrix $A$. Almost every $x\in B$ satisfies

\[ H(x+h)=H(x)+\nabla H(x)\cdot h+\tfrac 12h^ tA(x)h+o(|h|^2)\qquad (h\to 0), \]

and the complex Hessian $(H_{j\bar k})$ computed from $A$ is positive semidefinite almost everywhere and satisfies $(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}H)^ n=n!\pi ^{-n}\det (H_{j\bar k})\, \mathrm dV_{2n}=\det (H_{j\bar k})\, \eta ^ n$. Since $(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}H)^ n\ge f\eta ^ n$, we have $\det (H_{j\bar k})\ge f$ almost everywhere, and it remains to prove the reverse inequality.

Suppose, to the contrary, that $\det (H_{j\bar k}(x))>f(x)$ at a point $x$ at which the Taylor expansion holds and $(H_{j\bar k}(x))$ is positive semidefinite. The quadratic Taylor polynomial $Q(x+h)=H(x)+\nabla H(x)\cdot h+\frac12h^ tA(x)h$ has the constant complex Hessian $(H_{j\bar k}(x))$, which is positive semidefinite with positive determinant, hence positive definite. Choose $\varepsilon >0$ so small that the matrix $(H_{j\bar k}(x))-\varepsilon I$ is positive definite and its determinant exceeds $f(x)$. By the continuity of $f$ and the Taylor expansion at $x$, there is $r>0$ such that $\overline{B(x,r)}\subset B$, this determinant exceeds $f$ on $B(x,r)$, and $|H-Q|<\varepsilon r^2/2$ on $\partial B(x,r)$. The function

\[ q=Q-\varepsilon |z-x|^2+\varepsilon r^2/4 \]

is plurisubharmonic (proposition 8.2.2), and $(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}q)^ n=\det \bigl ((H_{j\bar k}(x))-\varepsilon I\bigr )\eta ^ n\ge f\eta ^ n$ on $B(x,r)$. Moreover $q<H-\varepsilon r^2/4$ on $\partial B(x,r)$, while $q(x)=H(x)+\varepsilon r^2/4>H(x)$.

Let $\widehat H=\max \{ H,q\} $ on $B(x,r)$ and $\widehat H=H$ on $B\setminus B(x,r)$. Since $q$ and $H$ are continuous and $q<H$ on the sphere $\partial B(x,r)$, we have $q<H$ on a neighborhood of this sphere. Hence $\widehat H$ is plurisubharmonic by proposition 8.2.5, and it coincides with $H$ near $\partial B(x,r)$. On $B(x,r)$, lemma 9.8.1, applied with $F=G=f^{1/n}$, gives $(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\widehat H)^ n\ge f\eta ^ n$. Every other point of $B$ has a neighborhood on which $\widehat H=H$, so $(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\widehat H)^ n\ge f\eta ^ n$ near it as well. Since every nonnegative test function on $B$ is a finite sum of nonnegative test functions supported in such neighborhoods (lemma 1.4.2), the inequality holds on all of $B$. Thus $\widehat H$ is a subsolution with $\widehat H(x)>H(x)$, a contradiction. Consequently $\det (H_{j\bar k})=f$ almost everywhere, that is, $(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}H)^ n=f\eta ^ n$. As $H$ is continuous on $B$ with boundary limits $\varphi $ (Step 1), it extends to a solution in $\mathrm{PSH}(B)\cap C(\overline B)$, which is locally $C^{1,1}$ on $B$.

Step 4. Stability and continuous data. Let $(f,\varphi )$ and $(g,\chi )$ be data of class $C^2$, with envelopes $H_{f,\varphi }$ and $H_{g,\chi }$ as in Step 1. Put $\delta =\| \varphi -\chi \| _{L^\infty (\partial B)}$ and $\sigma =\| f^{1/n}-g^{1/n}\| _{L^\infty (B)}$. By lemma 9.8.1,

\[ \bigl (\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}(H_{g,\chi }+\sigma \psi -\delta )\bigr )^ n\ge (g^{1/n}+\sigma )^ n\eta ^ n\ge f\eta ^ n, \]

and $H_{g,\chi }+\sigma \psi -\delta $ has boundary limits $\chi -\delta \le \varphi $. It is therefore a subsolution for $(f,\varphi )$, and since $\psi \ge -1$ we get $H_{g,\chi }-\sigma -\delta \le H_{f,\varphi }$. Exchanging the roles of the two data gives (9.33) with $R=1$ for data of class $C^2$.

Now let $f$ and $\varphi $ be continuous. Extend $F$ to $\mathbb C^ n$ by $F(z)=F(z/|z|)$ for $|z|>1$ and convolve with mollifiers; this gives smooth functions $F_ j\ge 0$ with $F_ j\to F$ uniformly on $\overline B$ (lemma 1.4.1(1)). Similarly, extending $\varphi $ to a neighborhood of $\partial B$ by $\varphi (z)=\varphi (z/|z|)$ and mollifying, we obtain smooth functions $\varphi _ j$ on a neighborhood of $\partial B$ with $\varphi _ j\to \varphi $ uniformly on $\partial B$. The data $(F_ j^ n,\varphi _ j)$ are of class $C^2$, and by Steps 1–3 the corresponding envelopes $H_ j=H_{F_ j^ n,\varphi _ j}$ are solutions which are continuous on $\overline B$ with boundary values $\varphi _ j$. By the estimate just proved,

\[ \| H_ j-H_ k\| _{L^\infty (B)}\le \| \varphi _ j-\varphi _ k\| _{L^\infty (\partial B)}+\| F_ j-F_ k\| _{L^\infty (B)}, \]

so $(H_ j)$ is a uniform Cauchy sequence on $\overline B$. Its uniform limit $U$ is continuous on $\overline B$ and equal to $\varphi $ on $\partial B$. By proposition 9.1.4, $U$ is plurisubharmonic on $B$ and $(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}H_ j)^ n\to (\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}U)^ n$ weakly; since $F_ j^ n\to f$ uniformly on $\overline B$, we obtain $(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}U)^ n=f\eta ^ n$.

Step 5. The envelope, uniqueness and stability. Let $U$ be a solution of the problem that is continuous on $\overline B$, for instance the one constructed in Step 4, and let $v$ be a subsolution for $(f,\varphi )$. For $\varepsilon >0$ put $v_\varepsilon =v+\varepsilon \psi $. By lemma 9.8.1, $(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}v_\varepsilon )^ n\ge (F+\varepsilon )^ n\eta ^ n$, while $(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}U)^ n=F^ n\eta ^ n$. Since $\varliminf _{z\to \zeta }(U(z)-v_\varepsilon (z))\ge 0$ for every $\zeta \in \partial B$, the comparison principle theorem 9.2.8 gives

\[ \int _{\{ U<v_\varepsilon \} }(F+\varepsilon )^ n\eta ^ n \le \int _{\{ U<v_\varepsilon \} }(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}v_\varepsilon )^ n \le \int _{\{ U<v_\varepsilon \} }F^ n\eta ^ n. \]

These integrals are finite, because $F$ is bounded on $B$, and $(F+\varepsilon )^ n-F^ n\ge \varepsilon ^ n$, so the Borel set $\{ U<v_\varepsilon \} $ is Lebesgue null. Hence $\max \{ U,v_\varepsilon \} =U$ almost everywhere, and the uniqueness assertion of theorem 8.2.1 gives $U\ge v_\varepsilon $ everywhere. Letting $\varepsilon \downarrow 0$, we get $U\ge v$. Since $U$ is itself a subsolution, it is the pointwise supremum of all subsolutions. In particular, any two solutions in $\mathrm{PSH}(B)\cap C(\overline B)$ coincide with this supremum, which proves uniqueness. For data of class $C^2$ the solution is therefore the envelope of Step 1, which is locally $C^{1,1}$ by Step 3.

Finally, if $(f,\varphi )$ and $(g,\chi )$ are continuous data, approximate both as in Step 4. By uniqueness, the corresponding solutions converge uniformly to $H_{f,\varphi }$ and $H_{g,\chi }$, and (9.33) for the approximating data of class $C^2$ passes to the limit. Undoing the affine change of variables proves all assertions of the theorem. $\square$

9.9 Bounded measurable densities

We now allow an arbitrary bounded nonnegative measurable density $f$, approximate it by continuous densities $f_ j$ obtained by convolution, and pass to the limit in the solutions given by theorem 9.8.2. Since $f_ j$ need not converge to $f$ uniformly, the stability estimate (9.33) is of no use here. However, the $f_ j$ have a common bound and converge in $L^1$, and we show that this suffices for convergence of the solutions in capacity. A comparison argument (lemma 9.9.1) bounds the capacity of the set where an approximate solution lies far below the limit in terms of the common bound and the $L^1$ convergence, while Hartogs' lemma and quasi-continuity control the set where it lies far above. The convergence theorem (theorem 9.4.8) then identifies the Monge–Ampère measure of the limit.

Lemma 9.9.1. Let $D\subset \mathbb C^ n$ be a bounded open set, let $u,v$ be locally bounded plurisubharmonic functions on $D$, and let $s\in \mathbb R$ and $t>0$. If $\{ u<v-s\} \Subset D$, then

\[ t^ n\operatorname {Cap}_ D(\{ u<v-s-t\} ) \le \int _{\{ u<v-s\} }(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u)^ n. \]

Proof. Let $w\in \mathrm{PSH}(D)$ with $-1\le w\le 0$. Then

\[ \{ u<v-s-t\} \subset \{ u<v-s+tw\} \subset \{ u<v-s\} , \]

so the middle set is relatively compact in $D$. Hence every $x\in \partial D$ has a neighborhood on whose intersection with $D$ we have $u\ge v-s+tw$, and in particular $\varliminf _{D\ni z\to x}\bigl (u(z)-(v(z)-s+tw(z))\bigr )\ge 0$. Expanding $(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}v+t\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}w)^ n$ by additivity and symmetry of the products (proposition 9.1.2, theorem 9.2.5), and using that all the mixed products of the locally bounded plurisubharmonic functions $v$ and $w$ are positive, we get $\bigl (\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}(v-s+tw)\bigr )^ n\ge t^ n(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}w)^ n$. The comparison principle theorem 9.2.8, applied to $u$ and $v-s+tw$, therefore gives

\[ \begin{aligned} t^ n\int _{\{ u<v-s-t\} }(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}w)^ n & \le \int _{\{ u<v-s+tw\} }\bigl (\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}(v-s+tw)\bigr )^ n\\ & \le \int _{\{ u<v-s+tw\} }(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u)^ n \le \int _{\{ u<v-s\} }(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u)^ n. \end{aligned} \]

As $w$ ranges over these functions, $w+1$ ranges over the competitors for $\operatorname {Cap}_ D$ (definition 9.3.2). Taking the supremum over $w$ proves the lemma. $\square$

Theorem 9.9.2. Let $B=B(a,R)\subset \mathbb C^ n$ with $n\ge 1$ and $R>0$, let $f\in L^\infty (B)$ be nonnegative, and let $\varphi \in C(\partial B)$ be real. There is a unique bounded plurisubharmonic function $U_{f,\varphi }$ on $B$ such that

\[ (\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}U_{f,\varphi })^ n=f\eta ^ n, \qquad \lim _{B\ni z\to x}U_{f,\varphi }(z)=\varphi (x) \quad (x\in \partial B). \]

It is the pointwise supremum of the bounded plurisubharmonic functions $v$ on $B$ such that $(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}v)^ n\ge f\eta ^ n$ and $\varlimsup _{B\ni z\to x}v(z)\le \varphi (x)$ for every $x\in \partial B$. The stability estimate (9.33) also holds with $U_{f,\varphi }$ and $U_{g,\chi }$ in place of $H_{f,\varphi }$ and $H_{g,\chi }$, for every nonnegative $g\in L^\infty (B)$ and real $\chi \in C(\partial B)$, the norm of $f^{1/n}-g^{1/n}$ being the essential supremum.

Moreover, let $f_ j\in L^\infty (B)$ and $\varphi _ j\in C(\partial B)$ be such that $0\le f_ j\le M$ almost everywhere for a constant $M$, $f_ j\to f$ in $L^1(B)$ and $\varphi _ j\to \varphi $ uniformly on $\partial B$. Then

\[ U_{f_ j,\varphi _ j}\longrightarrow U_{f,\varphi } \quad \text{in }L^1(B)\text{ and locally in capacity}. \]

Proof. Throughout the proof, $H_{g,\chi }$ denotes the solution of theorem 9.8.2 for continuous data $(g,\chi )$.

Step 1. Approximation and barriers. Extend $f$ by zero outside $B$, let $M=\| f\| _{L^\infty (B)}$, and convolve $f$ with mollifiers. The resulting smooth functions $f_ j$ on $\mathbb C^ n$ satisfy $0\le f_ j\le M$, and $f_ j\to f$ in $L^1(B)$ by lemma 1.4.1(2), applied on $\mathbb C^ n$ with the compact set $\overline B$; thus, with $\varphi _ j=\varphi $, they satisfy the hypotheses of the second part of the theorem. Let $u_ j=H_{f_ j,\varphi }$. The constant density $M$ and the zero density give two barriers. Indeed, since $M\eta ^ n\ge f_ j\eta ^ n\ge 0$, the function $H_{M,\varphi }$ belongs to the family whose supremum is $u_ j$ in theorem 9.8.2, and $u_ j$ belongs to the family whose supremum is $H_{0,\varphi }$. Hence

\[ H_{M,\varphi }\le u_ j\le H_{0,\varphi }. \]

Both barriers are continuous on $\overline B$ and equal $\varphi $ on $\partial B$.

By theorem 8.3.4, after passing to a subsequence (not relabeled), $u_ j$ converges in $L^1_{\mathrm{loc}}(B)$ to a plurisubharmonic function $U$; convergence to $-\infty $ is excluded by the lower barrier. For every compact set $K\subset B$ we have $(H_{M,\varphi }-U)_+\le |u_ j-U|$ and $(U-H_{0,\varphi })_+\le |u_ j-U|$, so the integrals of these two functions over $K$ are bounded by $\| u_ j-U\| _{L^1(K)}\to 0$ and hence vanish. Thus $H_{M,\varphi }\le U\le H_{0,\varphi }$ almost everywhere, and hence everywhere by the uniqueness assertion of theorem 8.2.1, applied to $\max \{ U,H_{M,\varphi }\} $ and $U$, and to $\max \{ U,H_{0,\varphi }\} $ and $H_{0,\varphi }$. Consequently $U$ is bounded and $\lim _{B\ni z\to x}U(z)=\varphi (x)$ for every $x\in \partial B$. Since $u_ j$ and $U$ lie between the two bounded barriers, $|u_ j-U|$ is bounded independently of $j$, so the convergence also holds in $L^1(B)$: given $\varepsilon >0$, choose a compact set $K\subset B$ with $|B\setminus K|<\varepsilon $ and use the convergence in $L^1(K)$.

Step 2. Convergence in capacity. Fix a compact set $K\subset B$ and $\delta >0$. We first show that the outer capacity of $K\cap \{ u_ j>U+\delta \} $ tends to zero. Let $\varepsilon >0$. By theorem 9.4.2, there is an open set $G\subset B$ with $\operatorname {Cap}_ B(G)<\varepsilon $ such that the restriction of $U$ to $B\setminus G$ is continuous. We claim that $u_ j<U+\delta $ on the compact set $K\setminus G$ for all sufficiently large $j$. Let $x\in K\setminus G$. By upper semicontinuity, $U<U(x)+\delta /4$ on a neighborhood of a closed ball $\overline{B'}$ centered at $x$, so Hartogs' lemma (theorem 8.3.4), applied with the constant majorant $U(x)+\delta /4$, gives $u_ j<U(x)+\delta /4$ on $\overline{B'}$ for all large $j$. Shrinking $B'$, we may also assume that $U>U(x)-\delta /4$ on $(K\setminus G)\cap B'$, since the restriction of $U$ to $B\setminus G$ is continuous. Hence $u_ j-U<\delta /2$ on $(K\setminus G)\cap B'$ for all large $j$, and a finite cover of $K\setminus G$ by such balls proves the claim. Consequently $K\cap \{ u_ j>U+\delta \} \subset G$ for all large $j$, so that $\varlimsup _ j\operatorname {Cap}_ B^*(K\cap \{ u_ j>U+\delta \} )\le \operatorname {Cap}_ B(G)<\varepsilon $. Since $\varepsilon >0$ was arbitrary, the limit is zero.

Next we consider the set where $u_ j$ lies below $U$. Since $H_{M,\varphi }\le u_ j$ and $U\le H_{0,\varphi }$, the set $\{ u_ j<U-\delta \} $ is contained in $\{ H_{M,\varphi }-H_{0,\varphi }\le -\delta \} $, which is a compact subset of $B$ because the continuous function $H_{M,\varphi }-H_{0,\varphi }$ on $\overline B$ vanishes on $\partial B$. Lemma 9.9.1, applied with $s=t=\delta $ to $u_ j$ and $U$, gives

\[ \begin{split} \delta ^ n\operatorname {Cap}_ B(\{ u_ j<U-2\delta \} ) & \le \int _{\{ u_ j<U-\delta \} }f_ j\eta ^ n\\ & \le \frac M\delta \int _ B(U-u_ j)_+\eta ^ n\longrightarrow 0, \end{split} \]

because $(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_ j)^ n=f_ j\eta ^ n\le M\eta ^ n$, $U-u_ j>\delta $ on the domain of integration, $\eta ^ n=n!\pi ^{-n}\, \mathrm dV_{2n}$, and $u_ j\to U$ in $L^1(B)$. To pass from capacity to the outer capacity of definition 9.4.5, we use quasi-continuity. The function $(U-u_ j)_+$ is bounded and quasi-continuous: $U$ and $u_ j$ are real and quasi-continuous by theorem 9.4.2, so their difference, and its composition with the continuous map $t\mapsto t_+$, are quasi-continuous by the remark following definition 9.4.1. Let $L\subset B$ be a compact neighborhood of $K$. By lemma 9.4.4,

\[ \begin{aligned} \operatorname {Cap}_ B^*(K\cap \{ u_ j<U-4\delta \} ) & \le \operatorname {Cap}_ B(L\cap \{ (U-u_ j)_+>2\delta \} )\\ & \le \operatorname {Cap}_ B(\{ u_ j<U-2\delta \} )\longrightarrow 0. \end{aligned} \]

As $K$ and $\delta $ are arbitrary, $u_ j\to U$ locally in capacity in the sense of definition 9.4.5.

Step 3. The equation, the envelope and uniqueness. The functions $u_ j$ are uniformly bounded and converge to $U$ locally in capacity, so theorem 9.4.8 gives $(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_ j)^ n\to (\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}U)^ n$ weakly. Since $f_ j\to f$ in $L^1(B)$, also $f_ j\eta ^ n\to f\eta ^ n$ weakly, and therefore $(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}U)^ n=f\eta ^ n$.

Let $v$ be a bounded plurisubharmonic function on $B$ with $(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}v)^ n\ge f\eta ^ n$ and boundary upper limits at most $\varphi $, and put $v_\varepsilon =v+\varepsilon (|z-a|^2-R^2)$ for $\varepsilon >0$. Expanding $(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}v+\varepsilon \eta )^ n$ and using positivity of the mixed products, we get $(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}v_\varepsilon )^ n\ge (f+\varepsilon ^ n)\eta ^ n$. Since $\varliminf _{z\to x}(U(z)-v_\varepsilon (z))\ge 0$ for every $x\in \partial B$, the comparison principle theorem 9.2.8 gives

\[ \int _{\{ U<v_\varepsilon \} }(f+\varepsilon ^ n)\eta ^ n\le \int _{\{ U<v_\varepsilon \} }f\eta ^ n. \]

These integrals are finite, so $\{ U<v_\varepsilon \} $ is Lebesgue null. By the uniqueness assertion of theorem 8.2.1, applied to $\max \{ U,v_\varepsilon \} $ and $U$, we obtain $U\ge v_\varepsilon $ everywhere, and letting $\varepsilon \downarrow 0$ gives $U\ge v$. Since $U$ itself is such a function $v$, it is the pointwise supremum in the statement; in particular, it does not depend on the subsequence chosen in Step 1. If $U'$ is another bounded solution with boundary limits $\varphi $, then $U'\le U$, and the same argument with $U'$ in place of $U$ gives $U\le U'$. This proves existence and uniqueness, and we write $U_{f,\varphi }=U$.

Step 4. Stability. Let $f_ j$, $\varphi _ j$ and $M$ be as in the second part of the theorem, and suppose first that $\varphi _ j=\varphi $ for all $j$. By the envelope characterization just proved, $H_{M,\varphi }\le U_{f_ j,\varphi }\le H_{0,\varphi }$. Steps 1–3 apply without change to the sequence $U_{f_ j,\varphi }$, since they used only these barrier inequalities, the equations $(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}U_{f_ j,\varphi })^ n=f_ j\eta ^ n$ with $0\le f_ j\le M$, and the convergence $f_ j\to f$ in $L^1(B)$. Hence every subsequence of $U_{f_ j,\varphi }$ has a further subsequence converging in $L^1(B)$ and locally in capacity to a bounded solution with data $(f,\varphi )$, that is, to $U_{f,\varphi }$. Therefore the whole sequence converges to $U_{f,\varphi }$ in $L^1(B)$ and locally in capacity.

For varying boundary data we use that, for a fixed density $f$, the function $U_{f,\chi }-\| \varphi -\chi \| _{L^\infty (\partial B)}$ belongs to the family whose supremum is $U_{f,\varphi }$; by symmetry, $\| U_{f,\varphi }-U_{f,\chi }\| _{L^\infty (B)}\le \| \varphi -\chi \| _{L^\infty (\partial B)}$. Applied with $f_ j$ in place of $f$, this gives $\| U_{f_ j,\varphi _ j}-U_{f_ j,\varphi }\| _{L^\infty (B)}\le \| \varphi _ j-\varphi \| _{L^\infty (\partial B)}\to 0$. Since $B$ is bounded, it follows that $U_{f_ j,\varphi _ j}-U_{f_ j,\varphi }\to 0$ in $L^1(B)$. Moreover, for every $\delta >0$ and all large $j$, the set $\{ U_{f_ j,\varphi _ j}>U_{f,\varphi }+\delta \} $ is contained in $\{ U_{f_ j,\varphi }>U_{f,\varphi }+\delta /2\} $, and similarly for the sets where the functions lie below $U_{f,\varphi }-\delta $. The general case therefore follows from the case of fixed boundary data.

Finally, we prove (9.33) for bounded measurable densities. Let $f,g\in L^\infty (B)$ be nonnegative and $\varphi ,\chi \in C(\partial B)$. Extend $f^{1/n}$ and $g^{1/n}$ by zero outside $B$, and convolve them with the same mollifiers. The resulting smooth functions $F_ j,G_ j\ge 0$ satisfy $\| F_ j-G_ j\| _{L^\infty (B)}\le \| f^{1/n}-g^{1/n}\| _{L^\infty (B)}$. Moreover $F_ j\to f^{1/n}$ and $G_ j\to g^{1/n}$ in $L^1(B)$ with a common bound, so $F_ j^ n\to f$ and $G_ j^ n\to g$ in $L^1(B)$ with a common bound. By theorem 9.8.2,

\[ \| H_{F_ j^ n,\varphi }-H_{G_ j^ n,\chi }\| _{L^\infty (B)}\le c, \qquad c=\| \varphi -\chi \| _{L^\infty (\partial B)}+R^2\| f^{1/n}-g^{1/n}\| _{L^\infty (B)}. \]

By uniqueness, $H_{F_ j^ n,\varphi }=U_{F_ j^ n,\varphi }$ and $H_{G_ j^ n,\chi }=U_{G_ j^ n,\chi }$, and by the stability just proved these functions converge in $L^1(B)$ to $U_{f,\varphi }$ and $U_{g,\chi }$. Since $|H_{F_ j^ n,\varphi }-H_{G_ j^ n,\chi }|\le c$ on $B$, the triangle inequality gives

\[ \bigl (|U_{f,\varphi }-U_{g,\chi }|-c\bigr )_+\le |U_{f,\varphi }-H_{F_ j^ n,\varphi }|+|H_{G_ j^ n,\chi }-U_{g,\chi }|, \]

and integrating over $B$ and letting $j\to \infty $, we obtain $|U_{f,\varphi }-U_{g,\chi }|\le c$ almost everywhere. The uniqueness assertion of theorem 8.2.1, applied to $\max \{ U_{f,\varphi },U_{g,\chi }+c\} $ and $U_{g,\chi }+c$, and to $\max \{ U_{g,\chi },U_{f,\varphi }+c\} $ and $U_{f,\varphi }+c$, shows that this inequality holds everywhere. $\square$

9.10 Local energy moments

To solve $(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u)^ n=\mu $ for a measure $\mu $ which need not be absolutely continuous, we approximate $\mu $ by the measures $\mu _ j$ obtained by averaging $\mu $ over the cubes of finer and finer grids, and we solve the equations for $\mu _ j$ by theorem 9.9.2. Each $\mu _ j$ has a bounded density, but the bounds are not uniform in $j$, so the arguments of the preceding section do not apply.

A bounded subsolution provides a substitute. If $\mu \le (\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}w)^ n$ for a bounded plurisubharmonic function $w$, then $\mu $ integrates the powers $(-\varphi )^ p$ of plurisubharmonic functions $\varphi $ with zero boundary values, with a bound in terms of the local energy moments $\int (-\varphi )^ p(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\varphi )^ n$ (theorem 9.10.2). The cube averages inherit this control (proposition 9.10.3). In the subsections that follow, this control replaces the common density bound. It yields convergence of the energy integrals and convergence in capacity of the solutions for $\mu _ j$, and hence the solution of the Dirichlet problem for compactly supported measures $\mu $ of this kind (theorem 9.10.12). The section closes with an inequality for mixed energies (theorem 9.10.13), which is needed in section 9.11. Throughout this section, $B\subset \mathbb C^ n$ is a ball, $n\geq 1$.

Definition 9.10.1. Let $B\subset \mathbb C^ n$, $n\geq 1$, be a ball. We denote by $\mathcal E_0(B)$ the class of bounded plurisubharmonic functions $\varphi \leq 0$ on $B$ such that

\[ \lim _{B\ni z\to \zeta }\varphi (z)=0\quad (\zeta \in \partial B), \qquad \int _ B(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\varphi )^ n<\infty . \]

For $\varphi \in \mathcal E_0(B)$ and real $p\geq 1$, the local energy moment of order $p$ is

\[ e_ p(\varphi )=\int _ B(-\varphi )^ p(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\varphi )^ n. \]
This class is due to Cegrell; see section B.9. The zero function belongs to $\mathcal E_0(B)$. Since $\varphi $ is bounded and its Monge–Ampère measure has finite mass, every moment $e_ p(\varphi )$ is finite. Since $\partial B$ is compact, the boundary limit is uniform: for every $s>0$ there is a compact set $K_ s\subset B$ such that $\varphi >-s$ on $B\setminus K_ s$. Indeed, every $\zeta \in \partial B$ has an open neighborhood $N_\zeta $ with $\varphi >-s$ on $N_\zeta \cap B$; finitely many of these neighborhoods cover $\partial B$, and we may take for $K_ s$ the complement of their union in $\overline B$. In particular, each set $\{ \varphi \leq -s\} $, $s>0$, lies in a compact subset of $B$.

Theorem 9.10.2. Let $B\subset \mathbb C^ n$ be a ball, and let $\mu $ be a finite positive Borel measure on $B$ such that $\mu \leq (\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}w)^ n$ for some bounded plurisubharmonic function $w$ on $B$. Put $m=\mu (B)$ and $M=\sup _ Bw-\inf _ Bw$, and for real $p\geq 1$ put

\[ \kappa _{n,p}=2^{n+p}(n+1)!M^ n, \qquad A_ p=\Bigl (1+\frac pn\Bigr )\kappa _{n,p}^{p/(n+p)}m^{n/(n+p)}. \]

Then, for every $\varphi \in \mathcal E_0(B)$, every real $p\geq 1$ and every $t>0$,

9.42
\begin{equation} \mu (\{ \varphi <-t\} ) \leq \min \{ m,\kappa _{n,p}e_ p(\varphi )t^{-(n+p)}\} . \end{equation}

Consequently,

9.43
\begin{equation} \int _ B(-\varphi )^ p\, \mathrm d\mu \leq A_ p\, e_ p(\varphi )^{p/(n+p)}. \end{equation}

The constant $A_ p$ depends only on $n$, $p$, $m$ and $M$.

Proof. We prove the tail bound by applying Błocki's estimate (proposition 9.3.5) to truncations of $\varphi $, and then integrate it. For $s>0$ let

\[ h_ s=\max \{ \varphi ,-s\} ,\qquad d_ s=h_ s-\varphi =(-\varphi -s)_+. \]

By the remark following definition 9.10.1, there is a compact set $K_ s\subset B$ with $\varphi >-s$ on $B\setminus K_ s$, and since $K_ s$ is compact there is a ball $B_ s\Subset B$, concentric with $B$, which contains $K_ s$. Thus $h_ s=\varphi $ on $B_ s\setminus K_ s$, which is a collar of $\partial B_ s$, and $d_ s$ vanishes outside $K_ s$. The functions $\varphi $, $h_ s$ and $w$ are bounded and plurisubharmonic near $\overline{B_ s}$, $\varphi \le h_ s$, and the oscillation of $w$ on $B_ s$ is at most $M$. Błocki's estimate on $B_ s$, together with $\mu \leq (\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}w)^ n$, therefore gives

\[ \int _ B d_ s^{n+1}\, \mathrm d\mu \leq \int _{B_ s}d_ s^{n+1}(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}w)^ n \leq (n+1)!M^ n\int _{B_ s}d_ s(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\varphi )^ n. \]

Now take $s=t/2$. On $\{ \varphi <-t\} $ we have $d_ s>t/2$. On the other hand, $0\le d_ s\le -\varphi $, and $d_ s=0$ where $-\varphi \le t/2$; since $p\geq 1$, we have $-\varphi \le (t/2)^{1-p}(-\varphi )^ p$ where $-\varphi >t/2$, and hence

\[ \int _{B_ s}d_ s(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\varphi )^ n \leq \int _{\{ -\varphi >t/2\} }(-\varphi )(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\varphi )^ n \leq (t/2)^{1-p}e_ p(\varphi ). \]

Combining the two estimates and dividing by $(t/2)^{n+1}$, we obtain

\[ \mu (\{ \varphi <-t\} )\le (n+1)!M^ n(t/2)^{-n-p}e_ p(\varphi )=\kappa _{n,p}e_ p(\varphi )t^{-(n+p)}. \]

Since also $\mu (\{ \varphi <-t\} )\le m$, this proves (9.42).

If $m=0$, both sides of (9.43) vanish. If $\kappa _{n,p}e_ p(\varphi )=0$, then (9.42) shows that $\mu (\{ \varphi <-t\} )=0$ for every $t>0$, so the left side of (9.43) vanishes. Otherwise put

\[ T=\left(\frac{\kappa _{n,p}e_ p(\varphi )}m\right)^{1/(n+p)}. \]

The first equality below is the layer-cake formula, which follows from Tonelli's theorem applied to the nonnegative function $(z,t)\mapsto pt^{p-1}\mathbf1_{\{ \varphi (z)<-t\} }$ on $B\times (0,\infty )$. Using (9.42) with the bound $m$ for $t<T$ and the second bound for $t\ge T$, we obtain

\[ \begin{aligned} \int _ B(-\varphi )^ p\, \mathrm d\mu & =p\int _0^\infty t^{p-1}\mu (\{ \varphi <-t\} )\, \mathrm dt \leq mT^ p +p\kappa _{n,p}e_ p(\varphi )\int _ T^\infty t^{-n-1}\, \mathrm dt\\ & =mT^ p+\frac pn\kappa _{n,p}e_ p(\varphi )T^{-n}. \end{aligned} \]

By the choice of $T$,

\[ mT^ p=\kappa _{n,p}e_ p(\varphi )T^{-n}=\kappa _{n,p}^{p/(n+p)}m^{n/(n+p)}e_ p(\varphi )^{p/(n+p)}, \]

so the right-hand side equals $(1+p/n)mT^ p=A_ pe_ p(\varphi )^{p/(n+p)}$. This proves (9.43). $\square$

We now show that the cube averages of a compactly supported measure $\mu $ as in theorem 9.10.2 satisfy the same moment estimate up to a constant depending only on $n$ and $p$. We call the solutions $U_ j$ of $(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}U_ j)^ n=\mu _ j$ with zero boundary values constructed in the following proposition the canonical approximants of $\mu $ (for the chosen grids).

Proposition 9.10.3. Let $\mu $, $w$, $m$ and $A_ p$ be as in theorem 9.10.2, and assume moreover that the support $K$ of $\mu $ is a compact subset of $B$. Extend $\mu $ by zero to $\mathbb C^ n$. Let $h_ j\downarrow 0$, and for each $j$ let $\mathcal Q_ j$ be a partition of $\mathbb C^ n=\mathbb R^{2n}$ into half-open cubes of side length $h_ j$. Put

\[ \mu _ j=\sum _{Q\in \mathcal Q_ j}\frac{\mu (Q)}{|Q|}\mathbf1_ Q\, \mathrm dV, \]

where $|Q|=h_ j^{2n}$ is the volume of $Q$ and $\mathrm dV=\mathrm dV_{2n}$ is Lebesgue measure. For $z\in \mathbb C^ n$ and $r>0$ let $P(z,r)=\{ \zeta \in \mathbb C^ n:|\zeta _ k-z_ k|<r,\ 1\le k\le n\} $ be the polydisc of radius $r$ centered at $z$. Then $\mu _ j\to \mu $ weakly. Moreover, there are an index $j_0$ and, for every real $p\geq 1$, a constant $D_{n,p}>0$ depending only on $n$ and $p$, such that the following hold for all $j\geq j_0$ and $p\geq 1$.

  1. The measure $\mu _ j$ has a bounded density and compact support in $B$, and $\mu _ j(B)=m$.

  2. If $Q\in \mathcal Q_ j$ is a cube which meets $K$ and $z\in Q$, then $P(z,4\sqrt2\, h_ j)\subset B$, and every plurisubharmonic function $v\le 0$ on $P(z,4\sqrt2\, h_ j)$ satisfies

    9.46
    \begin{equation} \frac1{|Q|}\int _ Q(-v)^ p\, \mathrm dV \leq D_{n,p}(-v(z))^ p, \end{equation}

    where the right side is $+\infty $ if $v(z)=-\infty $.

  3. Every $\varphi \in \mathcal E_0(B)$ satisfies

    9.47
    \begin{equation} \int _ B(-\varphi )^ p\, \mathrm d\mu _ j \leq D_{n,p}A_ p e_ p(\varphi )^{p/(n+p)}. \end{equation}

  4. The bounded plurisubharmonic solution $U_ j$ of $(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}U_ j)^ n=\mu _ j$ with zero boundary values, given by theorem 9.9.2, belongs to $\mathcal E_0(B)$ and satisfies $e_ p(U_ j)\leq (D_{n,p}A_ p)^{(n+p)/n}$.

Finally, the sequence $(U_ j)_{j\geq j_0}$ is relatively compact in $L^1(B)$, and the limit of every subsequence converging in $L^1(B)$ has a nonpositive plurisubharmonic representative.

Proof. If $m=0$, then $\mu _ j=0$ and $U_ j=0$ for all $j$, and all assertions hold. Assume that $m>0$, put $d=\operatorname {dist}(K,\mathbb C^ n\setminus B)>0$, and choose $j_0$ such that $h_ j<d/(6\sqrt{2n})$ for $j\geq j_0$; in the rest of the proof we consider only $j\geq j_0$. Since $\mu $ is a finite Borel measure, it is a Radon measure (proposition 1.1.2), so $\mu $ vanishes outside its support $K$. Hence a cube $Q$ with $\mu (Q)>0$ meets $K$, and as its diameter is $\sqrt{2n}\, h_ j<d/6$, it lies in the $(d/6)$-neighborhood of $K$, which is contained in $B$. These cubes are disjoint, each has volume $h_ j^{2n}$, and they lie in a bounded set; so there are only finitely many of them. Therefore $\mu _ j$ is a finite sum of measures with constant densities on cubes contained in $B$, so it has a bounded density and compact support in $B$, and $\mu _ j(B)=\sum _ Q\mu (Q)=m$. This proves (1).

For the weak convergence, let $g$ be a continuous function on $\mathbb C^ n$. Then

\[ \int g\, \mathrm d\mu _ j-\int g\, \mathrm d\mu =\sum _{Q\in \mathcal Q_ j}\int _ Q\left(\frac1{|Q|}\int _ Qg\, \mathrm dV-g\right)\mathrm d\mu , \]

and on every cube with $\mu (Q)>0$ the integrand is bounded by the modulus of continuity of $g$ at $\sqrt{2n}\, h_ j$ on the closed $(d/6)$-neighborhood of $K$. Hence the difference tends to zero.

To prove (2), fix a cube $Q\in \mathcal Q_ j$ which meets $K$ and a point $z\in Q$, and put $\varrho =4\sqrt2\, h_ j$. Every point of $Q$ differs from $z$ by less than $\sqrt2\, h_ j$ in each complex coordinate, so $Q\subset P(z,\varrho /2)$. Moreover $P(z,\varrho )$ lies in the Euclidean ball of radius $4\sqrt{2n}\, h_ j$ about $z$, and $\operatorname {dist}(z,K)\le \sqrt{2n}\, h_ j$, so this polydisc lies in the $(5d/6)$-neighborhood of $K$ and hence in $B$. Let $v\le 0$ be a plurisubharmonic function on $P(z,\varrho )$; if $v(z)=-\infty $, there is nothing to prove, so we assume that $v(z)>-\infty $. The function $\zeta \mapsto v(z+\varrho \zeta )$ is plurisubharmonic and nonpositive on the unit polydisc (proposition 8.2.3), with the finite value $v(z)$ at the origin. The first estimate of proposition 8.3.6, with its constant $C_{n,p}$, and the change of variables $\zeta \mapsto z+\varrho \zeta $, whose Jacobian is $\varrho ^{2n}$, give

\[ \int _ Q(-v)^ p\, \mathrm dV\le \int _{P(z,\varrho /2)}(-v)^ p\, \mathrm dV\le \varrho ^{2n}C_{n,p}^ p(-v(z))^ p. \]

Since $|Q|=h_ j^{2n}$ and $(\varrho /h_ j)^{2n}=32^ n$, we obtain (9.46) with $D_{n,p}=32^ nC_{n,p}^ p$.

For (3), let $\varphi \in \mathcal E_0(B)$. Since $\varphi \le 0$ is plurisubharmonic on $B$, (9.46) applies to $v=\varphi $ at every point $z$ of every cube with $\mu (Q)>0$. Integrating over $z\in Q$ with respect to $\mu $ and summing over the cubes, we obtain

\[ \int _ B(-\varphi )^ p\, \mathrm d\mu _ j =\sum _{Q\in \mathcal Q_ j}\frac{\mu (Q)}{|Q|}\int _ Q(-\varphi )^ p\, \mathrm dV \leq D_{n,p}\int _ B(-\varphi )^ p\, \mathrm d\mu , \]

and (9.47) follows from (9.43).

For (4), note that $\mu _ j$ equals $f_ j\eta ^ n$ for a bounded density $f_ j\ge 0$, so the solution $U_ j$ exists by theorem 9.9.2, applied with zero boundary values. Since $U_ j$ is subharmonic (theorem 8.2.1) with boundary limits $0$, the maximum principle (proposition 3.1.4) gives $U_ j\le 0$, and its Monge–Ampère measure has finite mass $\mu _ j(B)=m$; hence $U_ j\in \mathcal E_0(B)$. Taking $\varphi =U_ j$ in (9.47), we get

\[ e_ p(U_ j)=\int _ B(-U_ j)^ p\, \mathrm d\mu _ j\le D_{n,p}A_ pe_ p(U_ j)^{p/(n+p)}. \]

If $e_ p(U_ j)>0$, dividing by $e_ p(U_ j)^{p/(n+p)}$ gives the asserted bound; if $e_ p(U_ j)=0$, there is nothing to prove.

Finally write $B=B(a,R)$ and $\rho (z)=|z-a|^2-R^2$. The function $\rho $ is bounded and plurisubharmonic on $B$, and $(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\rho )^ n=\eta ^ n=n!\pi ^{-n}\, \mathrm dV$. Applying theorem 9.10.2 to the finite measure $(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\rho )^ n$ on $B$, with $p=2$, and using the uniform bound for $e_2(U_ j)$, we obtain $S=\sup _ j\int _ BU_ j^2\, \mathrm dV<\infty $. Since $U_ j\le 0$, theorem 8.3.4 shows that every subsequence has a further subsequence converging in $L^1_{\mathrm{loc}}(B)$ to a plurisubharmonic function $u$; convergence to $-\infty $ is excluded by the $L^2$ bound. Exhausting $B$ by compact sets and passing to a diagonal subsequence, we may assume that $U_ j\to u$ almost everywhere on $B$, so Fatou's lemma gives $\int _ Bu^2\, \mathrm dV\le S$. For a compact set $L\subset B$, the Cauchy–Schwarz inequality gives

\[ \int _{B\setminus L}|U_ j-u|\, \mathrm dV\le 2S^{1/2}|B\setminus L|^{1/2}, \]

which is small when $|B\setminus L|$ is small. Since $U_ j\to u$ in $L^1(L)$, it follows that $U_ j\to u$ in $L^1(B)$ along this subsequence. In particular, if a subsequence of $(U_ j)$ converges in $L^1(B)$, its limit is represented by such a function $u$. Finally $u\le 0$ almost everywhere, hence everywhere, by the uniqueness assertion of theorem 8.2.1 applied to $\max \{ u,0\} $ and $0$. $\square$

9.10.1 Convergence of energy integrals

We shall identify the limit $u$ of a convergent subsequence of canonical approximants as a solution of $(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u)^ n=\mu $. For this we must pass to the limit in integrals such as $\int U_ j\, \mathrm d\mu _ j$, in which both the integrand and the measure depend on $j$, and the energy bounds of proposition 9.10.3 control only the large negative values of the integrand. We first prove that $L^1_{\mathrm{loc}}$ convergence of uniformly bounded plurisubharmonic functions implies $L^1$ convergence against any fixed finite measure that does not charge sets of outer capacity zero (lemma 9.10.4). Writing integrals against $\mu _ j$ as integrals of cube averages against $\mu $, we then transfer this continuity to the varying measures $\mu _ j$ (theorem 9.10.5).

Lemma 9.10.4. Let $D\subset \mathbb C^ n$ be a bounded open set, and let $\nu $ be a finite positive Borel measure on $D$ such that $\nu (E)=0$ for every Borel set $E\subset D$ satisfying

\[ \operatorname {Cap}_ D^*(E\cap K)=0\qquad \text{for every compact }K\subset D. \]

Let $L\geq 0$, let $v_ j$ be plurisubharmonic functions on $D$ with $|v_ j|\leq L$, and suppose that $v_ j\to v$ in $L^1_{\mathrm{loc}}(D)$, where $v$ is the plurisubharmonic representative of the limit (theorem 8.3.4, applied on each connected component of $D$). Then

\[ \int _ D|v_ j-v|\, \mathrm d\nu \longrightarrow 0. \]

The hypothesis on $\nu $ holds if $\nu \leq (\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}w)^ n$ for a bounded plurisubharmonic function $w$ on $D$.

Proof. We show that $\varlimsup _ jv_ j=v$ $\nu $-almost everywhere (Step 1) and that $\int v_ j\, \mathrm d\nu \to \int v\, \mathrm d\nu $ (Step 2), and combine the two facts (Step 3). First, the bounds $-L\le v\le L$ hold almost everywhere: for every compact set $K\subset D$ we have $(|v|-L)_+\le |v-v_ j|$, so $\int _ K(|v|-L)_+\, \mathrm dV\le \| v_ j-v\| _{L^1(K)}\to 0$. They hold everywhere by the uniqueness assertion of theorem 8.2.1, applied on each connected component of $D$ to the plurisubharmonic functions $\max \{ v,-L\} $ and $v$, and to $\max \{ v,L\} $ and the constant $L$.

Step 1. The upper limit of $v_ j$. We show that

9.48
\begin{equation} \varlimsup _{j\to \infty }v_ j=v\qquad \nu \text{-almost everywhere}. \end{equation}

For $\ell \ge 1$ let $b_\ell =(\sup _{j\geq \ell }v_ j)^*$ be the upper semicontinuous regularization of the supremum. By theorem 8.3.2, these are plurisubharmonic functions with values in $[-L,L]$, and they decrease as $\ell $ increases. We claim that $b_\ell \downarrow v$ everywhere. Let $z\in D$ and $\varepsilon >0$. By upper semicontinuity, $v<v(z)+\varepsilon $ on a neighborhood of a closed ball $\overline{B'}\subset D$ centered at $z$, and Hartogs' lemma (theorem 8.3.4), applied on the connected component of $D$ containing $z$ with the constant majorant $v(z)+\varepsilon $, gives $v_ j<v(z)+\varepsilon $ on $\overline{B'}$ for all large $j$. Hence $\sup _{j\ge \ell }v_ j\le v(z)+\varepsilon $ on $B'$ for all large $\ell $, so $b_\ell (z)\le v(z)+\varepsilon $, and the decreasing limit of $b_\ell $ is at most $v$.

Conversely, $b_\ell \ge v_ j$ for all $j\ge \ell $, so $(v-b_\ell )_+\le |v-v_ j|$ for $j\ge \ell $; integrating over a compact set $K\subset D$ and letting $j\to \infty $, we find that $b_\ell \ge v$ almost everywhere on $K$, hence almost everywhere on $D$. The decreasing limit of the $b_\ell $ is bounded below by $-L$, so it is plurisubharmonic by theorem 8.3.1; it is at most $v$ and equals $v$ almost everywhere, hence everywhere by the uniqueness of plurisubharmonic representatives, applied on each connected component of $D$.

The sets $N_\ell =\{ \sup _{j\ge \ell }v_ j<b_\ell \} $ are Borel, and by corollary 9.5.2 they satisfy $\operatorname {Cap}_ D^*(N_\ell \cap K)=0$ for every compact $K\subset D$. By countable subadditivity of outer capacity (proposition 9.3.3), the union $N=\bigcup _\ell N_\ell $ has the same property, so $\nu (N)=0$ by hypothesis. For $z\notin N$ we have $\sup _{j\ge \ell }v_ j(z)=b_\ell (z)$ for every $\ell $, and therefore $\varlimsup _ jv_ j(z)=\lim _\ell b_\ell (z)=v(z)$. This proves (9.48). The argument applies to every sequence of plurisubharmonic functions with values in $[-L,L]$ that converges to $v$ in $L^1_{\mathrm{loc}}(D)$.

Step 2. Convergence of $\int v_ j\, \mathrm d\nu $. We first construct convex combinations of the $v_ j$ whose integrals converge to $\int v\, \mathrm d\nu $. Let $\mathcal C_\ell $ be the set of finite convex combinations of $v_\ell ,v_{\ell +1},\ldots $; its elements are plurisubharmonic functions with values in $[-L,L]$ (proposition 8.2.5). Let

\[ d_\ell =\inf _{r\in \mathcal C_\ell }\| r\| _{L^2(\nu )}. \]

Since $\mathcal C_{\ell +1}\subset \mathcal C_\ell $, the numbers $d_\ell $ increase, and they are bounded by $L\, \nu (D)^{1/2}$; let $d$ be their limit. Choose $r_\ell \in \mathcal C_\ell $ with

\[ \| r_\ell \| _{L^2(\nu )}^2\leq d_\ell ^2+\ell ^{-1}. \]

For $k\geq \ell $ the midpoint $(r_ k+r_\ell )/2$ belongs to the convex set $\mathcal C_\ell $, so its norm is at least $d_\ell $, and the parallelogram identity gives

\[ \| r_ k-r_\ell \| _{L^2(\nu )}^2 =2\| r_ k\| _{L^2(\nu )}^2+2\| r_\ell \| _{L^2(\nu )}^2-4\Bigl \| \frac{r_ k+r_\ell }2\Bigr \| _{L^2(\nu )}^2 \leq 2(d^2-d_\ell ^2)+4\ell ^{-1}. \]

The right-hand side tends to zero as $\ell \to \infty $, so we can choose $\ell _1<\ell _2<\cdots $ with $\| r_{\ell _{i+1}}-r_{\ell _ i}\| _{L^2(\nu )}\le 2^{-i}$. By the triangle inequality in $L^2(\nu )$ and the monotone convergence theorem, the function $g=\sum _ i|r_{\ell _{i+1}}-r_{\ell _ i}|$ satisfies $\| g\| _{L^2(\nu )}\le 1$. Hence $g<\infty $ $\nu $-almost everywhere, and at every point where $g$ is finite the sequence $(r_{\ell _ i})$ converges.

On the other hand, the $r_{\ell _ i}$ are plurisubharmonic with values in $[-L,L]$, and $r_\ell \to v$ in $L^1_{\mathrm{loc}}(D)$, since $\| r_\ell -v\| _{L^1(K)}\le \sup _{j\ge \ell }\| v_ j-v\| _{L^1(K)}$ for every compact $K\subset D$. By Step 1, applied to the sequence $(r_{\ell _ i})$, we have $\varlimsup _ ir_{\ell _ i}=v$ $\nu $-almost everywhere. Therefore $r_{\ell _ i}\to v$ $\nu $-almost everywhere, and dominated convergence, with the constant bound $L$ and the finite measure $\nu $, gives $\int r_{\ell _ i}\, \mathrm d\nu \to \int v\, \mathrm d\nu $.

We now show that $\int v_ j\, \mathrm d\nu \to \int v\, \mathrm d\nu $. Otherwise there are $c>0$ and a subsequence $(v_{j_ k})$ such that either $\int v_{j_ k}\, \mathrm d\nu \ge \int v\, \mathrm d\nu +c$ for all $k$, or $\int v_{j_ k}\, \mathrm d\nu \le \int v\, \mathrm d\nu -c$ for all $k$. The subsequence satisfies the hypotheses of the lemma, so the construction above, applied to it, provides convex combinations of its terms whose integrals converge to $\int v\, \mathrm d\nu $. This is impossible, since every convex combination of the $v_{j_ k}$ satisfies the same inequality as the $v_{j_ k}$ themselves.

Step 3. Conclusion. By Step 1, $\varlimsup _ jv_ j\le v$ everywhere, so $(v_ j-v)_+\to 0$ pointwise. These functions are bounded by $2L$, so $\int (v_ j-v)_+\, \mathrm d\nu \to 0$ by dominated convergence. Since

\[ |v_ j-v|=2(v_ j-v)_+-(v_ j-v), \]

Step 2 proves the assertion.

Finally, suppose that $\nu \le (\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}w)^ n$ with $w$ bounded and plurisubharmonic on $D$, and put $M=\sup _ Dw-\inf _ Dw$. If $M=0$, then $w$ is constant and $\nu =0$. Otherwise $(w-\inf _ Dw)/M$ is a competitor for $\operatorname {Cap}_ D$ (definition 9.3.2), whose Monge–Ampère measure is $M^{-n}(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}w)^ n$. Hence $\nu (A)\leq M^ n\operatorname {Cap}_ D(A)$ for every Borel set $A\subset D$, and $\operatorname {Cap}_ D(A)\le \operatorname {Cap}_ D^*(A)$ because $\operatorname {Cap}_ D$ is increasing. If $E$ is as in the hypothesis, then $\nu (E\cap K)=0$ for every compact $K\subset D$, and exhausting $D$ by countably many compact sets gives $\nu (E)=0$. $\square$

Theorem 9.10.5. Let $\mu $, $\mu _ j$ and $U_ j$ be as in proposition 9.10.3. Suppose that a subsequence of $(U_ j)$, relabeled $(U_ j)$ together with the corresponding measures $\mu _ j$, converges in $L^1(B)$, and let $u$ be the plurisubharmonic representative of its limit, which is nonpositive by proposition 9.10.3. Then $u\in L^2(\mu )$ and

9.49
\begin{equation} \int _ B|U_ j-u|\, \mathrm d\mu \longrightarrow 0, \qquad \int _ B|U_ j-u|\, \mathrm d\mu _ j\longrightarrow 0. \end{equation}

In particular,

\[ \int _ B U_ j(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}U_ j)^ n\longrightarrow \int _ Bu\, \mathrm d\mu . \]

Proof. If $m=0$, then $\mu =\mu _ j=0$ and there is nothing to prove. We assume that $m>0$, and use the cubes $Q\in \mathcal Q_ j$, the polydiscs $P(z,r)$ and the estimate (9.46) of proposition 9.10.3; recall that $K$ denotes the support of $\mu $.

Step 1. Convergence against the fixed measure $\mu $. By theorem 9.10.2 with $p=2$ and the uniform bound for $e_2(U_ j)$ in proposition 9.10.3, we have $S=\sup _ j\int _ BU_ j^2\, \mathrm d\mu <\infty $. Fix $T>0$. The truncations $\max \{ U_ j,-T\} $ are plurisubharmonic with values in $[-T,0]$, and they converge to $\max \{ u,-T\} $ in $L^1_{\mathrm{loc}}(B)$, since $s\mapsto \max \{ s,-T\} $ is $1$-Lipschitz. The measure $\mu $ satisfies the hypothesis of lemma 9.10.4, because $\mu \le (\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}w)^ n$, so the truncations converge in $L^1(\mu )$. Since $|a^2-b^2|\le 2T|a-b|$ for $a,b\in [-T,0]$, it follows that $\int \max \{ u,-T\} ^2\, \mathrm d\mu \le S$. Letting $T\to \infty $, monotone convergence gives $\int u^2\, \mathrm d\mu \le S$; in particular $u\in L^2(\mu )$, and $u$ is finite $\mu $-almost everywhere. For every measurable $v\le 0$ we have $\int _ B|v-\max \{ v,-T\} |\, \mathrm d\mu \le T^{-1}\int _ Bv^2\, \mathrm d\mu $, because $0\le \max \{ v,-T\} -v\le v^2/T$. Applying this to $v=U_ j$ and $v=u$, and using the convergence of the truncations in $L^1(\mu )$, we get $\varlimsup _ j\int _ B|U_ j-u|\, \mathrm d\mu \le 2S/T$, and letting $T\to \infty $ proves the first convergence in (9.49).

Step 2. Cube averages. For $z\in K$ let $Q_ j(z)$ be the cube of $\mathcal Q_ j$ containing $z$, and write

\[ \mathcal A_ jv(z)=\frac1{|Q_ j(z)|}\int _{Q_ j(z)}v\, \mathrm dV \]

for the average over this cube of a function $v$ integrable on it. If $v$ is $\mu _ j$-integrable, then $v$ is integrable on every cube $Q$ with $\mu (Q)>0$; on a cube with $\mu (Q)=0$ on which $v$ is not integrable we set $\mathcal A_ jv=0$, which does not affect integrals against $\mu $. Since $\mu $ is carried by $K$, the definition of $\mu _ j$ gives

9.50
\begin{equation} \int _ Bv\, \mathrm d\mu _ j=\int _ K\mathcal A_ jv\, \mathrm d\mu . \end{equation}

For a plurisubharmonic function $v\le 0$ on $B$ and $z\in K$, the Cauchy–Schwarz inequality for the probability measure $\mathrm dV/|Q_ j(z)|$ on $Q_ j(z)$ and (9.46) with $p=2$ give

9.51
\begin{equation} |\mathcal A_ jv(z)|^2 \leq \mathcal A_ j(v^2)(z)\leq D_{n,2}|v(z)|^2; \end{equation}

if $v(z)=-\infty $, the right side is infinite. This applies in particular to the possibly unbounded function $u$.

Fix $z\in K$ with $u(z)>-\infty $ and $\varepsilon >0$, and put $c=u(z)+2\varepsilon $. By upper semicontinuity of $u$ and Hartogs' lemma (theorem 8.3.4), there is a neighborhood of $z$ on which $u\le c$ and $U_ j\le c$ for all large $j$. Since the polydiscs $P(z,4\sqrt2\, h_ j)$ shrink to $z$, for $v=u$ or $v=U_ j$ and all large $j$ the function $v-c$ is plurisubharmonic and nonpositive on $P(z,4\sqrt2\, h_ j)$. Then (9.46) with $p=1$, applied to $v-c$, gives $\mathcal A_ j(c-v)(z)\le D_{n,1}(c-v(z))$, while $\mathcal A_ jv(z)\le c$ because $v\le c$ on $Q_ j(z)$. Thus, for all large $j$,

\[ c-D_{n,1}(c-v(z))\leq \mathcal A_ jv(z)\leq c, \qquad v=u\ \text{or}\ v=U_ j. \]

Consequently $\mathcal A_ ju(z)\to u(z)$, and $\mathcal A_ jU_ j(z)\to u(z)$ whenever $U_ j(z)\to u(z)$. Indeed, in both cases the lower bound tends to $u(z)+2\varepsilon (1-D_{n,1})$ as $j\to \infty $, the upper bound is $u(z)+2\varepsilon $, and $\varepsilon >0$ is arbitrary.

We use the following elementary fact: if Borel functions $F_ j$ on $K$ converge to zero $\mu $-almost everywhere and are bounded in $L^2(\mu )$, then $\int _ K|F_ j|\, \mathrm d\mu \to 0$. Indeed, for $\varepsilon >0$ the Cauchy–Schwarz inequality gives

\[ \int _ K|F_ j|\, \mathrm d\mu \leq \varepsilon m+\| F_ j\| _{L^2(\mu )} \mu (\{ |F_ j|>\varepsilon \} )^{1/2}, \]

and $\mu (\{ |F_ j|>\varepsilon \} )=\int _ K\mathbf1_{\{ |F_ j|>\varepsilon \} }\, \mathrm d\mu \to 0$ by dominated convergence; we let first $j\to \infty $ and then $\varepsilon \downarrow 0$.

By (9.51) and Step 1, the sequences $(\mathcal A_ jU_ j-u)$ and $(\mathcal A_ ju-u)$ are bounded in $L^2(\mu )$. Since $u$ is finite $\mu $-almost everywhere, we have shown that $\mathcal A_ ju\to u$ $\mu $-almost everywhere, so $\mathcal A_ ju\to u$ in $L^1(\mu )$ by the elementary fact. By the first convergence in (9.49), every subsequence of $(U_ j)$ has a further subsequence $(U_{j_ k})$ with $\sum _ k\int _ B|U_{j_ k}-u|\, \mathrm d\mu <\infty $. By the monotone convergence theorem, $\sum _ k|U_{j_ k}-u|$ is $\mu $-integrable, hence finite $\mu $-almost everywhere, so $U_{j_ k}\to u$ $\mu $-almost everywhere. By the pointwise convergence proved above, applied along this subsequence, $\mathcal A_{j_ k}U_{j_ k}\to u$ $\mu $-almost everywhere, and hence in $L^1(\mu )$. Consequently the whole sequence $\mathcal A_ jU_ j$ converges to $u$ in $L^1(\mu )$. The function $U_ j-u$ is $\mu _ j$-integrable, since $u$ is locally integrable and $\mu _ j$ has a bounded density with compact support. Therefore (9.50) gives

9.52
\begin{equation} \int _ B(U_ j-u)\, \mathrm d\mu _ j=\int _ K(\mathcal A_ jU_ j-\mathcal A_ ju)\, \mathrm d\mu \longrightarrow 0. \end{equation}

Step 3. The absolute difference. Put $g_ j=\mathcal A_ j((U_ j-u)_+)$ on $K$. Let $z\in K$ with $u(z)>-\infty $, and let $c$ be as in Step 2. For all large $j$ we have $U_ j\le c$ and $u\le c$ on $Q_ j(z)$, so $(U_ j-u)_+\le c-u$ there, and hence $0\le g_ j(z)\le c-\mathcal A_ ju(z)$. Since $\mathcal A_ ju(z)\to u(z)$, letting $j\to \infty $ and then $\varepsilon \downarrow 0$ shows that $g_ j\to 0$ $\mu $-almost everywhere. The Cauchy–Schwarz inequality and (9.51) give

\[ g_ j(z)^2\leq 2\mathcal A_ j(U_ j^2)(z) +2\mathcal A_ j(u^2)(z) \leq 2D_{n,2}\bigl (U_ j(z)^2+u(z)^2\bigr ), \]

so the $g_ j$ are bounded in $L^2(\mu )$ by Step 1. The elementary fact of Step 2 gives $\int _ Kg_ j\, \mathrm d\mu \to 0$, and by (9.50) this integral equals $\int _ B(U_ j-u)_+\, \mathrm d\mu _ j$. Combining this with (9.52) and the identity $|U_ j-u|=2(U_ j-u)_+-(U_ j-u)$, we obtain the second convergence in (9.49).

Finally, since $(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}U_ j)^ n=\mu _ j$, we have $\int _ BU_ j(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}U_ j)^ n=\int _ B(U_ j-u)\, \mathrm d\mu _ j+\int _ Bu\, \mathrm d\mu _ j$. The first term tends to zero by the second convergence in (9.49), and by (9.50) and Step 2 the second term equals $\int _ K\mathcal A_ ju\, \mathrm d\mu $, which tends to $\int _ Bu\, \mathrm d\mu $. This proves the last assertion. $\square$

9.10.2 Capacity convergence and truncated measures

In theorem 9.9.2, a common bound for the densities forced the solutions to converge in capacity, and theorem 9.4.8 then identified the limiting equation. The canonical approximants $U_ j$ of proposition 9.10.3 have no such bound unless $\mu $ itself has a bounded density, since a bound $\mu _ j\leq M\, \mathrm dV$ for all $j$ would pass to the weak limit $\mu $; nor are the functions $U_ j$ uniformly bounded. We show that the convergence of the energy integrals in theorem 9.10.5 takes the place of the density bound once we pass to the uniformly bounded truncations $\max \{ U_ j,-T\} $, whose Monge–Ampère measures differ from $\mu _ j$ by an error controlled by the energy (lemma 9.10.7). Thus a subsequence of canonical approximants that converges in $L^1(B)$ also converges locally in capacity, and the Monge–Ampère measures of the truncations of its limit approximate $\mu $ in total variation (theorem 9.10.9).

As throughout this section, $B$ is a ball in $\mathbb C^ n$, $n\geq 1$. For finite positive Borel measures $\nu _1,\nu _2$ on $B$ we use the total variation distance

\[ \| \nu _1-\nu _2\| _{\mathrm{TV}} =\sup \left\{ \left|\int _ Bf\, \mathrm d(\nu _1-\nu _2)\right|: f\text{ real Borel},\ |f|\leq 1\right\} . \]

The supremum does not change if $f$ ranges only over the real functions in $C_ c(B)$ with $|f|\leq 1$. Indeed, given a real Borel function $f$ with $|f|\leq 1$, proposition 1.2.3 provides functions in $C_ c(B)$ converging to $f$ in $L^1(\nu _1+\nu _2)$, which we may take real by passing to real parts; clamping them to $[-1,1]$ keeps them in $C_ c(B)$ and does not increase their distance from $f$.

Let $T>0$ and $\delta >0$, and let $f,g$ be functions on $B$ with values in $[-\infty ,\infty )$. Then

9.53
\begin{equation} \begin{aligned} \bigl \{ \max \{ f,-T\} >\max \{ g,-T\} +\delta \bigr \} & \subset \{ f>g+\delta \} ,\\ \bigl \{ \max \{ f,-T\} <\max \{ g,-T\} -\delta \bigr \} & \subset \{ f<g-\delta \} . \end{aligned} \end{equation}

Indeed, at a point of the first set we have $\max \{ f,-T\} >-T$, so $f=\max \{ f,-T\} >\max \{ g,-T\} +\delta \geq g+\delta $; the second inclusion is the first with $f$ and $g$ interchanged. Consequently, truncation at a fixed level preserves convergence locally in capacity (definition 9.4.5): if $f_ j\to f$, then $\max \{ f_ j,-T\} \to \max \{ f,-T\} $.

Modifying a locally bounded plurisubharmonic function on a compact set does not change its total Monge–Ampère mass.

Lemma 9.10.6. Let $\Omega \subset \mathbb C^ n$ be open, and let $\varphi ,\psi $ be locally bounded plurisubharmonic functions on $\Omega $ which coincide outside a compact set $K\subset \Omega $. Then $(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\varphi )^ n$ and $(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\psi )^ n$ agree on $\Omega \setminus K$ and assign the same finite mass to $K$. In particular $\int _\Omega (\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\varphi )^ n=\int _\Omega (\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\psi )^ n$, where both sides may be infinite.

Proof. The two measures agree on the open set $\Omega \setminus K$, because the products are local (proposition 9.1.2). For $0\leq k\leq n-1$ put $S_ k=(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\varphi )^ k\wedge (\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\psi )^{n-1-k}$. By the symmetry of the products (theorem 9.2.5) and definition 9.1.1,

\[ \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}(\varphi S_ k)=(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\varphi )^{k+1}\wedge (\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\psi )^{n-1-k}, \qquad \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}(\psi S_ k)=(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\varphi )^ k\wedge (\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\psi )^{n-k}, \]

so the sum over $k$ telescopes and

\[ (\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\varphi )^ n-(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\psi )^ n=\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}R,\qquad R=(\varphi -\psi )\sum _{k=0}^{n-1}S_ k. \]

The coefficient measures of the current $R$ of order zero vanish on $\Omega \setminus K$, so $\operatorname{Supp}R\subset K$. By lemma 1.4.1 there is $\chi \in C^\infty _ c(\Omega )$ with $0\leq \chi \leq 1$ which equals one on a neighborhood of $K$. Then $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\chi $ vanishes on a neighborhood of $\operatorname{Supp}R$, and therefore

\[ \int \chi \, (\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\varphi )^ n-\int \chi \, (\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\psi )^ n=R(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\chi )=0, \]

where both integrals are finite. Since $\chi =1$ on $K$ and the two measures agree on $\Omega \setminus K$, it follows that $(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\varphi )^ n(K)=(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\psi )^ n(K)<\infty $, and adding the equal masses of $\Omega \setminus K$ gives the last assertion. $\square$

Lemma 9.10.7. Let $\varphi \in \mathcal E_0(B)$ and $T>0$, and put $\varphi _ T=\max \{ \varphi ,-T\} $. Then $\varphi _ T\in \mathcal E_0(B)$, and for every $p\geq 1$,

\[ \int _ B(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\varphi _ T)^ n=\int _ B(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\varphi )^ n, \qquad e_ p(\varphi _ T)\leq e_ p(\varphi ), \]
9.56
\begin{equation} \| (\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\varphi _ T)^ n-(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\varphi )^ n\| _{\mathrm{TV}} \leq 2e_ p(\varphi )T^{-p}. \end{equation}

Moreover, for every Borel set $E\subset B$,

9.57
\begin{equation} \int _ E(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\varphi )^ n \leq T^ n\operatorname {Cap}_ B(E)+e_ p(\varphi )T^{-p}. \end{equation}

Proof. Since $\varphi $ tends to zero uniformly at $\partial B$, there is an open neighborhood $V$ of $\partial B$ such that $\varphi >-T$ on $V\cap B$; there $\varphi _ T=\varphi $. Thus $\varphi _ T$ is bounded, nonpositive and plurisubharmonic, it has zero boundary values, and it coincides with $\varphi $ outside the compact set $\overline B\setminus V\subset B$. By lemma 9.10.6, the measures $(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\varphi _ T)^ n$ and $(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\varphi )^ n$ have the same total mass, which is finite; in particular $\varphi _ T\in \mathcal E_0(B)$.

Put $F_ T=\{ \varphi \leq -T\} $. By maximum locality (proposition 9.2.7), the two measures agree on the complement $\{ \varphi >-T\} $. Since their total masses are equal and finite, they also assign the same mass $m_ T$ to $F_ T$, and Chebyshev's inequality gives

\[ m_ T\leq T^{-p}\int _{F_ T}(-\varphi )^ p(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\varphi )^ n\leq e_ p(\varphi )T^{-p}. \]

As $\varphi _ T=-T$ on $F_ T$, we get

\[ e_ p(\varphi _ T)=\int _{\{ \varphi >-T\} }(-\varphi )^ p(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\varphi )^ n+T^ pm_ T \leq e_ p(\varphi ). \]

The two measures differ only on $F_ T$, where each has mass $m_ T$; this proves (9.56). Finally, $(\varphi _ T+T)/T$ is plurisubharmonic with values in $[0,1]$, so it is a competitor for $\operatorname {Cap}_ B$ (definition 9.3.2), and therefore $\int _ E(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\varphi _ T)^ n\leq T^ n\operatorname {Cap}_ B(E)$. Since the two measures agree on $E\setminus F_ T$, we conclude
