\[ \int _ E(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\varphi )^ n \leq \int _{E\setminus F_ T}(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\varphi _ T)^ n+m_ T \leq T^ n\operatorname {Cap}_ B(E)+e_ p(\varphi )T^{-p}. \]
$\square$

Proposition 9.10.8. Let $p\geq 1$.

  1. For every $\varphi \in \mathcal E_0(B)$ and every $T>0$,

    \[ \operatorname {Cap}_ B(\{ \varphi <-T\} ) \leq 2^{n+p}(n+1)!\, e_ p(\varphi )T^{-(n+p)}. \]

  2. Let $\varphi _ j\in \mathcal E_0(B)$ converge in $L^1_{\mathrm{loc}}(B)$, let $u$ be the plurisubharmonic representative of the limit (theorem 8.3.4), and suppose that $E_ p=\sup _ je_ p(\varphi _ j)<\infty $. For every compact set $K\subset B$ there is a constant $C_{K,p}$, depending only on $K$, $p$ and the ball $B$, such that

    9.58
    \begin{equation} \operatorname {Cap}_ B^*(K\cap \{ u<-T\} ) \leq C_{K,p}E_ p^{p/(n+p)}T^{-p}\qquad (T>0). \end{equation}

    In particular, $\operatorname {Cap}^*_ B(K\cap \{ u=-\infty \} )=0$ for every compact set $K\subset B$.

Proof. (1) Let $h$ be a competitor for $\operatorname {Cap}_ B$, that is, $h\in \mathrm{PSH}(B)$ and $0\leq h\leq 1$. The set $\{ \varphi <-T\} $ is open, because $\varphi $ is upper semicontinuous, and it is relatively compact in $B$, because $\varphi $ has zero boundary values. Hence $\nu =\mathbf1_{\{ \varphi <-T\} }(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}h)^ n$ is a finite measure, and it is dominated by $(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}h)^ n$. The tail estimate (9.42) of theorem 9.10.2, applied to $\nu $ and $w=h$, involves the constant $\kappa _{n,p}=2^{n+p}(n+1)!M^ n$, where $M\leq 1$ is the oscillation of $h$ on $B$, but not the mass of $\nu $; it gives

\[ \int _{\{ \varphi <-T\} }(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}h)^ n=\nu (\{ \varphi <-T\} ) \leq 2^{n+p}(n+1)!\, e_ p(\varphi )T^{-(n+p)}. \]

Taking the supremum over $h$ proves (1).

(2) Choose an open set $U$ and compact sets $L,L'\subset B$ with $K\subset U\Subset \operatorname{Int}L$ and $L\subset \operatorname{Int}L'$. By the Chern–Levine–Nirenberg inequality (theorem 9.1.3, with the base current $1$ and the pair $L\subset \operatorname{Int}L'$), there is a constant $C_ L$ with $\int _ L(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}h)^ n\leq C_ L$ for every competitor $h$, since $\| h\| _{L^\infty (L')}\leq 1$. The finite measure $\mathbf1_ L(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}h)^ n$ is dominated by $(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}h)^ n$ and has mass at most $C_ L$, so the constant $A_ p$ of theorem 9.10.2 for this measure and $w=h$ is at most

\[ C_{K,p}=\Bigl (1+\frac pn\Bigr )\bigl (2^{n+p}(n+1)!\bigr )^{p/(n+p)}C_ L^{n/(n+p)}. \]

Hence the moment estimate (9.43), applied to $\varphi _ j$, gives

\[ \int _ L(-\varphi _ j)^ p(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}h)^ n \leq C_{K,p}E_ p^{p/(n+p)} \]

for all $j$ and all competitors $h$.

We pass this bound to the limit $u$. Since the $\varphi _ j$ are nonpositive, $u\leq 0$ almost everywhere, and hence everywhere by the submean inequality. Fix $S>0$. The truncations $\max \{ \varphi _ j,-S\} $ take values in $[-S,0]$ and converge to the plurisubharmonic function $\max \{ u,-S\} $ in $L^1_{\mathrm{loc}}(B)$, since $t\mapsto \max \{ t,-S\} $ is $1$-Lipschitz. Since $\mathbf1_ L(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}h)^ n\leq (\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}h)^ n$, lemma 9.10.4 shows that they also converge in $L^1(\mathbf1_ L(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}h)^ n)$. As $t\mapsto t^ p$ is Lipschitz on $[0,S]$ and $(-\max \{ \varphi _ j,-S\} )^ p\leq (-\varphi _ j)^ p$, it follows that

\[ \begin{aligned} \int _ L(-\max \{ u,-S\} )^ p(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}h)^ n & =\lim _{j\to \infty }\int _ L(-\max \{ \varphi _ j,-S\} )^ p(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}h)^ n\\ & \leq C_{K,p}E_ p^{p/(n+p)}. \end{aligned} \]

Letting $S\to \infty $, monotone convergence gives the same bound for $\int _ L(-u)^ p(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}h)^ n$. The set $U\cap \{ u<-T\} $ is open, because $u$ is upper semicontinuous, and it contains $K\cap \{ u<-T\} $. By Chebyshev's inequality its $(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}h)^ n$-measure is at most $C_{K,p}E_ p^{p/(n+p)}T^{-p}$. Taking the supremum over $h$ bounds $\operatorname {Cap}_ B(U\cap \{ u<-T\} )$, and hence the outer capacity in (9.58). Finally, $K\cap \{ u=-\infty \} $ is contained in $K\cap \{ u<-T\} $ for every $T$, so letting $T\to \infty $ shows that it has zero outer capacity. $\square$

Theorem 9.10.9. Under the hypotheses of theorem 9.10.5, the relabeled subsequence $U_ j$ converges to $u$ locally in capacity. Moreover, let $E_2=\sup _ je_2(U_ j)$, which is finite by proposition 9.10.3. For every $T>0$, the Monge–Ampère measure of the bounded plurisubharmonic function $u_ T=\max \{ u,-T\} $ has total mass at most $m$, and

9.61
\begin{equation} \| (\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_ T)^ n-\mu \| _{\mathrm{TV}}\leq 2E_2T^{-2}. \end{equation}

Proof. We estimate the capacities of the sets where the truncation $\max \{ U_ j,-T\} $ lies far below or far above $u_ T$ (Steps 1 and 2) and deduce convergence in capacity (Step 3). The estimate for $(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_ T)^ n$ then follows from the convergence of the truncated measures (Step 4). Fix $T>0$ and write $a_ j=\max \{ U_ j,-T\} $ and $b=u_ T$. These plurisubharmonic functions take values in $[-T,0]$, and $a_ j\to b$ in $L^1_{\mathrm{loc}}(B)$ because truncation is $1$-Lipschitz. By theorem 9.10.5, $\varepsilon _ j=\int _ B|U_ j-u|\, \mathrm d\mu _ j\to 0$.

Step 1. The sets where $a_ j<b-\delta $. Let $\delta >0$. The set $\{ a_ j<b-\delta /2\} $ is relatively compact in $B$, because $b\leq 0$ while $a_ j$ tends to zero at $\partial B$. Applying lemma 9.9.1 with $s=t=\delta /2$ and then Chebyshev's inequality, we obtain

\[ \begin{aligned} \operatorname {Cap}_ B(\{ a_ j<b-\delta \} ) & \leq \left(\frac2\delta \right)^ n\int _{\{ a_ j<b-\delta /2\} }(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}a_ j)^ n\\ & \leq 2^{n+1}\delta ^{-(n+1)}\int _ B(b-a_ j)_+(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}a_ j)^ n. \end{aligned} \]

The weight $(b-a_ j)_+$ takes values in $[0,T]$, and it is at most $|U_ j-u|$, since truncation does not increase distances. By (9.56) with $p=2$, $\| (\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}a_ j)^ n-\mu _ j\| _{\mathrm{TV}}\leq 2E_2T^{-2}$, where $\mu _ j=(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}U_ j)^ n$. Hence

\[ \int _ B(b-a_ j)_+(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}a_ j)^ n \leq \int _ B(b-a_ j)_+\, \mathrm d\mu _ j+T\cdot 2E_2T^{-2} \leq \varepsilon _ j+\frac{2E_2}T, \]

and therefore $\operatorname {Cap}_ B(\{ a_ j<b-\delta \} )\leq 2^{n+1}\delta ^{-(n+1)}(\varepsilon _ j+2E_2/T)$.

Step 2. The sets where $a_ j>b+\delta $. Let $L\subset B$ be compact. By theorem 9.4.2, for every $\sigma >0$ there is an open set $G\subset B$ with $\operatorname {Cap}_ B(G)<\sigma $ such that $b$ is continuous on $B\setminus G$. We claim that $L\cap \{ a_ j>b+\delta \} \subset G$ for all sufficiently large $j$. Let $z\in L\setminus G$. Since $b$ is upper semicontinuous, $b<b(z)+\delta /3$ on a closed ball $\overline{B(z,r)}\subset B$. Hartogs' lemma (theorem 8.3.4), applied to the sequence $a_ j$ with the constant majorant $b(z)+\delta /3$, gives $a_ j<b(z)+\delta /3$ on $\overline{B(z,r)}$ for all large $j$. Since $b$ is continuous on $B\setminus G$, after shrinking $r$ we also have $b>b(z)-\delta /3$ on $(L\setminus G)\cap B(z,r)$. Hence $a_ j-b<2\delta /3$ on $(L\setminus G)\cap B(z,r)$ for all large $j$. As $L\setminus G$ is compact, finitely many such balls cover it, which proves the claim. Consequently $\varlimsup _ j\operatorname {Cap}_ B(L\cap \{ a_ j>b+\delta \} )\leq \sigma $ for every $\sigma >0$, that is, $\operatorname {Cap}_ B(L\cap \{ a_ j>b+\delta \} )\to 0$.

Step 3. Convergence in capacity. Let $K\subset B$ be compact, and apply Step 2 to a compact set $L\subset B$ with $K\subset \operatorname{Int}L$. The bounded functions $a_ j-b$ are quasi-continuous by theorem 9.4.2 and the remark following definition 9.4.1. Therefore lemma 9.4.4, together with Steps 1 and 2 applied with $\delta /2$ in place of $\delta $, gives

\[ \begin{aligned} & \varlimsup _{j\to \infty }\operatorname {Cap}_ B^*(K\cap \{ |a_ j-b|>\delta \} )\\ & \qquad \leq \varlimsup _{j\to \infty }\bigl (\operatorname {Cap}_ B(L\cap \{ a_ j>b+\delta /2\} ) +\operatorname {Cap}_ B(\{ a_ j<b-\delta /2\} )\bigr )\\ & \qquad \leq 2^{2n+3}\delta ^{-(n+1)}\frac{E_2}T. \end{aligned} \]

It remains to remove the truncation. Outside $\{ U_ j<-T\} \cup \{ u<-T\} $ we have $a_ j=U_ j$ and $b=u$. Hence both sets $K\cap \{ U_ j>u+\delta \} $ and $K\cap \{ U_ j<u-\delta \} $ in definition 9.4.5 are contained in

\[ \bigl (K\cap \{ |a_ j-b|>\delta \} \bigr )\cup \{ U_ j<-T\} \cup \bigl (K\cap \{ u<-T\} \bigr ). \]

By proposition 9.10.8 with $p=2$, applied in part (1) to $U_ j$, whose energy is at most $E_2$, and in part (2) to the sequence $(U_ j)$,

\[ \operatorname {Cap}_ B(\{ U_ j<-T\} )\leq \frac{2^{n+2}(n+1)!\, E_2}{T^{n+2}},\qquad \operatorname {Cap}_ B^*(K\cap \{ u<-T\} )\leq \frac{C_{K,2}E_2^{2/(n+2)}}{T^2}, \]

and the capacity of the open set $\{ U_ j<-T\} $ equals its outer capacity. By subadditivity of the outer capacity, the upper limits as $j\to \infty $ of the outer capacities of the two sets in definition 9.4.5 are bounded by the sum of three terms, each of which tends to zero as $T\to \infty $. Since these upper limits do not depend on $T$, they vanish. Thus $U_ j\to u$ locally in capacity.

Step 4. The truncated limit measure. Fix $T>0$ again. By Step 3 and (9.53), the uniformly bounded functions $a_ j$ converge to $b$ locally in capacity, so $(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}a_ j)^ n\to (\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_ T)^ n$ weakly on $B$ by theorem 9.4.8. By lemma 9.10.7, each $(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}a_ j)^ n$ has total mass $\mu _ j(B)=m$. Testing with functions $\chi \in C_ c(B)$, $0\leq \chi \leq 1$, and letting $\chi $ increase to one, we see that $(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_ T)^ n$ has total mass at most $m$. Moreover, (9.56) gives

\[ \left|\int _ B\chi \, (\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}a_ j)^ n-\int _ B\chi \, \mathrm d\mu _ j\right|\leq 2E_2T^{-2} \]

for every real $\chi \in C_ c(B)$ with $|\chi |\leq 1$. Since $\mu _ j\to \mu $ weakly by proposition 9.10.3, letting $j\to \infty $ yields the same bound with $(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_ T)^ n$ and $\mu $ in place of $(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}a_ j)^ n$ and $\mu _ j$. Both limit measures are finite, so by the remark following the definition of the total variation distance, the supremum over such $\chi $ equals $\| (\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_ T)^ n-\mu \| _{\mathrm{TV}}$. This proves (9.61). $\square$

9.10.3 A bounded subsolution and the limiting equation

We can now solve the Dirichlet problem

\[ (\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}U)^ n=\mu \quad \text{on }B,\qquad U=0\quad \text{on }\partial B, \]

for a compactly supported measure $\mu $ dominated by the Monge–Ampère measure of a bounded plurisubharmonic function, and show that the whole sequence of canonical approximants of $\mu $ converges to the solution (theorem 9.10.12). By theorem 9.10.9, the limit $u$ of a subsequence of canonical approximants converging in $L^1(B)$ satisfies $(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\max \{ u,-T\} )^ n\to \mu $ as $T\to \infty $, so it remains to show that $u$ is bounded and has zero boundary values. For this we build from the bounded subsolution a barrier with zero boundary values and compare the approximants with a strict perturbation of the barrier. The comparison involves the masses that the varying measures $\mu _ j$ give to varying sublevel sets, and we first prove a convergence lemma for such masses.

Lemma 9.10.10. Let $K_0\subset B$ be compact, and let $\nu _ j,\nu $ be finite positive Borel measures on $B$, supported in $K_0$, with $\nu _ j\to \nu $ weakly. Assume that there is a nondecreasing function $\omega \colon [0,+\infty ]\to [0,+\infty ]$ with $\omega (t)\to 0$ as $t\downarrow 0$ such that

\[ \nu _ j(E)\leq \omega (\operatorname {Cap}_ B^*(E)) \]

for all $j$ and every Borel set $E\subset B$. Let $f_ j,f$ be real Borel functions on $B$ bounded by a common constant, with $f$ quasi-continuous on a neighborhood of $K_0$, and suppose that $\operatorname {Cap}_ B^*(K_0\cap \{ |f_ j-f|>\delta \} )\to 0$ for every $\delta >0$. Then

\[ \int _ Bf_ j\, \mathrm d\nu _ j\longrightarrow \int _ Bf\, \mathrm d\nu . \]

Moreover, for every $c\in \mathbb R$ with $\nu (\{ f=c\} )=0$,

9.67
\begin{equation} \nu _ j(\{ f_ j<c\} )\longrightarrow \nu (\{ f<c\} ). \end{equation}

Proof. Let $C$ be a common bound for $|f_ j|$ and $|f|$. Since $\omega $ is nonnegative and nondecreasing with $\omega (t)\to 0$ as $t\downarrow 0$, we have $\omega (0)=0$ and $\omega (t_ j)\to 0$ whenever $t_ j\geq 0$ and $t_ j\to 0$. Choose $\chi \in C_ c(B)$ with $0\leq \chi \leq 1$ and $\chi =1$ on $K_0$. Since the measures are carried by $K_0$, we have $\nu _ j(B)=\int \chi \, \mathrm d\nu _ j\to \int \chi \, \mathrm d\nu =\nu (B)$, so the masses of the $\nu _ j$ and of $\nu $ have a common bound $M$. The capacity bound passes to $\nu $ on open sets: if $G\subset B$ is open, then $\operatorname {Cap}^*_ B(G)=\operatorname {Cap}_ B(G)$, and proposition 1.5.2 gives $\nu (G)\leq \varliminf _ j\nu _ j(G)\leq \omega (\operatorname {Cap}_ B(G))$.

We first consider the fixed weight $f$. Let $L\subset B$ be a compact neighborhood of $K_0$ on which $f$ is quasi-continuous, and let $\kappa >0$. Choose an open set $G\subset B$ with $\operatorname {Cap}_ B(G)<\kappa $ such that $f$ is continuous on the compact set $L\setminus G$. By lemma 9.4.3, this restriction extends to a continuous function on $\mathbb C^ n$ bounded by $C$. Multiplying the extension by a cutoff in $C_ c(\operatorname{Int}L)$ with values in $[0,1]$ and equal to one on $K_0$, we obtain $g\in C_ c(B)$ with $|g|\leq C$ and $g=f$ on $K_0\setminus G$. Since $\nu _ j$ and $\nu $ are carried by $K_0$,

\[ \left|\int _ Bf\, \mathrm d\nu _ j-\int _ Bg\, \mathrm d\nu _ j\right|\leq 2C\nu _ j(G)\leq 2C\omega (\kappa ), \]

and similarly for $\nu $, since $\nu (G)\leq \omega (\operatorname {Cap}_ B(G))\leq \omega (\kappa )$. Since $\int g\, \mathrm d\nu _ j\to \int g\, \mathrm d\nu $, we get $\varlimsup _ j|\int f\, \mathrm d\nu _ j-\int f\, \mathrm d\nu |\leq 4C\omega (\kappa )$. Letting $\kappa \downarrow 0$ proves the assertion for the fixed weight.

For the varying weights, let $\delta >0$. Since $|f_ j-f|\leq 2C$ and $\nu _ j$ is carried by $K_0$,

\[ \int _ B|f_ j-f|\, \mathrm d\nu _ j \leq \delta M+2C\omega \left( \operatorname {Cap}_ B^*(K_0\cap \{ |f_ j-f|>\delta \} )\right). \]

As $j\to \infty $, the capacity on the right tends to zero, and hence so does the second term. Letting $\delta \downarrow 0$ gives $\int |f_ j-f|\, \mathrm d\nu _ j\to 0$, which together with the fixed-weight case proves the first assertion.

For the level sets, fix $s>0$ and let $\sigma ^-_ s,\sigma ^+_ s\colon \mathbb R\to [0,1]$ be the continuous piecewise linear functions such that $\sigma ^-_ s$ equals one on $(-\infty ,c-s]$, zero on $[c,\infty )$ and is linear in between, and $\sigma ^+_ s$ equals one on $(-\infty ,c]$, zero on $[c+s,\infty )$ and is linear in between. Then

\[ \mathbf1_{(-\infty ,c-s)}\leq \sigma ^-_ s \leq \mathbf1_{(-\infty ,c)}\leq \sigma ^+_ s \leq \mathbf1_{(-\infty ,c+s]}. \]

Since $\sigma ^\pm _ s$ are Lipschitz, $\sigma ^\pm _ s\circ f_ j$ converge to $\sigma ^\pm _ s\circ f$ in the same sense as $f_ j\to f$, and $\sigma ^\pm _ s\circ f$ is quasi-continuous on a neighborhood of $K_0$ (remark following definition 9.4.1). The first assertion therefore gives

\[ \nu (\{ f<c-s\} )\leq \varliminf _{j\to \infty }\nu _ j(\{ f_ j<c\} ) \leq \varlimsup _{j\to \infty }\nu _ j(\{ f_ j<c\} )\leq \nu (\{ f\leq c+s\} ). \]

As $s\downarrow 0$, the two outer terms tend to $\nu (\{ f<c\} )$ and $\nu (\{ f\leq c\} )$, which are equal since $\nu (\{ f=c\} )=0$. This proves (9.67). $\square$

Corollary 9.10.11. Let $\varphi _ j\in \mathcal E_0(B)$ with $\sup _ je_2(\varphi _ j)<\infty $ converge in $L^1_{\mathrm{loc}}(B)$, and let $u$ be the plurisubharmonic representative of the limit (theorem 8.3.4). If $\nu $ is a finite positive Borel measure on $B$ with $\nu \leq (\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}w)^ n$ for some bounded $w\in \mathrm{PSH}(B)$, then $u\in L^2(\nu )$ and $\varphi _ j\to u$ in $L^1(\nu )$.

Proof. By the moment estimate (9.43) with $p=2$, the integrals $\int _ B\varphi _ j^2\, \mathrm d\nu $ are bounded uniformly in $j$. Since the $\varphi _ j$ are nonpositive, so is $u$, by the submean inequality. Fix $T>0$. By lemma 9.10.4, the truncations $\max \{ \varphi _ j,-T\} $ converge to $\max \{ u,-T\} $ in $L^1(\nu )$, and so do their squares, since squaring is Lipschitz on $[-T,0]$. Hence $\int _ B\max \{ u,-T\} ^2\, \mathrm d\nu \leq \sup _ j\int _ B\varphi _ j^2\, \mathrm d\nu $, and letting $T\to \infty $ shows that $u\in L^2(\nu )$ with the same bound. For a function $v\leq 0$ we have $0\leq \max \{ v,-T\} -v\leq v^2/T$, so the $L^1(\nu )$ distances between $\varphi _ j,u$ and their truncations are at most $T^{-1}$ times a bound independent of $j$. Letting first $j\to \infty $ and then $T\to \infty $ proves that $\varphi _ j\to u$ in $L^1(\nu )$. $\square$

Theorem 9.10.12. Let $B\subset \mathbb C^ n$ be a ball, $n\geq 1$, and let $\mu $ be a finite positive Borel measure with compact support in $B$. Suppose that $\mu \leq (\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}w)^ n$ for some bounded $w\in \mathrm{PSH}(B)$. Then there is a unique bounded plurisubharmonic function $U$ on $B$ with

\[ (\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}U)^ n=\mu ,\qquad \lim _{B\ni z\to \zeta }U(z)=0\quad (\zeta \in \partial B). \]

It belongs to $\mathcal E_0(B)$, and it is the pointwise supremum of the bounded plurisubharmonic functions $v$ on $B$ such that $(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}v)^ n\geq \mu $ and $\varlimsup _{B\ni z\to \zeta }v(z)\leq 0$ for every $\zeta \in \partial B$. Moreover, for every choice of grids, the whole sequence of canonical approximants $U_ j$ of $\mu $ (proposition 9.10.3) converges to $U$ in $L^1(B)$ and locally in capacity, and

\[ \int _ B|U_ j-U|\, \mathrm d\mu +\int _ B|U_ j-U|\, \mathrm d\mu _ j\longrightarrow 0. \]

Proof. If $\mu =0$, then all $\mu _ j$ and all $U_ j$ vanish, and $U=0$ is a solution. By the maximum principle (proposition 3.1.4), every plurisubharmonic function on $B$ whose upper boundary limits are at most zero is nonpositive, so $U=0$ is the stated supremum, and the last assertion of theorem 9.2.8, applied in both directions, shows that any two bounded solutions coincide. We may therefore assume that $m=\mu (B)>0$. We construct a barrier $W$ (Step 1) and, along a convergent subsequence of canonical approximants, prove that the masses of certain sublevel sets converge (Step 2). The comparison theorem then shows that the limit lies above $W$ and is a solution (Step 3), and uniqueness gives the convergence of the whole sequence (Step 4).

Step 1. A barrier with zero boundary values. Write $B=B(a,R)$ and $\rho (z)=|z-a|^2-R^2$, let $M=\sup _ Bw-\inf _ Bw$, and put $w_0=w-\sup _ Bw-1$, so that $-M-1\leq w_0\leq -1$. Since $\operatorname{Supp}\mu $ is a compact subset of $B$, the function $\rho $ is bounded away from zero on an open neighborhood $N\Subset B$ of $\operatorname{Supp}\mu $, and we can choose $A>0$ with $A\rho <-M-2$ on $N$. Put

\[ W=\max \{ w_0,A\rho \} , \]

a plurisubharmonic function on $B$ (proposition 8.2.5). Then $W=w_0$ on $N$, while $W=A\rho $ near $\partial B$, because $A\rho \to 0$ at $\partial B$ and $w_0\leq -1$. Thus $W$ is bounded and negative and has zero boundary values. By locality on the open set $N$ (proposition 9.1.2), $(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}W)^ n=(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}w)^ n\geq \mu $ there; as $\mu $ is carried by $N$, we get $(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}W)^ n\geq \mu $ on $B$.

For $\varepsilon >0$ put $W_\varepsilon =W+\varepsilon \rho $. It equals $(A+\varepsilon )\rho $ near $\partial B$, so its Monge–Ampère measure is a smooth measure there and is finite on the remaining compact part of $B$; hence it has finite total mass. Expanding $(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}W+\varepsilon \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\rho )^ n$ and dropping the mixed terms, which are positive, we obtain

9.68
\begin{equation} \nu _\varepsilon :=(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}W_\varepsilon )^ n \geq \mu +\varepsilon ^ n(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\rho )^ n. \end{equation}

Step 2. Convergence of the masses of sublevel sets. By proposition 9.10.3, a subsequence of the canonical approximants, still denoted $U_ j$ (together with the corresponding measures $\mu _ j$), converges in $L^1(B)$; let $u$ be the nonpositive plurisubharmonic representative of the limit. By theorem 9.10.9, $U_ j\to u$ locally in capacity, and $E_2=\sup _ je_2(U_ j)<\infty $. By corollary 9.10.11 with $\nu =\nu _\varepsilon $, we have $U_ j\to u$ in $L^1(\nu _\varepsilon )$ for every $\varepsilon >0$.

We check that lemma 9.10.10 applies to the measures $\mu _ j$ and $\mu $. Let $K$ be the support of $\mu $ and $d=\operatorname {dist}(K,\mathbb C^ n\setminus B)>0$, and let $h_ j$ be the side lengths of the cubes defining $\mu _ j$. A cube $Q$ with $\mu _ j(Q)>0$ satisfies $\mu (Q)>0$, so it meets $K$. Since $h_ j\to 0$, after discarding finitely many indices every such cube has diameter $\sqrt{2n}\, h_ j<d/2$, and then $\mu _ j$ is supported in the compact set $K_0=\{ z:\operatorname {dist}(z,K)\leq d/2\} \subset B$, which also contains the support of $\mu $. By proposition 9.10.3, $\mu _ j\to \mu $ weakly. By (9.57) with $p=2$, and since $\operatorname {Cap}_ B(E)\leq \operatorname {Cap}_ B^*(E)$, we have $\mu _ j(E)\leq T^ n\operatorname {Cap}_ B^*(E)+E_2T^{-2}$ for every Borel set $E\subset B$ and every $T>0$. Let $\omega (t)$ be the supremum of $\mu _ j(E)$ over all $j$ and all Borel sets $E\subset B$ with $\operatorname {Cap}_ B^*(E)\leq t$. Then $\omega $ is nondecreasing with values in $[0,m]$, and $\omega (t)\leq T^ nt+E_2T^{-2}$ for every $T>0$. Choosing first $T$ large and then $t$ small shows that $\omega (t)\to 0$ as $t\downarrow 0$.

Fix $\varepsilon >0$. The level sets $\{ u-W_\varepsilon =-\delta \} $, $0<\delta <1$, are pairwise disjoint Borel sets, and $\mu +\nu _\varepsilon $ is a finite measure, so by lemma 1.1.5 all but countably many of these sets are null for $\mu +\nu _\varepsilon $, hence for both measures. Choose $\delta \in (0,1)$ such that $\{ u-W_\varepsilon =-\delta \} $ is null for both $\mu $ and $\nu _\varepsilon $, and fix $T>\| W_\varepsilon \| _\infty +2$. Since $W_\varepsilon -\delta >-T$, the sets $\{ U_ j<W_\varepsilon -\delta \} $ and $\{ u<W_\varepsilon -\delta \} $ do not change when $U_ j$ and $u$ are replaced by $a_ j=\max \{ U_ j,-T\} $ and $b=\max \{ u,-T\} $.

We apply lemma 9.10.10 to the functions $f_ j=a_ j-W_\varepsilon $ and $f=b-W_\varepsilon $, which are bounded by $T+\| W_\varepsilon \| _\infty $ and quasi-continuous by theorem 9.4.2 and the remark following definition 9.4.1. Since $U_ j\to u$ locally in capacity, (9.53) shows that $a_ j\to b$ locally in capacity, and hence $f_ j\to f$, because $W_\varepsilon $ is finite. Moreover, $\mu (\{ f=-\delta \} )=0$ by the choice of $\delta $, since $\{ f=-\delta \} =\{ u-W_\varepsilon =-\delta \} $: indeed, $b=u$ wherever $u\geq -T$, while $u-W_\varepsilon <-2$ and $b-W_\varepsilon \leq -T+\| W_\varepsilon \| _\infty <-2$ wherever $u<-T$. Thus (9.67), with $c=-\delta $, gives

\[ \mu _ j(\{ U_ j<W_\varepsilon -\delta \} ) \longrightarrow \mu (\{ u<W_\varepsilon -\delta \} ). \]

The analogous convergence holds for the fixed measure $\nu _\varepsilon $. Indeed, for $s>0$, outside $\{ |U_ j-u|>s\} $, the indicators of $\{ U_ j<W_\varepsilon -\delta \} $ and $\{ u<W_\varepsilon -\delta \} $ can differ only on $\{ |u-W_\varepsilon +\delta |\leq s\} $. The $\nu _\varepsilon $-mass of the first set tends to zero as $j\to \infty $, by the convergence in $L^1(\nu _\varepsilon )$, and that of the second set tends to $\nu _\varepsilon (\{ u-W_\varepsilon =-\delta \} )=0$ as $s\downarrow 0$. Hence

\[ \nu _\varepsilon (\{ U_ j<W_\varepsilon -\delta \} )\longrightarrow \nu _\varepsilon (\{ u<W_\varepsilon -\delta \} ). \]

Step 3. The limit is a bounded solution. Since $U_ j$ and $W_\varepsilon $ have zero boundary values, the difference $U_ j-(W_\varepsilon -\delta )$ has boundary limits $\delta >0$. The comparison theorem (theorem 9.2.8) therefore gives

\[ \nu _\varepsilon (\{ U_ j<W_\varepsilon -\delta \} ) \leq \mu _ j(\{ U_ j<W_\varepsilon -\delta \} ). \]

Let $j\to \infty $ and put $S=\{ u<W_\varepsilon -\delta \} $. By (9.68),

\[ \mu (S)+\varepsilon ^ n\int _ S(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\rho )^ n\leq \nu _\varepsilon (S)\leq \mu (S). \]

Since $\mu (S)$ is finite, $S$ has Lebesgue measure zero. Thus $u\geq W_\varepsilon -\delta $ almost everywhere, and hence everywhere, by the uniqueness assertion of theorem 8.2.1 applied to $\max \{ u,W_\varepsilon -\delta \} $ and $u$. Letting $\delta \downarrow 0$ through admissible values and then $\varepsilon \downarrow 0$, we obtain $W\leq u\leq 0$.

As $W$ has zero boundary values, $u$ is bounded and has zero boundary values. For $T>\| u\| _\infty $ the truncation $\max \{ u,-T\} $ is $u$ itself, so (9.61) gives $\| (\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u)^ n-\mu \| _{\mathrm{TV}}\leq 2E_2T^{-2}$ for all large $T$. Hence $(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u)^ n=\mu $. In particular $(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u)^ n$ has finite mass $m$, and $u\in \mathcal E_0(B)$.

Step 4. Uniqueness and convergence of the whole sequence. Let $v$ be a bounded plurisubharmonic function on $B$ with $(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}v)^ n\geq \mu $ and upper boundary limits at most zero, and put $v_\varepsilon =v+\varepsilon \rho $ for $\varepsilon >0$. As in Step 1, $(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}v_\varepsilon )^ n\geq \mu +\varepsilon ^ n(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\rho )^ n$. Since $u-v_\varepsilon $ has nonnegative lower boundary limits, the comparison theorem, applied to $u$ and $v_\varepsilon $, gives

\[ \mu (\{ u<v_\varepsilon \} ) +\varepsilon ^ n\int _{\{ u<v_\varepsilon \} }(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\rho )^ n \leq \mu (\{ u<v_\varepsilon \} ). \]

The two $\mu $-masses are finite, so the set $\{ u<v_\varepsilon \} $ is Lebesgue null. Therefore $u\geq v_\varepsilon $ everywhere, by the uniqueness assertion of theorem 8.2.1 applied to $\max \{ u,v_\varepsilon \} $ and $u$. Letting $\varepsilon \downarrow 0$ gives $u\geq v$. Since $u$ is itself such a function, it is the pointwise supremum in the statement. Any bounded solution with zero boundary values is such a function, hence is at most $u$; exchanging the roles of the two solutions proves uniqueness. We denote the solution by $U$.

Finally, by proposition 9.10.3, every subsequence of the canonical approximants has a further subsequence converging in $L^1(B)$. By Step 3 and uniqueness, its limit is $U$, and theorem 9.10.5, theorem 9.10.9 give the asserted convergence of the integrals and convergence in capacity along it. If one of the asserted convergences failed for the whole sequence, some subsequence would stay away from the stated limit, and no further subsequence could converge as just described. This contradiction proves all the assertions. $\square$

9.10.4 Mixed energy and truncation

In the next section we approximate several potentials at once by canonical approximants and pass to the limit in their mixed Monge–Ampère products. The approximants converge in capacity and have bounded energies, but we have no uniform bound for them, so theorem 9.4.8 does not apply to them directly. We therefore compare the mixed products with those of the truncated potentials, and for this we need to know that the sets where one of the potentials is very negative carry little mass of a mixed product. This follows from the mixed energy inequality of theorem 9.10.13, which bounds a mixed energy by a constant multiple of the geometric mean of the energies $e_1$ of definition 9.10.1.

Theorem 9.10.13. Let $u_0,\ldots ,u_ n\in \mathcal E_0(B)$. Then

9.69
\begin{equation} \int _ B(-u_0)\, \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_1\wedge \cdots \wedge \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_ n \leq C_ n\prod _{l=0}^ n e_1(u_ l)^{1/(n+1)}, \qquad C_ n=2^{n+2}. \end{equation}

In particular the integral is finite, and it vanishes if one of the energies $e_1(u_ l)$ vanishes.

Proof. Write $B=B(a,R)$ and $\rho (z)=|z-a|^2-R^2$. We say that $v\in \mathcal E_0(B)$ has a quadratic collar if $v=c\rho $ on a neighborhood of $\partial B$ in $B$ for some constant $c>0$. We first prove the inequality with constant one for potentials with quadratic collars (Steps 1 and 2), and then approximate arbitrary elements of $\mathcal E_0(B)$ by such potentials while keeping their energies under control (Steps 3 and 4). For functions $b_0,\ldots ,b_ n$ which either all have quadratic collars, or are smooth real functions on a neighborhood of $\overline B$ with $b_2,\ldots ,b_ n$ plurisubharmonic, put

\[ I(b_0,\ldots ,b_ n) =\int _ B(-b_0)\, \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}b_1\wedge \cdots \wedge \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}b_ n. \]

The integral is finite: in the first case every mixed product of the $b_ l$ is smooth near $\partial B$ and locally finite on the rest of $B$, and in the second the integrand is a smooth form on a neighborhood of $\overline B$.

Step 1. Smooth functions. Let $b_0,\ldots ,b_ n$ be smooth real functions on a neighborhood of $\overline B$, with $b_2,\ldots ,b_ n$ plurisubharmonic and $b_0=0$ on $\partial B$, and put $S=\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}b_2\wedge \cdots \wedge \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}b_ n$, with $S=1$ if $n=1$. With $\mathrm d^{\mathrm c}$ as in (7.6), we claim that

9.70
\begin{equation} I(b_0,b_1,b_2,\ldots ,b_ n) =\int _ B\mathrm db_0\wedge \mathrm d^{\mathrm c}b_1\wedge S. \end{equation}

Fix a smooth function $\theta \colon \mathbb R\to [0,1]$ with $\theta =1$ on $(-\infty ,-2]$ and $\theta =0$ on $[-1,\infty )$, and put $\chi _ k=\theta (k\rho )$ for $k\geq 1$; then $\chi _ k\in C^\infty _ c(B)$. The $(2n-1)$-form $\chi _ kb_0\, \mathrm d^{\mathrm c}b_1\wedge S$ is smooth with compact support in $B$, and $S$ is closed, so proposition 7.1.3 and the Leibniz rule give

\[ 0=\int _ B\chi _ k\, \mathrm db_0\wedge \mathrm d^{\mathrm c}b_1\wedge S +\int _ Bb_0\, \mathrm d\chi _ k\wedge \mathrm d^{\mathrm c}b_1\wedge S +\int _ B\chi _ kb_0\, \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}b_1\wedge S. \]

As $k\to \infty $, $\chi _ k\to 1$ pointwise on $B$, and the forms $\mathrm db_0\wedge \mathrm d^{\mathrm c}b_1\wedge S$ and $b_0\, \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}b_1\wedge S$ have bounded coefficients on $B$; by dominated convergence, the first and the last integral tend to $\int _ B\mathrm db_0\wedge \mathrm d^{\mathrm c}b_1\wedge S$ and $-I(b_0,\ldots ,b_ n)$. In the middle integral, $\mathrm d\chi _ k=k\theta '(k\rho )\, \mathrm d\rho $ vanishes outside the set where $-2\leq k\rho \leq -1$. Let $\Lambda $ be the maximum of $|\nabla b_0|$ on $\overline B$. If $z\in B$ and $\zeta \in \partial B$ is a nearest boundary point, then $b_0(\zeta )=0$ and $|z-\zeta |=R-|z-a|$, so the mean value theorem on the segment from $z$ to $\zeta $ gives

\[ |b_0(z)|\leq \Lambda (R-|z-a|)\leq \frac{\Lambda }{R}(R^2-|z-a|^2)=\frac{\Lambda }{R}|\rho (z)|. \]

Hence $|b_0\, k\theta '(k\rho )|\leq (\Lambda /R)|k\rho |\, |\theta '(k\rho )|\leq 2\Lambda \sup |\theta '|/R$ on $B$, uniformly in $k$, and $b_0\, k\theta '(k\rho )\to 0$ pointwise on $B$. Since $\mathrm d\rho \wedge \mathrm d^{\mathrm c}b_1\wedge S$ has bounded coefficients on $B$, dominated convergence shows that the middle integral tends to zero. This proves (9.70).

As observed at the beginning of section 9.2, $\mathrm db\wedge \mathrm d^{\mathrm c}c\wedge S=\mathrm dc\wedge \mathrm d^{\mathrm c}b\wedge S$ for smooth real $b,c$. Moreover, $\mathrm db\wedge \mathrm d^{\mathrm c}b=(\mathrm i/2\pi )\partial b\wedge \bar\partial b$ is a positive $(1,1)$-form, and so are the forms $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}b_ l$, $l\geq 2$, since the complex Hessian of a smooth plurisubharmonic function is positive semidefinite (proposition 8.2.2). Positive $(1,1)$-forms are strongly positive (section 7.2), so by proposition 7.2.1 the $(n,n)$-form $\mathrm db\wedge \mathrm d^{\mathrm c}b\wedge S$ is a nonnegative multiple of the volume form. Consequently the real bilinear form

\[ Q(b,c)=\int _ B\mathrm db\wedge \mathrm d^{\mathrm c}c\wedge S, \]

defined for smooth real functions $b,c$ on a neighborhood of $\overline B$ vanishing on $\partial B$, is symmetric and positive semidefinite, and by (9.70) we have $Q(b,c)=I(b,c,b_2,\ldots ,b_ n)$. The Cauchy–Schwarz inequality for $Q$, which follows from the nonnegativity of the quadratic polynomial $t\mapsto Q(b+tc,b+tc)$, therefore gives

9.71
\begin{equation} I(b,c,b_2,\ldots ,b_ n)^2 \leq I(b,b,b_2,\ldots ,b_ n)I(c,c,b_2,\ldots ,b_ n). \end{equation}

The symmetry of $Q$ allows us to interchange the first two arguments of $I$, and the symmetry of the products allows us to permute the last $n$ arguments. Hence $I$ is symmetric in all $n+1$ arguments when they are smooth plurisubharmonic functions on a neighborhood of $\overline B$ vanishing on $\partial B$.

Step 2. Quadratic collars. Let $u_0,\ldots ,u_ n$ have quadratic collars, say $u_ l=c_ l\rho $ near $\partial B$. We first show that the assertions of Step 1 persist for them. Extend each $u_ l$ by $c_ l\rho $ to a plurisubharmonic function on a neighborhood of $\overline B$. Let $\vartheta _\varepsilon $ be the radial mollifiers of section 1.4, denoted $\rho _\varepsilon $ there; we change the letter because $\rho $ is now the quadratic function above. Because $\rho $ is a quadratic polynomial and $\vartheta _\varepsilon $ is radial, $\rho *\vartheta _\varepsilon =\rho +\gamma \varepsilon ^2$ with the constant $\gamma =\int |y|^2\vartheta _1(y)\, \mathrm dV(y)$. Hence

\[ u_{l,\varepsilon }=u_ l*\vartheta _\varepsilon -c_ l\gamma \varepsilon ^2 \]

equals $c_ l\rho $ on a fixed neighborhood of $\partial B$ for all small $\varepsilon $. By proposition 8.2.2, the convolutions $u_ l*\vartheta _\varepsilon $ are smooth and plurisubharmonic on a neighborhood of $\overline B$ for small $\varepsilon $, and they decrease to $u_ l$ as $\varepsilon \downarrow 0$. Hence each $u_{l,\varepsilon }$, which differs from $u_ l*\vartheta _\varepsilon $ by a constant, is smooth and plurisubharmonic on a neighborhood of $\overline B$ and vanishes on $\partial B$, although the $u_{l,\varepsilon }$ need not decrease. By theorem 9.2.5,

\[ (-u_0*\vartheta _\varepsilon )\, \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_{1,\varepsilon }\wedge \cdots \wedge \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_{n,\varepsilon } \longrightarrow (-u_0)\, \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_1\wedge \cdots \wedge \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_ n \]

weakly, and the constant $c_0\gamma \varepsilon ^2$ changes the integral against a fixed test function only by an amount tending to zero, by the local mass bounds of theorem 9.1.3. Near $\partial B$, all the $u_{l,\varepsilon }$ and $u_ l$ coincide with $c_ l\rho $. Splitting the integrals over $B$ by a cutoff in $C_ c(B)$ which equals one outside this neighborhood of $\partial B$, we conclude that $I(u_{0,\varepsilon },\ldots ,u_{n,\varepsilon })\to I(u_0,\ldots ,u_ n)$, and likewise for every rearrangement of the potentials. Passing to the limit in the smooth case proves that $I$ is symmetric and satisfies (9.71) for potentials with quadratic collars.

We now prove the inequality with constant one for these potentials. Since $u_ l=c_ l\rho $ near $\partial B$ with $c_ l>0$, the energy $e_1(u_ l)$ is positive: already its integral over a neighborhood of $\partial B$ is positive. Since $I$ is positively homogeneous of degree one in each argument and $e_1(tv)=t^{n+1}e_1(v)$ for $t>0$, both sides of the inequality are multiplied by the same factor when each $u_ l$ is replaced by $e_1(u_ l)^{-1/(n+1)}u_ l$. We may therefore assume that $e_1(u_ l)=1$ for every $l$. Consider the finitely many ordered $(n+1)$-tuples whose entries are taken from the functions $u_0,\ldots ,u_ n$, and let $F$ be the largest value of $I$ on these tuples. Since $I(u_ l,\ldots ,u_ l)=e_1(u_ l)=1$, we have $F\geq 1$. Among the tuples on which $I$ equals $F$, choose one for which the sum of the squares of the multiplicities of its distinct entries is largest.

Suppose that this tuple contains two distinct functions $b$ and $c$, with multiplicities $r$ and $s$. By symmetry we may place them in the first two slots. In (9.71), the left side is $F^2$, while the two factors on the right are nonnegative values of $I$ on tuples of our family, hence at most $F$. Therefore both factors equal $F$. Replacing one entry $c$ by $b$ changes the sum of the squares of the multiplicities by $(r+1)^2+(s-1)^2-r^2-s^2=2(r-s)+2$, and replacing one entry $b$ by $c$ changes it by $2(s-r)+2$. One of these numbers is positive, which contradicts the choice of the tuple. Hence the chosen tuple consists of a single function repeated $n+1$ times, and $F=1$. In particular $I(u_0,\ldots ,u_ n)\leq 1$, which is the inequality with constant one.

Step 3. Approximation with controlled energy. Let $u\in \mathcal E_0(B)$, put $m_\rho =\int _ B(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\rho )^ n$, and for $j\geq 1$ let

\[ \delta _ j=j^{-n-1},\qquad v_ j=\max \{ u-\delta _ j,j\rho \} . \]

Since $u-\delta _ j\leq -\delta _ j$ while $j\rho >-\delta _ j$ near $\partial B$, the function $v_ j$ equals $j\rho $ on a neighborhood of $\partial B$ in $B$. Since $v_ j$ and $j\rho $ coincide outside a compact subset of $B$, lemma 9.10.6 gives $\int _ B(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}v_ j)^ n=j^ nm_\rho $, and hence $v_ j\in \mathcal E_0(B)$. Moreover, $v_ j\to u$ uniformly on $B$. Indeed, let $s>0$, and choose a neighborhood $V$ of $\partial B$ with $u>-s$ on $V\cap B$. Since $u-\delta _ j\leq v_ j\leq 0$, we have $|v_ j-u|\leq s+\delta _ j$ on $V\cap B$. On the set $B\setminus V$, whose closure is a compact subset of $B$, the function $\rho $ is bounded away from zero and $u$ is bounded, so $j\rho <u-\delta _ j$, and hence $v_ j=u-\delta _ j$, there for all large $j$.

Next we estimate $e_1(v_ j)$. Let $t>4\delta _ j$. Since $u\leq v_ j+\delta _ j$ and $v_ j\leq 0$,

\[ \{ v_ j<-t\} \subset \{ u<v_ j/2-t/4\} \subset \{ u<-t/4\} ; \]

for the first inclusion note that on $\{ v_ j<-t\} $ we have $v_ j/2<-t/2<-t/4-\delta _ j$, hence $u\leq v_ j+\delta _ j<v_ j/2-t/4$. As $u$ tends to $0$ and $v_ j/2-t/4$ tends to $-t/4$ at $\partial B$, the comparison theorem (theorem 9.2.8) applies to $u$ and $v_ j/2-t/4$ and gives

\[ \int _{\{ v_ j<-t\} }(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}v_ j)^ n \leq 2^ n\int _{\{ u<v_ j/2-t/4\} }\bigl (\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}(v_ j/2)\bigr )^ n \leq 2^ n\int _{\{ u<-t/4\} }(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u)^ n. \]

For $0<t\leq 4\delta _ j$ we use the total mass $j^ nm_\rho $ instead. Integrating over $t$ by the layer-cake formula $e_1(v)=\int _0^\infty (\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}v)^ n(\{ v<-t\} )\, \mathrm dt$, we obtain

9.72
\begin{equation} e_1(v_ j)\leq 4\delta _ j j^ n m_\rho +2^{n+2}e_1(u) =\frac{4m_\rho }j+C_ ne_1(u). \end{equation}

Step 4. The general case. Apply the construction of Step 3 to each $u_ l$, obtaining functions $v_{l,j}\in \mathcal E_0(B)$ which equal $j\rho $ near $\partial B$. They are uniformly bounded, since $u_ l-1\leq v_{l,j}\leq 0$, and they converge uniformly, hence locally in capacity, to $u_ l$. Let $\chi \in C_ c(B)$ with $0\leq \chi \leq 1$. The weight $-u_0$ is bounded and quasi-continuous (theorem 9.4.2), so the weighted form of theorem 9.4.8 and Step 2 give

\[ \begin{aligned} \int _ B\chi (-u_0)\, \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_1\wedge \cdots \wedge \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_ n & =\lim _{j\to \infty }\int _ B\chi (-v_{0,j})\, \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}v_{1,j}\wedge \cdots \wedge \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}v_{n,j}\\ & \leq \varlimsup _{j\to \infty }\prod _{l=0}^ ne_1(v_{l,j})^{1/(n+1)}. \end{aligned} \]

By (9.72), the right side is at most

\[ \lim _{j\to \infty }\prod _{l=0}^ n\left(\frac{4m_\rho }j+C_ ne_1(u_ l)\right)^{1/(n+1)} =C_ n\prod _{l=0}^ ne_1(u_ l)^{1/(n+1)}. \]

Letting $\chi $ increase to one proves (9.69). Vanishing energies need no separate treatment, since the energies of the $v_{l,j}$ are positive. $\square$

Corollary 9.10.14. Let $E\geq 0$, and for $1\leq l\leq n$ and $j\geq 1$ let $u_{l,j}\in \mathcal E_0(B)$ with $e_1(u_{l,j})\leq E$. Suppose that, for each $l$, $u_{l,j}\to u_ l$ locally in capacity as $j\to \infty $, where $u_ l$ is a locally bounded plurisubharmonic function on $B$. Then

\[ \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_{1,j}\wedge \cdots \wedge \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_{n,j} \longrightarrow \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_1\wedge \cdots \wedge \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_ n \]

weakly on $B$.

Proof. We compare the products with those of the truncations at a level $-T$: the latter converge by theorem 9.4.8, and by theorem 9.10.13 the two differ by a term of order $1/T$. For $T>0$ put $a_{l,j}=\max \{ u_{l,j},-T\} $, and write

\[ R_ j=\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_{1,j}\wedge \cdots \wedge \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_{n,j},\qquad R_{j,T}=\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}a_{1,j}\wedge \cdots \wedge \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}a_{n,j}. \]

By lemma 9.10.7, $a_{l,j}\in \mathcal E_0(B)$ and $e_1(a_{l,j})\leq E$. For each $l$, theorem 9.10.13 with $u_0=u_{l,j}$, respectively $u_0=a_{l,j}$, gives

\[ \int _ B(-u_{l,j})\, R_ j\leq C_ nE,\qquad \int _ B(-a_{l,j})\, R_{j,T}\leq C_ nE. \]

Let $D_{j,T}=\bigcup _{l=1}^ n\{ u_{l,j}\leq -T\} $. On $\{ u_{l,j}\leq -T\} $ both $-u_{l,j}$ and $-a_{l,j}$ are at least $T$, so Chebyshev's inequality gives

\[ R_ j(D_{j,T})\leq nC_ nE/T,\qquad R_{j,T}(D_{j,T})\leq nC_ nE/T. \]

On the complement of $D_{j,T}$, every $u_{l,j}$ is greater than $-T$. For $0\leq k\leq n$ put

\[ P_ k=\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}a_{1,j}\wedge \cdots \wedge \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}a_{k,j}\wedge \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_{k+1,j}\wedge \cdots \wedge \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_{n,j}, \]

so that $P_0=R_ j$ and $P_ n=R_{j,T}$. By the symmetry of the products, $P_ k=\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}a_{k,j}\wedge \Theta _ k$ and $P_{k-1}=\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_{k,j}\wedge \Theta _ k$ with the same closed positive current $\Theta _ k$ formed by the other $n-1$ factors, and maximum locality (proposition 9.2.7 with $p=1$, $u=u_{k,j}$, $v=-T$ and base current $\Theta _ k$) gives $P_ k=P_{k-1}$ on $\{ u_{k,j}>-T\} $. Hence $R_ j=R_{j,T}$ on the complement of $D_{j,T}$, and for every $\chi \in C_ c(B)$,

\[ \left|\int _ B\chi \, (R_ j-R_{j,T})\right| \leq 2nC_ nE\| \chi \| _\infty /T. \]

Now fix $\chi \in C_ c(B)$, choose an open set $B'\Subset B$ containing $\operatorname{Supp}\chi $, and let $T>\max _ l\sup _{B'}|u_ l|$. By (9.53), the uniformly bounded functions $a_{l,j}$ converge to the locally bounded plurisubharmonic function $\max \{ u_ l,-T\} $ locally in capacity, and theorem 9.4.8 shows that $R_{j,T}\to \bigwedge _ l\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\max \{ u_ l,-T\} $ weakly on $B$. On $B'$ we have $\max \{ u_ l,-T\} =u_ l$, so by locality (proposition 9.1.2) this limit equals $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_1\wedge \cdots \wedge \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_ n$ there. Consequently

\[ \varlimsup _{j\to \infty }\left|\int _ B\chi \, R_ j-\int _ B\chi \, \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_1\wedge \cdots \wedge \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_ n\right|\leq 2nC_ nE\| \chi \| _\infty /T \]

for all large $T$, and letting $T\to \infty $ completes the proof. $\square$

9.11 Mixed products for a common measure

Corollary 9.7.6 bounds a mixed Monge–Ampère product from below when the pure Monge–Ampère measures of the factors have continuous lower densities with respect to Lebesgue measure. For non-pluripolar products in chapter 15 we need the same inequality relative to an arbitrary positive Radon measure $\mu $, which may be singular with respect to Lebesgue measure. Instead of regularizing the potentials by convolution, as in the proof of that corollary, we restrict each pure Monge–Ampère measure to a compact set and replace it by its cube averages, as in proposition 9.10.3. The averaged measures have piecewise constant densities, so the case of Lebesgue measure applies on each cube, and Hölder's inequality compares the resulting bound with the cube averages of the expected lower bound. The convergence results of section 9.10 then allow us to pass to the limit.

Theorem 9.11.1. Let $\Omega \subset \mathbb C^ n$ be open, $n\geq 1$, and let $u_1,\ldots ,u_ n$ be locally bounded plurisubharmonic functions on $\Omega $. Let $\mu $ be a positive Radon measure on $\Omega $, and let $f_1,\ldots ,f_ n$ be nonnegative Borel functions on $\Omega $. If

\[ (\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_ i)^ n\geq f_ i\mu \qquad (1\leq i\leq n), \]

then

9.75
\begin{equation} \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_1\wedge \cdots \wedge \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_ n \geq \left(\prod _{i=1}^ nf_ i^{1/n}\right)\mu . \end{equation}

For $n=1$ the assertion is the hypothesis.

Proof. Let $n\geq 2$, and put $\lambda =(\prod _ if_ i^{1/n})\mu $. We first prove the theorem when $\Omega $ is a ball $B=B(a_0,R)$ and all $u_ i$ belong to $\mathcal E_0(B)$ (Steps 1 and 2), and then reduce the general case to this one by modifying the potentials near the boundary of a ball (Step 3). For a ball $B=B(a_0,R)$ we write $\rho (z)=|z-a_0|^2-R^2$.

We use the generalized Hölder inequality

9.76
\begin{equation} \int _ A\prod _{i=1}^ ng_ i^{1/n}\, \mathrm d\nu \leq \prod _{i=1}^ n\Bigl (\int _ Ag_ i\, \mathrm d\nu \Bigr )^{1/n} \end{equation}

for a positive measure $\nu $, a measurable set $A$ and nonnegative measurable functions $g_ i$. If one of the integrals $c_ i=\int _ Ag_ i\, \mathrm d\nu $ vanishes, then $g_ i=0$ $\nu $-almost everywhere on $A$ and both sides vanish, with the convention $0\cdot \infty =0$; if none vanishes and one is infinite, the right side is infinite. Otherwise, integrating the arithmetic–geometric mean inequality $\prod _ i(g_ i/c_ i)^{1/n}\leq \frac1n\sum _ ig_ i/c_ i$ over $A$ gives $\int _ A\prod _ i(g_ i/c_ i)^{1/n}\, \mathrm d\nu \leq 1$, which is (9.76). For every compact $K\subset \Omega $ we have $\int _ Kf_ i\, \mathrm d\mu \leq (\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_ i)^ n(K)<\infty $ by hypothesis, so (9.76) shows that $\lambda $ is a positive Radon measure.

Step 1. Exhaustion by compactly supported measures. Choose closed balls $K_ l\subset B$ concentric with $B$, increasing with $l$ and exhausting $B$, and set

\[ \alpha _{i,l}=\mathbf1_{K_ l}(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_ i)^ n. \]

These measures are finite since $u_ i\in \mathcal E_0(B)$, they have compact support in $B$, and they are dominated by the Monge–Ampère measure of the bounded plurisubharmonic function $u_ i$. By theorem 9.10.12, the equation $(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}A)^ n=\alpha _{i,l}$ has a bounded solution $A_{i,l}\in \mathcal E_0(B)$ with zero boundary values, and $A_{i,l}$ is the largest bounded plurisubharmonic function $v$ on $B$ with $(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}v)^ n\geq \alpha _{i,l}$ and upper boundary limits at most zero. The function $u_ i$ has these properties for every $l$, and so has $A_{i,l+1}$, because $\alpha _{i,l+1}\geq \alpha _{i,l}$. Therefore

\[ u_ i\leq A_{i,l+1}\leq A_{i,l}\leq 0. \]

We claim that $A_{i,l}\downarrow u_ i$ as $l\to \infty $. The decreasing limit $A_{i,\infty }$ satisfies $u_ i\leq A_{i,\infty }\leq 0$, so it is a bounded plurisubharmonic function (theorem 8.3.1) with zero boundary values. By theorem 9.2.5, $(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}A_{i,\infty })^ n$ is the weak limit of $\alpha _{i,l}$, that is, $(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}A_{i,\infty })^ n=(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_ i)^ n$, and this measure has finite total mass. Let $\varepsilon >0$ and $S=\{ u_ i<A_{i,\infty }+\varepsilon \rho \} $. Since $u_ i-(A_{i,\infty }+\varepsilon \rho )$ tends to zero at $\partial B$, the comparison theorem (theorem 9.2.8) applies. Expanding $(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}(A_{i,\infty }+\varepsilon \rho ))^ n$ and dropping the mixed terms, which are positive, we obtain

\[ \int _ S(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_ i)^ n+\varepsilon ^ n\int _ S(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\rho )^ n \leq \int _ S\bigl (\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}(A_{i,\infty }+\varepsilon \rho )\bigr )^ n \leq \int _ S(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_ i)^ n. \]

The integrals of $(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_ i)^ n$ are finite, so $S$ is Lebesgue null. Hence $u_ i\geq A_{i,\infty }+\varepsilon \rho $ almost everywhere, and therefore everywhere on $B$, by the uniqueness assertion of theorem 8.2.1 applied to $\max \{ u_ i,A_{i,\infty }+\varepsilon \rho \} $ and $u_ i$. Letting $\varepsilon \downarrow 0$ gives $A_{i,\infty }=u_ i$, as claimed.

Step 2. A common grid. Fix $l$. Choose a sequence of partitions of $\mathbb C^ n=\mathbb R^{2n}$ into half-open cubes whose side lengths decrease to zero, the same for all $i$. Let $\alpha _{i,l,j}$ be the corresponding cube averages of $\alpha _{i,l}$, as in proposition 9.10.3, and let $A_{i,l,j}\in \mathcal E_0(B)$ be the corresponding canonical approximants of $\alpha _{i,l}$, that is, the zero-boundary solutions of $(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}A_{i,l,j})^ n=\alpha _{i,l,j}$; they are defined for all large $j$. Discarding finitely many indices $j$, we may assume that they are defined for all $j$ and all $i$, and that every cube meeting $K_ l$ has its closure in $B$. By theorem 9.10.12, $A_{i,l,j}\to A_{i,l}$ locally in capacity as $j\to \infty $. By proposition 9.10.3, applied with the bounded subsolution $u_ i$, the energies $e_1(A_{i,l,j})$ are bounded uniformly in $j$; since there are only finitely many indices $i$, they have a common bound. (If $\alpha _{i,l}=0$, all these solutions vanish.) Therefore corollary 9.10.14 gives

9.77
\begin{equation} \bigwedge _{i=1}^ n\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}A_{i,l,j} \longrightarrow \bigwedge _{i=1}^ n\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}A_{i,l} \end{equation}

weakly on $B$.

Let $\lambda _ l=\mathbf1_{K_ l}\lambda $, a finite measure, and let $\lambda _{l,j}$ be its cube averages. For every cube $Q$, the hypothesis gives $\alpha _{i,l}(Q)\geq \int _{Q\cap K_ l}f_ i\, \mathrm d\mu $, and (9.76) then gives

9.78
\begin{equation} \prod _{i=1}^ n\left(\frac{\alpha _{i,l}(Q)}{|Q|}\right)^{1/n} \geq \frac1{|Q|}\int _{Q\cap K_ l}\prod _{i=1}^ nf_ i^{1/n}\, \mathrm d\mu =\frac{\lambda _ l(Q)}{|Q|}. \end{equation}

Let $Q$ be a cube with $\lambda _ l(Q)>0$; it meets $K_ l$, so its interior lies in $B$. There the measures $(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}A_{i,l,j})^ n=\alpha _{i,l,j}$ have the constant densities $\alpha _{i,l}(Q)/|Q|$ with respect to Lebesgue measure $\mathrm dV$. Recall that $\eta ^ n=c_ n\, \mathrm dV$ with $\eta =\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}|z|^2$ and $c_ n=n!\pi ^{-n}$, by (9.2). Applying corollary 9.7.6 on the open set $\operatorname{Int}Q$ to the constant densities $c_ n^{-1}\alpha _{i,l}(Q)/|Q|$ with respect to $\eta ^ n$, and using (9.78), we obtain on $\operatorname{Int}Q$

\[ \bigwedge _{i=1}^ n\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}A_{i,l,j} \geq \prod _{i=1}^ n\left(\frac{\alpha _{i,l}(Q)}{c_ n|Q|}\right)^{1/n}\eta ^ n =\prod _{i=1}^ n\left(\frac{\alpha _{i,l}(Q)}{|Q|}\right)^{1/n}\mathrm dV \geq \lambda _{l,j}. \]

The factor $c_ n$ cancels because the $n$ exponents $1/n$ sum to one. The measure $\lambda _{l,j}$ vanishes on the cubes with $\lambda _ l(Q)=0$ and does not charge the cube boundaries, which are Lebesgue null. Since the mixed product is positive, we conclude that $\bigwedge _ i\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}A_{i,l,j}\geq \lambda _{l,j}$ on $B$.

As $j\to \infty $, $\lambda _{l,j}\to \lambda _ l$ weakly. Indeed, for $\chi \in C_ c(B)$, replacing the values of $\chi $ by its cube averages changes $\int \chi \, \mathrm d\lambda _ l$ by at most $\lambda _ l(B)$ times the modulus of continuity of $\chi $ at the cube diameter. Letting $j\to \infty $ and using (9.77), we obtain $\bigwedge _ i\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}A_{i,l}\geq \lambda _ l$. Finally let $l\to \infty $. By Step 1, $A_{i,l}\downarrow u_ i$ with $u_ i\leq A_{i,l}\leq 0$, so theorem 9.2.5 gives $\bigwedge _ i\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}A_{i,l}\to \bigwedge _ i\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_ i$ weakly, while $\lambda _ l$ increases to $\lambda $. Testing with nonnegative functions in $C_ c(B)$ and using monotone convergence on the right, we obtain (9.75) for potentials in $\mathcal E_0(B)$.

Step 3. Localization. Now let $u_1,\ldots ,u_ n$ be locally bounded on $\Omega $. Choose a ball $B=B(a_0,R)$ with $\overline B\subset \Omega $ and a compact set $K\subset B$. Since adding constants does not change the products, we may assume that $u_ i\leq -1$ on $B$. With constants $C_ i>0$ so large that $C_ i\rho <\inf _ Bu_ i$ on a neighborhood of $K$, put

\[ \widetilde u_ i=\max \{ u_ i,C_ i\rho \} . \]

Then $\widetilde u_ i=u_ i$ on a neighborhood of $K$, and $\widetilde u_ i=C_ i\rho $ near $\partial B$, because $C_ i\rho \to 0$ at $\partial B$ while $u_ i\leq -1$. Thus $\widetilde u_ i\in \mathcal E_0(B)$: its Monge–Ampère measure is smooth near $\partial B$ and locally finite on the rest of $B$. By locality of the products on the open neighborhood of $K$ where $\widetilde u_ i=u_ i$, we have $(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\widetilde u_ i)^ n\geq f_ i\mathbf1_ K\mu $ on $B$. The case already proved, applied to the measure $\mathbf1_ K\mu $, gives $\bigwedge _ i\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\widetilde u_ i\geq \mathbf1_ K\lambda $, and on a neighborhood of $K$ the left side equals $\bigwedge _ i\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_ i$ by locality. Thus $P=\bigwedge _ i\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_ i$ satisfies $P(E)\geq \lambda (E)$ for every Borel set $E\subset K$. The interiors of such compact sets $K$ cover $\Omega $, and since $\Omega $ has a countable basis, countably many of them, say $\operatorname{Int}L_1,\operatorname{Int}L_2,\ldots $, already cover $\Omega $. A Borel set $E\subset \Omega $ is the disjoint union of the Borel sets $E_ m=(E\cap L_ m)\setminus \bigcup _{m'<m}L_{m'}$, and therefore $P(E)=\sum _ mP(E_ m)\geq \sum _ m\lambda (E_ m)=\lambda (E)$. $\square$

Corollary 9.11.2. Let $u,v$ be locally bounded plurisubharmonic functions on an open set $\Omega \subset \mathbb C^ n$, $n\geq 1$, and let $\mu $ be a positive Radon measure on $\Omega $. If $f,g$ are nonnegative Borel functions on $\Omega $ and

\[ (\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u)^ n\geq f\mu ,\qquad (\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}v)^ n\geq g\mu , \]

then, for each integer $1\leq k\leq n-1$,

9.79
\begin{equation} (\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u)^ k\wedge (\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}v)^{n-k} \geq f^{k/n}g^{(n-k)/n}\mu . \end{equation}

For $k=0$ and $k=n$ the corresponding inequalities are the hypotheses; for $n=1$ only these occur.

Proof. Apply theorem 9.11.1 to $k$ copies of $u$ with density $f$ and $n-k$ copies of $v$ with density $g$. $\square$

Corollary 9.11.3. Under the hypotheses of corollary 9.11.2,

\[ (\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}(u+v))^ n\geq (f^{1/n}+g^{1/n})^ n\mu . \]

Proof. By additivity and symmetry of the products, $(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}(u+v))^ n=\sum _{k=0}^ n\binom nk(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u)^ k\wedge (\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}v)^{n-k}$. Bound the terms with $1\leq k\leq n-1$ by corollary 9.11.2 and the terms with $k=0,n$ by the hypotheses; the binomial formula then gives the assertion. The density on the right is locally $\mu $-integrable, since $f^{k/n}g^{(n-k)/n}\leq f+g$ by the weighted arithmetic–geometric mean inequality, and $f$ and $g$ are locally $\mu $-integrable because $f\mu \leq (\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u)^ n$ and $g\mu \leq (\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}v)^ n$. $\square$

9.12 Bounded complex Hessians and contact sets

Monge–Ampère measures of envelopes are computed on contact sets: if a potential $u$ lies below an obstacle $f$ and $u=f$ on a set $E$, one expects the Monge–Ampère measure of $u$ on $E$ to be determined by the Hessian of $f$. A contact set may have empty interior, and then the locality of the products on open sets (proposition 9.1.2) says nothing about it. We show that weak derivatives are local on arbitrary measurable sets: two functions whose weak derivatives up to order two are locally integrable have the same such derivatives almost everywhere on the set where they agree. For potentials with bounded complex Hessians this yields a formula for the Bedford–Taylor product restricted to a contact set (proposition 9.12.4), which is used for envelopes in chapter 16.

We use the Sobolev spaces $W^{k,p}_{\mathrm{loc}}(D)$ of section 6.2, for open sets $D\subset \mathbb R^ d$, integers $k\ge 0$ and $p\in \{ 1,2\} $; thus a function belongs to $W^{k,p}_{\mathrm{loc}}(D)$ if it and its distributional derivatives of order at most $k$ belong to $L^ p_{\mathrm{loc}}(D)$. Since $L^2_{\mathrm{loc}}(D)\subset L^1_{\mathrm{loc}}(D)$, we have $W^{k,2}_{\mathrm{loc}}(D)\subset W^{k,1}_{\mathrm{loc}}(D)$.

Lemma 9.12.1. Let $D\subset \mathbb R^ d$ be open and let $g\in W^{1,1}_{\mathrm{loc}}(D)$ be real. Then $\nabla g=0$ almost everywhere on $\{ g=0\} $. If moreover $g\in W^{2,1}_{\mathrm{loc}}(D)$, then all first and second weak derivatives of $g$ vanish almost everywhere on $\{ g=0\} $. Here $\{ g=0\} $ may be formed with any measurable representative of $g$, since changing $g$ on a null set changes this set only by a null set.

Proof. We first prove the chain rule

\[ \partial _ a\Phi (g)=\Phi '(g)\, \partial _ a g\qquad (1\le a\le d) \]

for a $C^1$ function $\Phi \colon \mathbb R\to \mathbb R$ with bounded derivative; note that $\Phi (g)\in L^1_{\mathrm{loc}}(D)$, since $|\Phi (g)|\leq |\Phi (0)|+\| \Phi '\| _\infty |g|$. Let $V\Subset D$ be open. The mollifications $g_\varepsilon =g*\rho _\varepsilon $ converge to $g$ in $L^1(V)$, together with their first derivatives $\partial _ ag_\varepsilon =(\partial _ ag)*\rho _\varepsilon $, by the remark preceding lemma 6.2.4; passing to a sequence $\varepsilon _ k\to 0$, we may also assume that $g_{\varepsilon _ k}\to g$ almost everywhere on $V$. Since $\Phi $ is Lipschitz, $\Phi (g_{\varepsilon _ k})\to \Phi (g)$ in $L^1(V)$. Moreover,

\[ \Phi '(g_{\varepsilon _ k})\partial _ ag_{\varepsilon _ k}-\Phi '(g)\partial _ ag =\Phi '(g_{\varepsilon _ k})(\partial _ ag_{\varepsilon _ k}-\partial _ ag) +\bigl (\Phi '(g_{\varepsilon _ k})-\Phi '(g)\bigr )\partial _ ag \]

tends to zero in $L^1(V)$: the first term is bounded by $\| \Phi '\| _\infty |\partial _ ag_{\varepsilon _ k}-\partial _ ag|$, and the second tends to zero by dominated convergence, since it tends to zero almost everywhere, $\Phi '$ being continuous, and is bounded by $2\| \Phi '\| _\infty |\partial _ ag|$. For $\psi \in C^\infty _ c(V)$ the classical chain rule gives $\int \Phi (g_{\varepsilon _ k})\partial _ a\psi =-\int \Phi '(g_{\varepsilon _ k})\partial _ ag_{\varepsilon _ k}\, \psi $, and letting $k\to \infty $ we obtain $\int \Phi (g)\partial _ a\psi =-\int \Phi '(g)\partial _ ag\, \psi $. As $V$ was arbitrary, this proves the chain rule.

Choose a smooth function $\chi $ on $\mathbb R$ supported in $(-2,2)$, with $0\le \chi \le 1$ and $\chi =1$ on $[-1,1]$, and put $\Phi _\varepsilon (t)=\int _0^ t\chi (s/\varepsilon )\, \mathrm ds$ for $\varepsilon >0$. Then $\Phi _\varepsilon $ is smooth with $0\leq \Phi _\varepsilon '\leq 1$, $|\Phi _\varepsilon |\le 2\varepsilon $, and by the chain rule

\[ \nabla \Phi _\varepsilon (g)=\chi (g/\varepsilon )\nabla g \longrightarrow \mathbf1_{\{ g=0\} }\nabla g \quad \text{in }L^1_{\mathrm{loc}}(D) \]

as $\varepsilon \to 0$, by dominated convergence, since $\chi (g/\varepsilon )\to \mathbf1_{\{ g=0\} }$ pointwise and $|\chi (g/\varepsilon )\nabla g|\leq |\nabla g|$. On the other hand, $\Phi _\varepsilon (g)\to 0$ uniformly, so $\nabla \Phi _\varepsilon (g)\to 0$ in the sense of distributions. Since convergence in $L^1_{\mathrm{loc}}(D)$ implies convergence in the sense of distributions, the locally integrable function $\mathbf1_{\{ g=0\} }\nabla g$ defines the zero distribution, and hence vanishes almost everywhere (proposition 1.4.3). This is the first assertion.

If $g\in W^{2,1}_{\mathrm{loc}}(D)$, then each $\partial _ ag$ belongs to $W^{1,1}_{\mathrm{loc}}(D)$ and, by the first assertion, vanishes almost everywhere on $\{ g=0\} $. Applying the first assertion to $\partial _ ag$, formed with any measurable representative, shows that $\nabla \partial _ ag=0$ almost everywhere on $\{ \partial _ ag=0\} $, a set which contains $\{ g=0\} $ up to a null set. Since there are finitely many derivatives, the union of the exceptional null sets is null. $\square$

Lemma 9.12.2. Let $D\subset \mathbb R^ d$ be open, and let $f\in L^2_{\mathrm{loc}}(D)$. If the distributional Laplacian $\Delta f$ belongs to $L^2_{\mathrm{loc}}(D)$, then $f\in W^{2,2}_{\mathrm{loc}}(D)$. In particular, this holds if $f$ is continuous and $\Delta f$ is a locally bounded measurable function.

Proof. Considering real and imaginary parts separately, we may assume that $f$ is real. Let $f_\varepsilon =f*\rho _\varepsilon $ be the mollifications of $f$. For every compact set $L\subset D$ they are smooth on a neighborhood of $L$ for small $\varepsilon $, and by proposition 1.4.3 and lemma 1.4.1, $f_\varepsilon \to f$ and $\Delta f_\varepsilon =(\Delta f)*\rho _\varepsilon \to \Delta f$ in $L^2(L)$ as $\varepsilon \to 0$.

We first show that the gradients converge. Let $\chi $ be a real test function, and let $h$ be a real smooth function on a neighborhood of $\operatorname{Supp}\chi $. Since $\int \partial _ a(\chi ^2h\, \partial _ ah)=0$ for each $a$, by Fubini's theorem and the fundamental theorem of calculus, we have

\[ \begin{aligned} \int \chi ^2|\nabla h|^2& =-\int \chi ^2h\Delta h-2\int \chi h\nabla \chi \cdot \nabla h\\ & \le \tfrac 12\int \chi ^2|\nabla h|^2 +\int \chi ^2|h\Delta h|+2\int h^2|\nabla \chi |^2, \end{aligned} \]

where we used $2xy\le x^2/2+2y^2$. Hence

\[ \int \chi ^2|\nabla h|^2\leq 2\| \chi \| _\infty ^2\| h\| _{L^2(S)}\| \Delta h\| _{L^2(S)}+4\| \nabla \chi \| _\infty ^2\| h\| _{L^2(S)}^2, \qquad S=\operatorname{Supp}\chi . \]

Let $L\subset D$ be compact, and choose $\chi \in C^\infty _ c(D)$ equal to one on $L$ (lemma 1.4.1). Apply the last inequality to $h=f_\varepsilon -f_\delta $. As $\varepsilon ,\delta \to 0$, the norms $\| f_\varepsilon -f_\delta \| _{L^2(\operatorname{Supp}\chi )}$ tend to zero, while the norms $\| \Delta f_\varepsilon -\Delta f_\delta \| _{L^2(\operatorname{Supp}\chi )}$ stay bounded. Hence the gradients $\nabla f_\varepsilon $ form a Cauchy family in $L^2(L)$. For $\psi \in C^\infty _ c(\operatorname{Int}L)$ we have $\int \partial _ af_\varepsilon \, \psi =-\int f_\varepsilon \, \partial _ a\psi \to -\int f\, \partial _ a\psi $, so the $L^2$ limits are the distributional first derivatives of $f$ on $\operatorname{Int}L$. Since $L$ was arbitrary, the first derivatives of $f$ belong to $L^2_{\mathrm{loc}}(D)$, and $\nabla f_\varepsilon \to \nabla f$ in $L^2(L)$ for every compact $L\subset D$.

For the second derivatives, let $L\subset D$ be compact, choose $\chi \in C^\infty _ c(D)$ equal to one on a neighborhood $N$ of $L$, and for small $\varepsilon $ put $v_\varepsilon =\chi f_\varepsilon $, extended by zero to $\mathbb R^ d$. These are smooth functions with compact support, and

\[ \Delta v_\varepsilon =\chi \Delta f_\varepsilon +2\nabla \chi \cdot \nabla f_\varepsilon +f_\varepsilon \Delta \chi \]

is a Cauchy family in $L^2(\mathbb R^ d)$, by the first part applied on $\operatorname{Supp}\chi $. For every real $v\in C^\infty _ c(\mathbb R^ d)$, integrating by parts twice gives

\[ \sum _{a,b=1}^ d\| \partial _ a\partial _ b v\| _2^2 =\sum _{a,b=1}^ d\int (\partial _ a^2v)(\partial _ b^2v) =\| \Delta v\| _2^2. \]

Applying this identity to $v_\varepsilon -v_\delta $, we see that all second derivatives of $v_\varepsilon $ form Cauchy families in $L^2(\mathbb R^ d)$. Testing against compactly supported smooth functions identifies their limits with the distributional second derivatives of $\chi f$, which agree with those of $f$ on $N$, where $\chi f=f$. Thus the second derivatives of $f$ are square-integrable on $L$. Since $L$ was arbitrary, $f\in W^{2,2}_{\mathrm{loc}}(D)$. The last assertion holds because continuous functions and locally bounded measurable functions are locally square-integrable. $\square$

Let $\Omega \subset \mathbb C^ n$ be open. We say that a continuous function $f$ on $\Omega $ has a bounded complex Hessian if its distributional derivatives $f_{j\bar k}=\partial ^2 f/\partial z_ j\partial \bar z_ k$ are locally bounded measurable functions. Since the Laplacian of $\mathbb C^ n=\mathbb R^{2n}$ is $4\sum _ j\partial ^2/\partial z_ j\partial \bar z_ j$ by (7.5), such a function belongs to $W^{2,2}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\Omega )$ by lemma 9.12.2.

Proposition 9.12.3. Let $f,g\in W^{2,1}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\Omega )$ be real, and put $E=\{ f=g\} $, formed with any measurable representatives of $f$ and $g$. Then the real weak first and second derivatives of $f$ and $g$ agree almost everywhere on $E$. In particular,

\[ \mathbf1_ E f_{j\bar k}=\mathbf1_ E g_{j\bar k} \quad \text{almost everywhere, for all }j,k. \]

Proof. Apply lemma 9.12.1 to $f-g\in W^{2,1}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\Omega )$, whose zero set, formed with the chosen representatives, is $E$. The complex derivatives $f_{j\bar k}$ and $g_{j\bar k}$ are fixed linear combinations of the real second derivatives, which gives the second assertion. $\square$

As in section 9.7.1, $D(A_1,\dots ,A_ n)$ denotes the mixed discriminant of Hermitian $n\times n$ matrices (section 7.2); in dimension zero we put $D()=1$.

Proposition 9.12.4. Let $u_1,\dots ,u_ n$ be continuous plurisubharmonic functions with bounded complex Hessians on $\Omega $, and let $A_ j=((u_ j)_{a\bar b})$ be their complex Hessian matrices. Then

\[ \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_1\wedge \cdots \wedge \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_ n =\frac{n!}{\pi ^ n}D(A_1,\dots ,A_ n)\, \mathrm dV_{2n}. \]

If $v_1,\dots ,v_ n\in W^{2,1}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\Omega )$ are real, with weak complex Hessian matrices $B_ j=((v_ j)_{a\bar b})$, and $E=\bigcap _ j\{ u_ j=v_ j\} $, formed with any Borel representatives of the $v_ j$, then

\[ \mathbf1_ E\, \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_1\wedge \cdots \wedge \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_ n =\frac{n!}{\pi ^ n}\mathbf1_ E D(B_1,\dots ,B_ n)\, \mathrm dV_{2n}. \]

In particular, the density $\mathbf1_ ED(B_1,\dots ,B_ n)$ is locally bounded. (In dimension zero, where $\mathbb C^0$ is a point and $\mathrm dV_0$ is the unit point mass, both sides of each identity equal $\mathrm dV_0$.)

Proof. For smooth $u_ j$ we polarize (9.2), as in the proof of corollary 9.7.6. Applied to the smooth function $\sum _ jt_ ju_ j$ with $t_1,\ldots ,t_ n\in \mathbb R$, it gives the identity

\[ \Bigl (\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\sum _ jt_ ju_ j\Bigr )^ n=\frac{n!}{\pi ^ n}\det \Bigl (\sum _ jt_ jA_ j\Bigr )\, \mathrm dV_{2n} \]

between exterior powers of smooth forms, and both sides are polynomials in $t_1,\ldots ,t_ n$. We compare the coefficients of $t_1\cdots t_ n$. Since $2$-forms commute, on the left this coefficient is $n!$ times the exterior product $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_1\wedge \cdots \wedge \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_ n$, which is the Bedford–Taylor product by proposition 9.1.2(1); on the right it is $(n!)^2\pi ^{-n}D(A_1,\dots ,A_ n)\, \mathrm dV_{2n}$, by the definition of the mixed discriminant. This proves the first formula for smooth $u_ j$.

For continuous $u_ j$, let $u_{j,\varepsilon }=u_ j*\rho _\varepsilon $ be their radial mollifications, which are smooth and plurisubharmonic on $\Omega _\varepsilon $ (proposition 8.2.2). They converge to $u_ j$ uniformly on compact sets (lemma 1.4.1), so their products converge weakly by proposition 9.1.4. By proposition 1.4.3, the complex Hessian matrix of $u_{j,\varepsilon }$ is the mollification $A_{j,\varepsilon }$ of the matrix $A_ j$ of locally bounded functions. Let $K\subset \Omega $ be compact. For small $\varepsilon $, the entries of $A_{j,\varepsilon }$ are bounded on $K$ by the bounds of the entries of $A_ j$ on a fixed compact neighborhood of $K$, and by lemma 1.4.1 they converge to those of $A_ j$ in $L^1(K)$. Products of functions which are bounded on $K$ uniformly in $\varepsilon $ and converge in $L^1(K)$ converge in $L^1(K)$, because $|ab-a'b'|\leq |a||b-b'|+|b'||a-a'|$. Since $D$ is a polynomial in the matrix entries, it follows that $D(A_{1,\varepsilon },\dots ,A_{n,\varepsilon })\to D(A_1,\dots ,A_ n)$ in $L^1(K)$. By the first part, $(n!/\pi ^ n)D(A_{1,\varepsilon },\dots ,A_{n,\varepsilon })\, \mathrm dV_{2n}$ is the product of the $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_{j,\varepsilon }$, so the weak limit of these products is $(n!/\pi ^ n)D(A_1,\dots ,A_ n)\, \mathrm dV_{2n}$. This proves the first formula in general.

By lemma 9.12.2, each $u_ j$ belongs to $W^{2,2}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\Omega )\subset W^{2,1}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\Omega )$, so proposition 9.12.3, applied to $u_ j$ and the chosen representative of $v_ j$, gives $A_ j=B_ j$ almost everywhere on $\{ u_ j=v_ j\} $, hence on $E$. Substituting into the first formula proves the second, and also shows that $\mathbf1_ ED(B_1,\dots ,B_ n)=\mathbf1_ ED(A_1,\dots ,A_ n)$ is locally bounded. $\square$

On a complex manifold, the second identity is applied in holomorphic charts to functions $u_ j$ which become plurisubharmonic after the addition of a smooth local potential $g_ j$ of a closed real $(1,1)$-form. If $g_ j+u_ j$ is plurisubharmonic with bounded complex Hessian, we apply proposition 9.12.4 to $g_ j+u_ j$ and $g_ j+v_ j$: adding the same smooth function to $u_ j$ and $v_ j$ does not change the contact set, and $g_ j+v_ j\in W^{2,1}_{\mathrm{loc}}$ whenever $v_ j\in W^{2,1}_{\mathrm{loc}}$.

9.13 Gradients against positive currents

Several later arguments differentiate the product $v\Theta $ of a bounded plurisubharmonic function $v$ and a closed positive current $\Theta $; examples are the proof that non-pluripolar products are closed (theorem 15.2.1) and cutoff arguments on compact manifolds. For smooth $v$ the derivative is $\mathrm dv\wedge \Theta $, and its size is controlled by the gradient measure $\mathrm dv\wedge \mathrm d^{\mathrm c}v\wedge \Theta $, with $\mathrm d^{\mathrm c}$ as in (7.6). We extend both objects, together with the chain rule, to locally bounded plurisubharmonic functions by decreasing approximation. In this section $\eta $ denotes a real one-form, not the form $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}|z|^2$ of the preceding sections.

Throughout, $U\subset \mathbb C^ n$ is open and $\Theta $ is a closed positive current of bidimension $(1,1)$ on $U$, with trace measure $\mu =\Theta \wedge \beta $. We first describe $\Theta $ by a matrix of densities with respect to $\mu $. For $1\leq j,k\leq n$ let $\nu _{jk}$ be the complex Radon measure $\frac{\mathrm i}{2\pi }\, \mathrm dz_ k\wedge \mathrm d\bar z_ j\wedge \Theta $. Up to a constant factor, $\nu _{jk}$ is a coefficient measure of $\Theta $, so by theorem 7.2.5 there is a constant $C$, depending only on $n$, with $|\nu _{jk}|\leq C\mu $. Hence $C\mu +\operatorname{Re}\nu _{jk}$ and $C\mu +\operatorname{Im}\nu _{jk}$ are positive measures bounded by $2C\mu $, and lemma 1.7.1, applied to them, gives bounded Borel functions $h_{jk}$ with

\[ \frac{\mathrm i}{2\pi }\, \mathrm dz_ k\wedge \mathrm d\bar z_ j\wedge \Theta =h_{jk}\, \mu \qquad (1\leq j,k\leq n). \]

Since $\Theta $ is real (theorem 7.2.5), the complex conjugate of $\nu _{jk}$ is $\frac{-\mathrm i}{2\pi }\, \mathrm d\bar z_ k\wedge \mathrm dz_ j\wedge \Theta =\nu _{kj}$; by the uniqueness of densities in lemma 1.7.1, $h_{kj}=\bar h_{jk}$ $\mu $-almost everywhere, so the matrix $H=(h_{jk})$ is Hermitian $\mu $-almost everywhere. Since $\sum _ j\frac{\mathrm i}{2\pi }\, \mathrm dz_ j\wedge \mathrm d\bar z_ j=\beta /\pi $, summing the identities with $j=k$ gives $\operatorname {tr}H=1/\pi $ $\mu $-almost everywhere.

We identify a $(1,0)$-form $\xi =\sum _ k\xi _ k\, \mathrm dz_ k$ with its coefficient vector $(\xi _1,\ldots ,\xi _ n)\in \mathbb C^ n$, and write $\langle a,b\rangle =\sum _ ja_ j\bar b_ j$ on $\mathbb C^ n$. For a constant $(1,0)$-form $\xi $, the measure $(\mathrm i/2\pi )\, \xi \wedge \bar\xi \wedge \Theta =\langle H\xi ,\xi \rangle \mu $ is positive, because $\mathrm i\xi \wedge \bar\xi $ is a strongly positive form and $\Theta $ is positive; so $\langle H\xi ,\xi \rangle \geq 0$ $\mu $-almost everywhere. Applying this to the countably many $\xi $ with rational real and imaginary parts, and using that $\xi \mapsto \langle H\xi ,\xi \rangle $ is continuous, we see that $H$ is positive semidefinite $\mu $-almost everywhere. Changing $H$ on a $\mu $-null set, we may assume that it is Hermitian and positive semidefinite with trace $1/\pi $ everywhere. Its eigenvalues then lie in $[0,1/\pi ]$, so that $\langle H\xi ,\xi \rangle \leq |\xi |^2/\pi $.

Let $H^{1/2}$ be the positive semidefinite square root of $H$. It is a bounded Borel function on $U$. Indeed, by the Weierstrass approximation theorem there are real polynomials $p_ m$ with $p_ m(t)\to \sqrt t$ uniformly on $[0,1/\pi ]$. Diagonalizing $H(x)$ by a unitary matrix, we see that every entry of $p_ m(H(x))-H(x)^{1/2}$ is bounded by $\sup _{[0,1/\pi ]}|p_ m(t)-\sqrt t|$. The entries of $p_ m(H)$ are polynomials in the $h_{jk}$, hence Borel functions, and so are their pointwise limits, the entries of $H^{1/2}$. Moreover $|H^{1/2}\xi |^2=\langle H\xi ,\xi \rangle \leq |\xi |^2/\pi $.

For a real $C^1$ function $w$, the coefficient vector of the form $\partial w$ is $(\partial w/\partial z_ k)_{k=1}^ n$, and we denote it also by $\partial w$. Since $\mathrm dw\wedge \mathrm d^{\mathrm c}w=(\mathrm i/2\pi )\, \partial w\wedge \bar\partial w$ by (9.7), it follows that

9.82
\begin{equation} \mathrm dw\wedge \mathrm d^{\mathrm c}w\wedge \Theta =\bigl |H^{1/2}\partial w\bigr |^2\mu . \end{equation}

Thus, for a compact set $K\subset U$, the square of the energy seminorm $\| H^{1/2}\partial w\| _{L^2(K,\mu )}$ is the mass that $\mathrm dw\wedge \mathrm d^{\mathrm c}w\wedge \Theta $ gives to $K$.

Similarly, let $\eta $ be a real one-form with continuous coefficients and $(1,0)$-part $\sum _ ke_ k\, \mathrm dz_ k$, and put $e=(e_1,\ldots ,e_ n)$ and $w_ k=\partial w/\partial z_ k$. Only the $(1,1)$-part

\[ \sum _{j,k}(e_ k\bar w_ j-\bar e_ jw_ k)\, \mathrm dz_ k\wedge \mathrm d\bar z_ j \]

of $\eta \wedge \mathrm dw$ contributes to $\eta \wedge \mathrm dw\wedge \Theta $, so that $\eta \wedge \mathrm dw\wedge \Theta =-2\pi \mathrm i\bigl (\langle He,\partial w\rangle -\langle H\partial w,e\rangle \bigr )\mu $. Since $H$ is Hermitian, the second inner product is the complex conjugate of the first, and therefore

9.83
\begin{equation} \eta \wedge \mathrm dw\wedge \Theta =4\pi \operatorname{Im}\bigl \langle H^{1/2}e,H^{1/2}\partial w\bigr \rangle \, \mu . \end{equation}

Lemma 9.13.1. Let $\Theta $ be a closed positive current of bidimension $(1,1)$ on an open set $U\subset \mathbb C^ n$, and let $v$ be a locally bounded plurisubharmonic function on $U$. Then the current $\mathrm d(v\Theta )$ has measure coefficients; we denote it by $\mathrm dv\wedge \Theta $. The current

9.84
\begin{equation} E_\Theta (v)=\mathrm dv\wedge \mathrm d^{\mathrm c}v\wedge \Theta =\tfrac 12\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}(v^2\Theta )-v\, \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}v\wedge \Theta \end{equation}

is a positive measure, the gradient measure of $v$ with respect to $\Theta $. If smooth plurisubharmonic functions $v_ l$ decrease to $v$ on a neighborhood of a compact set $K\subset U$, then their gradients $\partial v_ l$ converge in the energy seminorm on $K$, that is, $H^{1/2}\partial v_ l$ converges in $L^2(K,\mu )$. For every $f\in C^1(\mathbb R)$,

9.85
\begin{equation} \mathrm d(f(v)\Theta )=f'(v)\, \mathrm dv\wedge \Theta . \end{equation}

Finally, let $\eta $ be a compactly supported smooth real one-form on $U$, and let $\tau \in C_ c(U)$, $\tau \geq 0$, be equal to one on $\operatorname{Supp}\eta $. There is a constant $C_\eta $, depending only on $\eta $, such that for every bounded Borel function $a$,

9.86
\begin{equation} \left|\int a\eta \wedge \mathrm dv\wedge \Theta \right|^2 \leq C_\eta \left(\int \tau E_\Theta (v)\right) \left(\int _{\operatorname{Supp}\eta }|a|^2\, \Theta \wedge \beta \right). \end{equation}

Proof. We approximate $v$ by smooth plurisubharmonic functions $v_ l$ and pass to the limit, first in the gradients (Step 1) and then in each assertion of the lemma (Steps 2–5). All assertions are local, so we fix a compact set $K\subset U$ and an open neighborhood $W\subset U$ of $K$ on which smooth plurisubharmonic functions $v_ l$ decrease to $v$. For the convergence assertion, $W$ and the $v_ l$ are given. Otherwise we take for $W$ a relatively compact open neighborhood of $K$ in $U$ and for $v_ l$ the radial mollifications $v*\rho _{\varepsilon _ l}$, where $\varepsilon _ l\downarrow 0$ and $\varepsilon _1$ is less than the distance from $\overline W$ to $\mathbb C^ n\setminus U$; they are smooth and plurisubharmonic on $W$ and decrease to $v$ (proposition 8.2.2). Choose a compact set $K_1\subset W$ with $K\subset \operatorname{Int}K_1$, and a function $\sigma \in C^\infty _ c(W)$ with $\sigma \geq 0$ which equals one on a neighborhood of $K_1$. Since $v\leq v_ l\leq v_1$, the $v_ l$ are uniformly bounded on $\operatorname{Supp}\sigma $.

Step 1. Convergence of the gradients. For a smooth real function $w$, we have $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}(w^2)=2\, \mathrm dw\wedge \mathrm d^{\mathrm c}w+2w\, \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}w$, and $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}(w^2\Theta )=\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}(w^2)\wedge \Theta $ because $\Theta $ is closed. Hence

\[ \mathrm dw\wedge \mathrm d^{\mathrm c}w\wedge \Theta =\tfrac 12\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}(w^2\Theta )-w\, \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}w\wedge \Theta . \]

Apply this identity to $w=v_ l-v_ m$ and integrate against $\sigma $:

\[ \int \sigma \, \mathrm dw\wedge \mathrm d^{\mathrm c}w\wedge \Theta =\tfrac 12\int (v_ l-v_ m)^2\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\sigma \wedge \Theta -\int \sigma (v_ l-v_ m)\, \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}(v_ l-v_ m)\wedge \Theta . \]

Both terms on the right tend to zero as $l,m\to \infty $. For the first, $|v_ l-v_ m|\leq v_ N-v$ for $l,m\geq N$, and $v_ N-v\to 0$ pointwise and boundedly on $\operatorname{Supp}\sigma $; since $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\sigma \wedge \Theta $ is a signed measure with total variation bounded by a multiple of $\mu $ on $\operatorname{Supp}\sigma $, dominated convergence applies. For the second, expand it into the four integrals $\int \sigma v_ p\, \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}v_ q\wedge \Theta $ with $p,q\in \{ l,m\} $, taken with the signs $+,-,-,+$. Given indices $l_ k,m_ k\to \infty $, pass to a subsequence along which both are strictly increasing; then $v_{l_ k}$ and $v_{m_ k}$ decrease to $v$, and the weighted form of theorem 9.2.5 on $W$, with the base current $\Theta $, shows that each of the four integrals tends to $\int \sigma v\, \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}v\wedge \Theta $. Their signed sum therefore tends to zero, and since this holds for a subsequence of every such pair of index sequences, the second term tends to zero as $l,m\to \infty $. Since $\sigma \geq 0$, $\sigma =1$ on $K_1$ and $\mathrm dw\wedge \mathrm d^{\mathrm c}w\wedge \Theta $ is the positive measure $|H^{1/2}\partial w|^2\mu $, by (9.82), the left side bounds $\| H^{1/2}\partial v_ l-H^{1/2}\partial v_ m\| _{L^2(K_1,\mu )}^2$. Consequently $H^{1/2}\partial v_ l$ is a Cauchy sequence in $L^2(K_1,\mu )$; let $\Gamma \in L^2(K_1,\mu )$ be its limit. In particular, $H^{1/2}\partial v_ l$ converges in $L^2(K,\mu )$, which is the convergence assertion.

Step 2. The first derivative. Let $\eta $ be a smooth real one-form with compact support in $\operatorname{Int}K_1$, with $(1,0)$-part $\sum _ ke_ k\, \mathrm dz_ k$. By (9.83),

\[ \int \eta \wedge \mathrm dv_ l\wedge \Theta =4\pi \int \operatorname{Im}\bigl \langle H^{1/2}e,H^{1/2}\partial v_ l\bigr \rangle \, \mathrm d\mu \longrightarrow 4\pi \int \operatorname{Im}\bigl \langle H^{1/2}e,\Gamma \bigr \rangle \, \mathrm d\mu , \]

since $H^{1/2}e$ is bounded and vanishes outside $K_1$, and $\mu (K_1)<\infty $. On the other hand, $\mathrm dv_ l\wedge \Theta =\mathrm d(v_ l\Theta )$ since $\Theta $ is closed, and $v_ l\Theta \to v\Theta $ weakly on $W$ by dominated convergence on the coefficient measures; hence $\mathrm d(v_ l\Theta )\to \mathrm d(v\Theta )$ weakly.
